Severe restrictions must be narrowly drawn to advance a compelling interest; reasonable, nondiscriminatory restrictions ordinarily are justified by important regulatory interests, so strict scrutiny does not apply to every election rule.

**Authority.** White’s Opinion of the Court, joined by O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, and Thomas, supplies five votes for the complete rule and controls. Kennedy’s dissent, joined by Blackmun and Stevens, and Stone-Zsela’s separate dissent also accept this calibrated framework; their agreement does not make their contrary application controlling.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that constitutional review must account for the regulation and the election system in which it operates. Anderson v. Celebrezze supplies the inquiry into the actual injury, the precise state interests, and the need for the particular burden. Norman v. Reed preserves that inquiry while recognizing that States may require support in the electorate a candidate or party seeks to represent. Storer v. Brown explains why orderly elections require substantial regulation of candidate selection. Treating every restriction as severe would disable the primary procedures and qualification rules those decisions permit. The claimed injury here therefore cannot be measured simply by observing that Hawaii forbids write-ins. The Court must examine the other means of obtaining access and the function the prohibition serves within that system. This approach preserves demanding review when access is severely restricted and meaningful scrutiny when a lesser burden is asserted; it does not make ordinary administration immune from constitutional review.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Anderson v. Celebrezze, 460 U.S. 780 (1983): applied as the governing burden, interest, and necessity inquiry; no categorical strict-scrutiny rule replaces it.
- Norman v. Reed (January 14, 1992): applied for actual access review and permissible electorate-specific support requirements; its protection against cross-component exclusion remains effective.
- Storer v. Brown, 415 U.S. 724 (1974): applied for the State’s authority to organize candidate selection; severe barriers remain reviewable.

##### 2. Hawaii’s complete write-in prohibition is valid in its reasonably accessible system

**Holding and operative rule.** Where the complete ballot-access system imposes only reasonable burdens, a prohibition on write-in voting is presumptively valid and may be sustained by interests proportionate to that burden. Hawaii’s party and nonpartisan routes provide adequate access, and its write-in prohibition validly supports primary finality, prevention of sore-loser candidacies and party raiding, and its treatment of uncontested primaries; the electoral-choice challenge therefore fails.

**Authority.** White’s Opinion of the Court, joined by O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, and Thomas, controls with five votes. Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, and Kennedy disagree with this application and its resulting judgment. This is a majority rationale; Marks analysis is unnecessary.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that Hawaii’s prohibition imposes a limited burden when assessed with its nomination rules. Small signature requirements and party and nonpartisan routes permit access before the election, although the nonpartisan route requires a primary showing of support and a choice of primary ballot. Jenness v. Fortson permits reasonable demonstrations of support, and Munro v. Socialist Workers Party permits primary winnowing without proof of a particular past disruption. The Constitution does not require the State to accommodate every preference arising after its lawful selection deadlines. Storer v. Brown supports preserving the primary’s role in settling candidacies. The prohibition also prevents candidacies that would evade that selection process, discourages primary raiding, and complements the designation of certain unopposed primary victors. The contrary position underestimates these available access routes and treats the inability to vote for an unlisted choice as decisive. The Court sustains this system without deciding whether the same prohibition would survive materially more restrictive access rules.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Jenness v. Fortson, 403 U.S. 431 (1971): applied to permit reasonable candidate-support requirements; it does not validate every access barrier.
- Munro v. Socialist Workers Party, 479 U.S. 189 (1986): applied to permit primary winnowing and reasonable preventative regulation without an empirical-disruption prerequisite.
- Storer v. Brown, 415 U.S. 724 (1974): applied to primary finality and restrictions preventing circumvention of candidate selection.
- Anderson v. Celebrezze, 460 U.S. 780 (1983): applied to the complete Hawaii system; this decision supplies a limited-burden application, not an exception to the inquiry.

##### 3. The ballot need not serve as an official tally of arbitrary political messages

**Holding and operative rule.** The First and Fourteenth Amendments do not require the State to count and publicize write-in votes for fictional figures or arbitrary protest messages as an independent form of political expression. Elections select public officials, and a desire to use the ballot for such messages does not establish a constitutional entitlement to a write-in option.

**Authority.** White’s Opinion of the Court, joined by O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, and Thomas, controls. Kennedy’s dissent, joined by Blackmun and Stevens, and Stone-Zsela’s separate dissent expressly agree in rejecting this broader claim. All nine reject this component, although four would grant relief for genuine electoral choice.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the ballot’s purpose limits the claimed expressive entitlement. Voting carries political meaning, but an official election need not become a general forum in which the State records and disseminates any message a voter wishes to send. Storer v. Brown recognizes elections as a means of selecting officeholders through an orderly process, and the access inquiry under Anderson v. Celebrezze protects the ability to participate in that process. Neither principle requires an official tally for fictional figures or arbitrary statements. Burdick’s interest in expressing dissatisfaction therefore does not itself invalidate the prohibition. His distinct interest in supporting an actual alternative candidate requires the contextual analysis stated in the preceding holdings, and the Court has conducted it. Rejecting the broader theory does not diminish political expression outside the ballot or decide every restriction on votes for otherwise eligible candidates under a different access system.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Storer v. Brown, 415 U.S. 724 (1974): applied to the office-selecting function of elections; the ballot is not a general expressive forum.
- Anderson v. Celebrezze, 460 U.S. 780 (1983): distinguished from the demand for an arbitrary-message tally; meaningful electoral participation remains protected.

**Law after decision:** Effective June 8, 1992, Anderson’s calibrated review includes the Court’s holding that a write-in ban is presumptively valid when the complete access system imposes reasonable burdens, with Hawaii’s arrangement sustained on this record. No absolute right to a write-in option or arbitrary-message tally exists. This does not establish that every prohibition is valid: a materially different access system may impose a severe burden requiring narrow tailoring to a compelling interest.

Norman’s protection against unjustified name and cross-component disqualification survives; Hawaii’s accessible selection routes present a different application. Burson’s strict-scrutiny proposition concerns campaign-content restrictions on ordinary public sidewalks and is neither extended to all ballot rules nor displaced. Burson’s fractured judgment does not supply a controlling validity exception here. No precedent is overruled, limited in force beyond these distinctions, or restored after displacement. Candidate qualifications, reasonable write-in procedures elsewhere, and independent challenges to other Hawaii provisions remain open.

#### Eastman Kodak Co. v. Image Technical Services, Inc. — 1992-06-08

Authority: `records/Eastman_Kodak_Co_v_Image_Technical_Services_Inc_merits_1992-06-08.md`.

##### 1. Distinct parts and service demand and conditional sales present a triable tying claim

**Holding and operative rule.** For a Sherman Act § 1 tying claim, functionally related products are distinct when sufficient separate consumer demand makes separate provision efficient. Evidence that Kodak parts and service meet that test, and that Kodak conditions parts sales on buying its service or forgoing independent service, creates genuine disputes about distinct products and conditioning; respondents may proceed but must prove all remaining tying elements.

**Authority.** Blackmun’s Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, White, Stevens, Kennedy, and Souter, controls with six votes, including the author. The commerce volume is not disputed here; no liability judgment follows.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that practical consumer demand, rather than a product’s functional relationship to another product, determines whether the two are distinct for tying purposes. Jefferson Parish Hospital District No. 2 v. Hyde supplies that demand-based inquiry and the requirements of tying power and a substantial amount of tied commerce. The record shows that independent organizations have supplied service separately, that customers have preferred independent service, and that some customers service their own equipment. Those materials permit a finding that separate provision is efficient. Kodak’s restrictions on parts sales to independent organizations and on customers’ use of independent service also permit a finding of conditioning. The fact that parts are installed during service does not conclusively combine both into one product. Respondents still must establish the necessary market power and other elements; the Court holds only that the district court could not end this claim for want of separate products or an arguable tie.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Jefferson Parish Hospital District No. 2 v. Hyde, 466 U.S. 2 (1984): applied for separate demand, conditioning, tying power, and tied commerce; functional integration alone does not merge products.
- Celotex Corp. v. Catrett, 477 U.S. 317 (1986): applied to preserve respondents’ burden of producing evidence on the elements challenged at summary judgment.

##### 2. Equipment-market competition is relevant but not a categorical defense

**Holding and operative rule.** Competition in an equipment market does not as a matter of law preclude market power in its parts or service markets. Under Rule 56, a party cannot obtain summary judgment solely through an economic prediction contradicted by evidence permitting a rational inference of power, including material information and switching costs and actual price or exclusion effects; unsupported speculation remains insufficient.

**Authority.** Blackmun’s Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, White, Stevens, Kennedy, and Souter, controls with six votes. The holding rejects a conclusive defense, not the relevance of equipment competition.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that Kodak’s proposed equipment-market constraint must be tested against the record. Matsushita Electric Industrial Co. v. Zenith Radio Corp. requires a plausible inference from evidence; it does not authorize a court to replace disputed facts with an economic model. Here the alleged conduct can produce immediate aftermarket gains, unlike the prolonged and implausible loss-producing conspiracy examined there. Evidence of costly equipment replacement and difficult lifetime service-price comparisons permits a finding that some installed customers cannot readily escape unfavorable terms. Evidence of price and exclusion effects supports testing that account at trial. Kodak may show that informed new purchasers and equipment rivalry adequately discipline its policies, but that conclusion is not inevitable as a matter of law. Anderson v. Liberty Lobby, Inc. preserves the factfinder’s role when a genuine dispute could affect the result. The Court neither presumes power from imperfect information nor treats every durable-product seller as a monopolist.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Matsushita Electric Industrial Co. v. Zenith Radio Corp., 475 U.S. 574 (1986): applied as a rational-inference requirement and distinguished on the alleged economic mechanism; it creates no categorical equipment-market defense.
- Anderson v. Liberty Lobby, Inc., 477 U.S. 242 (1986): applied to identify genuine material disputes; evidence and reasonable inferences receive the ordinary Rule 56 treatment.
- Jefferson Parish Hospital District No. 2 v. Hyde, 466 U.S. 2 (1984): preserved; respondents must prove actual tying power despite rejection of a conclusive equipment-competition defense.

##### 3. A single brand may define a relevant market when actual substitutes do not constrain it

**Holding and operative rule.** A relevant product market may consist of one brand’s parts or service when the actual commercial alternatives and competitive constraints support that definition; brand identity alone neither establishes nor defeats such a market. Evidence of Kodak-specific compatibility, a very high parts share, a high service share, and entry and switching barriers creates a genuine dispute about monopoly power sufficient to resist summary judgment on § 2.

**Authority.** Blackmun’s Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, White, Stevens, Kennedy, and Souter, controls with six votes. The record permits a market and power finding; it does not conclusively establish either.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that market boundaries turn on the alternatives buyers can actually use. United States v. Grinnell Corp. requires monopoly power in a relevant market, and United States v. E. I. du Pont de Nemours & Co. directs attention to substitution and competitive restraint. Owners needing compatible parts cannot necessarily substitute another brand’s parts, and changing equipment may cost substantially more than the aftermarket service at issue. Respondents also present evidence of Kodak’s dominant parts position, high service share, and obstacles to independent supply. Together these materials permit an inference of power to control prices or exclude competition. Kodak’s equipment competitors remain relevant because they may constrain lifetime prices; the Court does not exclude that evidence. But their existence does not settle the market question. The result establishes neither a single-brand rule for all manufacturers nor a monopoly presumption from a patent, a proprietary design, or numerical share considered apart from actual constraints.

**Precedent treatment.**

- United States v. Grinnell Corp., 384 U.S. 563 (1966): applied to require monopoly power in a relevant market, distinct from exclusionary conduct.
- United States v. E. I. du Pont de Nemours & Co., 351 U.S. 377 (1956): applied to practical substitution and competitive constraints; no categorical prohibition on a one-brand market follows.

##### 4. Exclusionary maintenance and Kodak’s business explanations require factual determination

**Holding and operative rule.** Monopolization under § 2 requires monopoly power plus willful acquisition or maintenance of that power, distinguished from success through a superior product, business acumen, or accident. Evidence of parts-access restrictions that may exclude service rivals creates a genuine issue of unlawful maintenance, and Kodak’s asserted quality, inventory, and free-riding explanations do not conclusively eliminate it on this record; no general duty to supply rivals or final finding of pretext is imposed.

**Authority.** Blackmun’s Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, White, Stevens, Kennedy, and Souter, controls with six votes. Attempted monopolization remains a distinct claim with its own intent and dangerous-probability requirements, not a consequence automatically established by this holding.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the reasons and competitive effect of Kodak’s restrictions cannot be settled on this summary-judgment record. Grinnell distinguishes unlawful maintenance of monopoly power from lawful commercial success. Aspen Skiing Co. v. Aspen Highlands Skiing Corp. makes the character of challenged exclusion and its business justification material without imposing a general obligation to help competitors. Respondents’ evidence permits a finding that restricting access to necessary parts protects Kodak’s service position. The asserted quality rationale must be considered with the continued self-service exception and evidence about independent service. Inventory explanations must be tested against evidence that equipment failures and parts needs do not change with the provider, while supplier restrictions may impede alternative sources. Calling independent service free riding because the providers did not enter the equipment and parts businesses does not conclusively justify exclusion. Kodak may prove legitimate reasons and defeat liability. The Court awards no damages, orders no compulsory supply, and leaves ultimate liability and injury for further proceedings.

**Precedent treatment.**

- United States v. Grinnell Corp., 384 U.S. 563 (1966): applied to distinguish willful monopoly maintenance from lawful success.
- Aspen Skiing Co. v. Aspen Highlands Skiing Corp., 472 U.S. 585 (1985): applied to the challenged conduct and offered business reasons; no universal obligation to deal is created.
- Brunswick Corp. v. Pueblo Bowl-O-Mat, Inc., 429 U.S. 477 (1977): preserved; injury to a competitor alone does not establish antitrust injury or entitlement to damages.

**Law after decision:** Effective June 8, 1992, equipment competition remains relevant evidence but cannot be invoked as a categorical legal bar to a sufficiently supported aftermarket claim. Separate demand may distinguish parts from service; a single brand may comprise a relevant market where actual substitutes and constraints support that conclusion. Power and exclusion remain separate elements, and commercial success through superior products remains lawful. The business reasons offered for the challenged exclusion require factual evaluation here.

The Court applies received tying and monopolization law with ordinary Rule 56 burdens; it creates no automatic patent or brand monopoly, no universal duty to deal, and no present recovery right. Monopolization and attempt remain distinct. Ultimate markets, power, unlawful conduct, antitrust injury, causation, damages, and remedial design remain for further proceedings. Holmes’s actual March 24 RICO directness and SIPA participation holdings remain effective but do not decide these disputed Sherman Act elements or establish injury here. No prior decision is overruled.

#### United States v. Thompson/Center Arms Co. — 1992-06-08

Authority: `records/United_States_v_Thompson_Center_Arms_Co_merits_1992-06-08.md`.

##### The actual dual-use package is outside the making tax

**Holding and operative rule:** The National Firearms Act does not clearly treat the segregated Contender pistol and conversion package, which has genuine lawful pistol and long-rifle uses and has not been assembled as a short-barreled rifle, as the making of that regulated firearm. Because the same definition carries criminal consequences, the remaining statutory ambiguity is resolved in the manufacturer's favor in this civil refund action as well.

**Authority:** Souter and O'Connor's opinion, Scalia's concurrence joined by Thomas, and Stone-Zsela's concurrence expressly support this case-specific statutory construction and uniform application of residual lenity. Those five constitute a majority at this level of generality, although no complete opinion commands five joins. The holding rests on that explicit common proposition, not on a synthetic Marks rationale combining their differing accounts of the ambiguity.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that unresolved criminally consequential ambiguity prevents collection of this making tax. Crandon v. United States supplies the governing principle: the meaning of a statute with criminal applications does not change merely because the present case is civil. The inquiry begins with the enacted language and the related definitions. Section 5845 defines a rifle by its design, making, and intended shoulder firing, while other firearm definitions expressly address combinations of parts. Those provisions do not clearly settle this package, which permits a lawful pistol and a lawful long rifle as well as the regulated short rifle. The Government's concern that packaging must not defeat actual firearms regulation does not itself identify the statutory boundary here. The five Justices differ over how much broader parts coverage the text supports, but agree that the remaining ambiguity concerning this unit must be resolved narrowly. The decision does not decide criminal culpability, impose a storage protocol, or exempt an assembled short-barreled rifle.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Crandon v. United States, 494 U.S. 152 — applied: residual lenity supplies one construction of criminally consequential statutory language in civil litigation; no new criminal proof burden results.
- United States v. R. L. C., simulated March 24, 1992 — preserved: its adult-Guidelines ceiling does not establish a common new lenity method and supplies no different firearms rule.
- Marks v. United States, 430 U.S. 188 — no narrowest-opinion synthesis is necessary or adopted for the five Justices' expressly shared case-specific proposition; their broader incompatible rationales remain distinct.

##### Making is not invariably postponed until final assembly

**Holding and operative rule:** The National Firearms Act's references to manufacturing and otherwise producing a firearm are not confined to the final mechanical act of putting its parts together. The Federal Circuit's universal actual-assembly prerequisite is therefore rejected, without deciding tax liability for an unpresented pure-parts or sham arrangement.

**Authority:** Souter's opinion joined by O'Connor expressly adopts this construction, and White's dissent joined by Blackmun, Stevens and Kennedy expressly adopts the same proposition while disagreeing about the dual-use package. Stone-Zsela expressly supports the rejection of a universal final-assembly requirement in his concurrence while reserving liability for pure-parts kits and sham configurations. Seven participating Justices therefore support this exact predicate at the same level of generality. This is a directly shared statutory proposition across the writings, not a Marks combination of nonoverlapping rationales. Stone-Zsela joins no broader categorical ruling about the reserved arrangements. Scalia and Thomas disagree with this construction.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the statutory meaning of making cannot invariably wait for the purchaser to complete the final connection. Section 5845(i) identifies manufacturing and otherwise producing as well as putting together. Reading every term as requiring final assembly would deprive the broader production language of work in this setting. Drasen's analysis of the production terms illustrates why a universal final-assembly rule is too broad; liability for an unpresented pure-parts arrangement remains reserved. The express parts clauses for other regulated weapons do not compel a different answer so as to require a universal assembly prerequisite. The disagreement over this case concerns the additional lawful configurations, not whether the statute invariably waits for the last mechanical connection. This construction does not decide that every miscellaneous collection, incomplete kit, or set with genuine alternative lawful uses is a firearm. Nor does it turn a regulatory purpose into an independent tax: the production must satisfy the enacted definition.

**Precedent treatment:**

- United States v. Drasen, 845 F.2d 731 — its construction of manufacturing supports rejecting a universal actual-assembly prerequisite; the Court adopts no new judgment concerning its kits or its broader notice and criminal-responsibility discussion.
- Thompson/Center Arms Co. v. United States, 924 F.2d 1041 — the refund judgment is affirmed, but the universal actual-assembly rationale is rejected at the stated seven-Justice level.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No complete opinion controls the broader division between permissible and regulated combinations. The actual assembly of a short rifle, sham lawful configurations, miscellaneous or incomplete parts, unrelated firearm categories and separate criminal offenses are not adjudicated. No general intent-to-assemble element, criminal proof change, prospective injunction or storage requirement is created. The refund ruling does not exempt all parts kits. The broader utility taxonomy in Souter's opinion, beyond the independently supported rejection of a universal actual-assembly prerequisite, is not additional controlling law.

**Law after decision:** Effective June 8, 1992, the actual genuine dual-use package receives the narrow statutory refund construction. The seven-Justice rejection of a universal actual-assembly prerequisite rejects the Federal Circuit's universal final-assembly prerequisite. Residual lenity remains a rule of construction; civil refund and hypothetical criminal proof burdens remain distinct. R.L.C.'s juvenile ceiling and the unresolved choice among general interpretive methods remain unchanged. The plurality's broader usefulness taxonomy, Scalia/Thomas's separate account of shoulder-firing intent, and Stevens's civil-notice limitation do not become Court law.

#### Sochor v. Florida — 1992-06-08

Authority: `records/Sochor_v_Florida_merits_1992-06-08.md`.

##### The jury-heinousness instruction claim is outside review

**Holding and operative rule:** The Court cannot review a federal challenge to a penalty instruction when the state judgment clearly rests on an adequate independent failure-to-object ground, even if the state court also rejects the claim on its merits. Florida's express preservation ruling forecloses this jury-heinousness challenge; no sufficient basis to reject the ground's adequacy or independence has been established here.

**Authority:** Part II-A of Souter's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas; seven Justices. Blackmun and Stevens would reach the instructional claim.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that an alternative discussion of federal merits does not remove an independent state ground for judgment. Michigan v. Long supplies the requirement to identify clearly the state ground, and Herb v. Pitcairn supplies the jurisdictional consequence when that ground independently sustains the judgment. Florida's penalty-instruction paragraph states that no objection was made and that the claims were not preserved, then separately rejects their merits. Its accompanying note includes the heinousness instruction. That is a clear alternative procedural disposition. The possibility of fundamental-error review does not establish that this particular preservation ruling depended on deciding the federal claim. Nor do later state citations to the merits discussion withdraw the independent ground already given. The contrary reading would treat the state's power to consider an unpreserved error as if it necessarily had displaced the expressly stated procedural basis here. The Court reserves a supported challenge to a different procedural ground and does not decide this instruction's constitutional merits.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Michigan v. Long, 463 U.S. 1032 — applied: an expressly independent state disposition remains sufficient despite alternative federal merits discussion.
- Herb v. Pitcairn, 324 U.S. 117 — applied: an adequate independent state ground limits this Court's review.
- Coleman v. Thompson, 501 U.S. 722 — its pre-divergence distinction between independent state procedure and federal merits remains intact; this direct review does not create a habeas exception.

##### The judge's narrowed heinousness application survives

**Holding and operative rule:** A sentencing judge may apply an otherwise vague heinousness aggravator through an authoritative and sufficiently specific state narrowing construction. Florida's consistently recognized conscious-victim strangulation construction supplies adequate guidance on this record; inconsistent applications in other factual settings do not establish that this judge lacked that guidance.

**Authority:** Part II-B of Souter's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas; seven Justices. Blackmun and Stevens do not join the Court's disposition of the heinousness challenge, because they would require correction of the separate jury defect.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that this judicial application rests on a concrete limiting construction. Godfrey and Maynard require meaningful guidance rather than a bare invitation to label any murder especially evil. Walton permits a judge to apply the state's authoritative narrowing decisions and presumes familiarity with them. Florida's cases consistently recognize the fear and conscious suffering involved when a conscious victim is strangled; the judgment below identifies that specific construction through Tompkins, Johnson and Doyle. The record contains the victim's resistance, fear and the strangulation, so the judge was not left merely to repeat the statutory adjectives. The objection that Florida has used broader language in other cases does not show an absence of a governing boundary in this one. The Court does not approve every Florida heinousness decision, the bare instruction given to this jury, or an application involving a different victim's condition. It rejects only the preserved challenge to the judge's use of the narrowed factor here.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Godfrey v. Georgia, 446 U.S. 420, and Maynard v. Cartwright, 486 U.S. 356 — preserved and applied: the aggravator requires meaningful narrowing.
- Walton v. Arizona, 497 U.S. 639 — applied: the sentencing judge may use an authoritative state narrowing construction and is presumed familiar with it.
- Proffitt v. Florida, 428 U.S. 242 — its approval of a narrowed heinousness category is preserved; no approval of every later Florida application is inferred.

##### The general jury recommendation does not establish coldness reliance

**Holding and operative rule:** The submission of a legally available aggravating circumstance that lacks evidentiary support does not, by itself, establish that a Florida advisory jury relied on it when returning a general death recommendation that identifies no aggravator findings. The Court therefore rejects this asserted inference of jury-coldness error without finding that the jury actually rejected the factor or resolving a legally invalid instruction.

**Authority:** Part III-A of Souter's opinion, joined by White, O'Connor, Kennedy and Thomas; five Justices. Stone-Zsela reserves the independent jury-coldness question; Blackmun and Stevens would include the submission error in the corrective inquiry; Scalia does not join this independent holding.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the jury's undisclosed findings cannot be supplied by inference from the instruction alone. Griffin distinguishes a legally forbidden route, which a jury cannot be expected to recognize as forbidden, from a lawful route that the evidence fails to support. Stromberg and Yates retain their different protection against verdicts that may rest on legal invalidity. Cold, calculated and premeditated murder was a legally available aggravator; the state court's objection was the absence of proof of its heightened premeditation requirement. Florida's jury recommended death generally and identified no aggravators. On those circumstances, Griffin's distinction gives no basis to presume that the jury adopted the unsupported option. This conclusion does not declare what the jury found, convert a capital weighing recommendation into a guilt verdict for every purpose, or decide that all instructional errors are harmless. The judge's express coldness finding presents a different, established error that must be examined independently.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Griffin v. United States, simulated December 3, 1991 — its factual-versus-legal distinction is extended narrowly to reject this asserted inference from a general advisory recommendation; broader capital-jury doctrine remains open.
- Stromberg v. California, 283 U.S. 359, and Yates v. United States, 354 U.S. 298 — distinguished and preserved for legally invalid alternatives.
- Estelle v. McGuire, simulated December 4, 1991 — its whole-charge reasonable-likelihood rule remains unchanged; it neither removes the preservation bar nor supplies an automatic inference of factual reliance here.

##### The judge's documented coldness reliance requires correction

**Holding and operative rule:** When a Florida capital sentencing judge expressly weighs an aggravator that the state supreme court holds unsupported by the evidence, that invalid factor creates Eighth Amendment error in the weighing decision. The sentence requires lawful reweighing, constitutional harmless-error review, or resentencing; the existence of other valid aggravators does not itself supply the cure.

**Authority:** Part III-B-1 of Souter's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy and Thomas; eight Justices. Scalia rejects the constitutional weighing-error premise.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the judge's express written finding establishes actual reliance on the unsupported aggravator. Clemons requires a constitutionally adequate correction when a capital weighing decision incorporates an invalid aggravator. Parker applies that principle when Florida has found aggravating circumstances unsupported by the evidence, and Stringer explains why surviving valid factors alone do not remove the invalid factor from the sentencing balance. Here the judge found and weighed coldness, while Florida's supreme court held that heightened premeditation was not proved. The difference between ordinary murder premeditation and this additional aggravating requirement matters: the valid conviction does not establish the invalidated circumstance. The State may show that the error made no difference under Chapman, or use another legally available corrective procedure, but the error cannot be ignored because death eligibility survives. The Court does not invalidate the convictions, reopen the mitigation findings, or prescribe a life sentence upon removal of one aggravator.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Clemons v. Mississippi, 494 U.S. 738 — applied: a weighing error requires adequate appellate correction or resentencing.
- Parker v. Dugger, 498 U.S. 308 — applied to a judge's use of an evidentially unsupported Florida aggravator; the actual sentencing record controls.
- Stringer v. Black, simulated March 9, 1992 — its correction principle is applied to documented reliance on unsupported coldness on direct review; its retroactivity and procedural-default reservations do not bar this case.
- Zant v. Stephens, 462 U.S. 862 — its nonweighing setting is distinguished; surviving eligibility does not automatically cure a weighing error.
- Chapman v. California, 386 U.S. 18 — preserved as the constitutional harmlessness standard, with the burden on the State.

##### The existing appellate explanation does not cure the error

**Holding and operative rule:** A state appellate court affirming this sentence must substantively establish beyond a reasonable doubt that the invalid aggravator did not contribute to the sentence, or provide another authorized constitutional cure. Florida's existing proportionality discussion, count of surviving factors and mixed citations do not establish that inquiry here; no particular verbal formula is either required or sufficient.

**Authority:** Parts III-B-2 and IV of Souter's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor and Kennedy; six Justices. White and Thomas accept the error framework but find this review sufficient; Scalia would affirm on his separate constitutional ground.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that a strong harmlessness argument is not a substitute for the required appellate determination. Chapman places on the State the burden of showing beyond a reasonable doubt that the error did not contribute to the sentence. Clemons and Parker require review of the actual sentencing choice; proportionality asks instead how the sentence compares with other cases. Florida's paragraph principally frames that comparison and observes three valid factors against no found mitigation. Those facts may bear strongly on harmlessness, but they do not explain whether attributing an additional kind of culpability affected this weighing decision. The cited cases do not make the missing inquiry clear. The Court requires no prescribed words and does not suggest that appending a harmless-error label would be enough. The case returns for supported Chapman review, lawful reweighing if Florida law permits it, or resentencing. It remains for the state courts to choose and perform an adequate lawful correction.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Chapman v. California, 386 U.S. 18 — applied without changing the State's beyond-a-reasonable-doubt burden.
- Clemons v. Mississippi, 494 U.S. 738, and Parker v. Dugger, 498 U.S. 308 — applied: actual corrective analysis must address the sentence and record rather than rest on a proportionality comparison.
- Stringer v. Black, simulated March 9, 1992 — preserved: factor counting and proportionality alone do not cure constitutional weighing error.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The convictions are not disturbed. The Court decides no automatic life-sentence rule, mandatory new-penalty-jury rule, capital-punishment validity question, new mitigation finding, future execution date, or entitlement to a particular outcome after lawful correction. The merits of the procedurally barred jury-heinousness claim are not reached by the Court. The narrow jury-coldness holding does not decide broader capital-jury error rules or factual jury findings. Stone-Zsela expressly reserves that independent jury-coldness branch and supplies no vote or rationale to its holding.

**Law after decision:** Effective June 8, 1992, the Court applies the adequate-independent-state-ground rule to this instruction claim and Walton's judicial narrowing principle to conscious-victim strangulation. Five Justices extend Griffin's factual/legal distinction only to reject the asserted inference of jury-coldness reliance from this general recommendation. Eight identify the documented judicial coldness weighing error; six hold existing appellate treatment inadequate and require lawful correction. The actual Stringer, Griffin and Estelle rules remain in force within their stated scopes. No automatic factor-counting safe harbor, magic-words requirement, criminal burden revision, universal jury-resentencing rule or new habeas standard is created.

#### Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife — 1992-06-12

Authority: `records/Lujan_v_Defenders_of_Wildlife_merits_1992-06-12.md`.

##### 1. Article III elements must be supported at the litigation stage

**Holding and operative rule.** A plaintiff invoking federal jurisdiction must establish a concrete and particularized, actual or imminent injury, a causal connection fairly traceable to the defendant rather than independent third parties, and a likelihood that the requested relief will redress the injury. At summary judgment, the plaintiff must supply specific facts by affidavit or other evidence on the challenged elements; favorable reasonable inferences remain available, but allegations cannot substitute for the missing evidence.

**Authority.** Part II of Scalia's Opinion of the Court, joined by White, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas, controls with five votes. Stone-Zsela concurs separately in the judgment on the personal-injury ground and does not join this section. The general redressability element is controlling; its application to these absent agencies is not.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that standing is part of the plaintiff's case and must be supported as the litigation progresses. Warth v. Seldin and Allen v. Wright require a personal injury connected to the challenged conduct; Simon v. Eastern Kentucky Welfare Rights Organization requires likely redress through the requested judgment. Lujan v. National Wildlife Federation supplies the distinction between allegations sufficient at pleading and evidence required at summary judgment. Respondents therefore cannot rely on the earlier decision allowing their allegations to survive dismissal. They must produce specific facts supporting their own threatened injury. Rule 56 preserves favorable reasonable inferences from evidence; it does not authorize a court to presume facts necessary to establish jurisdiction merely because the claim otherwise fails. Where independent third parties determine whether the plaintiff will be harmed or helped, respondents must support the necessary causal connection. The Court resolves this action for want of personal injury and does not adopt the separate opinion's application of redressability to the absent financing agencies.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Warth v. Seldin, 422 U.S. 490 (1975): applied for the plaintiff's personal stake and burden to establish standing; its statutory-rights discussion remains subject to concrete injury.
- Allen v. Wright, 468 U.S. 737 (1984): applied for the constitutional elements and the limits on generalized judicial supervision.
- Simon v. Eastern Kentucky Welfare Rights Organization, 426 U.S. 26 (1976): applied for fair traceability and likely redress; no new majority application concerning these absent agencies follows.
- Lujan v. National Wildlife Federation, 497 U.S. 871 (1990): applied for the evidentiary distinction between pleading and summary judgment; its separate APA programmatic-review holding is not enlarged.
- Celotex Corp. v. Catrett, 477 U.S. 317 (1986), and Anderson v. Liberty Lobby, Inc., 477 U.S. 242 (1986): applied to require evidence of challenged essential elements while preserving genuine material disputes and reasonable inferences.

##### 2. Indefinite return intentions and general species interests do not establish threatened personal injury here

**Holding and operative rule.** Aesthetic, recreational and professional interests in observing wildlife can support standing, but these plaintiffs must show threatened interference with their own concrete use. Prior visits and an indefinite intention to return, without concrete plans or another supported basis for imminent renewed exposure, do not establish standing for these prospective claims; an ecosystem, animal or professional connection that supplies only general interest in distant species does not cure that defect.

**Authority.** Part III-A of Scalia's Opinion of the Court, joined by White, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas, controls with five votes. Stone-Zsela's separate concurrence supplies a sixth vote for this record-specific personal-injury conclusion without joining the Court's writing. No purchased-ticket or particular-document prerequisite is adopted.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that a recognized interest must be threatened in the plaintiff's own life. Sierra Club v. Morton recognizes aesthetic and recreational injury while requiring a member of the plaintiff organization to be among those harmed. The affidavits identify actual visits to the Egyptian and Sri Lankan areas and an interest in observing their wildlife. They provide no sufficiently definite return undertaking or continuing activity showing imminent exposure to the threatened loss. Los Angeles v. Lyons and Whitmore v. Arkansas require more than past exposure followed by a conjectural future injury. The Court accepts reasonable inferences from the supplied facts, but an unspecified intention cannot itself establish when personal use will recur. The proposed ecosystem and professional theories fail for the same evidentiary reason: they broaden the class of interested persons without showing a concrete effect on these members. National Wildlife Federation requires an affected personal use, not a general environmental concern. The Court does not decide a case involving actual use of an affected ecosystem or demonstrated injury transmitted across geographic distance.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Sierra Club v. Morton, 405 U.S. 727 (1972): applied; aesthetic injury is cognizable, but an organization's special interest cannot replace injury to a member.
- Hunt v. Washington State Apple Advertising Commission, 432 U.S. 333 (1977): applied to require an injured member for this associational claim; no other associational requirement is changed.
- Los Angeles v. Lyons, 461 U.S. 95 (1983): applied for the distinction between past exposure and a present or imminent injury supporting prospective relief.
- Whitmore v. Arkansas, 495 U.S. 149 (1990): applied for actual or imminent rather than conjectural injury.
- Lujan v. National Wildlife Federation, 497 U.S. 871 (1990): applied to specific affected use; no categorical bar to geographically transmitted injury is established.
- Japan Whaling Association v. American Cetacean Society, 478 U.S. 221 (1986): distinguished because its whale-watching and studying members alleged an adverse effect on their own activities; concrete professional or ecological injury remains possible.

##### 3. Citizen-suit authorization does not dispense with a threatened concrete interest

**Holding and operative rule.** ESA §11(g)'s authorization for any person to sue does not give Article III standing to a plaintiff asserting only the public's common interest in lawful administration. A procedural right may protect a plaintiff's threatened concrete interest without requiring proof that the procedure will certainly change the ultimate agency decision, but the procedural label cannot replace that personal interest; these respondents have not shown the required connection.

**Authority.** Part IV of Scalia's Opinion of the Court, joined by White, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas, controls with five votes. Stone-Zsela does not join this section or its broader congressional-power analysis; he separately concludes only that the claimed consultation deprivation fails to connect to sufficiently imminent interference with these members' concrete use.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that statutory permission to bring suit and constitutional injury remain distinct. Fairchild v. Hughes and the generalized-grievance decisions refuse federal jurisdiction based solely on every citizen's interest in lawful government. Section 11(g) authorizes enforcement but does not itself demonstrate that every violation injures every person. Respondents cannot establish the missing personal connection by treating consultation as an abstract entitlement available to the public at large. This conclusion does not exclude procedural injuries as a class. A person whose concrete interest a required procedure protects need not prove with certainty that performing the procedure will change the final agency choice. Sierra Club preserves the requirement of personal injury while Warth permits Congress to recognize legally protected injuries. Congress may recognize concrete factual injuries previously inadequate in law; the Court does not settle the outer reach of that power. Here, however, the citizen-suit provision does not identify a concrete injury to these members independently of the unsupported future use already examined. The Court leaves the statute's geographic reach undecided.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Fairchild v. Hughes, 258 U.S. 126 (1922): applied to reject a general right to compel lawful administration as a sufficient personal injury.
- Massachusetts v. Mellon, 262 U.S. 447 (1923), and Ex parte Levitt, 302 U.S. 633 (1937): applied for the distinction between direct injury and an undifferentiated public grievance.
- United States v. Richardson, 418 U.S. 166 (1974), and Schlesinger v. Reservists Committee to Stop the War, 418 U.S. 208 (1974): applied to generally shared governmental grievances without distinctive concrete harm.
- Valley Forge Christian College v. Americans United for Separation of Church and State, Inc., 454 U.S. 464 (1982), and Allen v. Wright, 468 U.S. 737 (1984): applied; a claimed right to lawful government conduct alone does not satisfy Article III.
- Warth v. Seldin, 422 U.S. 490 (1975), and Sierra Club v. Morton, 405 U.S. 727 (1972): preserved for Congress's power to recognize injuries and broaden protected interests while retaining the plaintiff's own concrete injury.
- Trafficante v. Metropolitan Life Insurance Co., 409 U.S. 205 (1972), and Hardin v. Kentucky Utilities Co., 390 U.S. 1 (1968): distinguished as statutory recognition of concrete personal interests, not authorization of a wholly abstract grievance.

**Law after decision:** Effective June 12, 1992, respondents' standing fails for insufficient threatened personal injury. Ordinary evidentiary burdens apply at each litigation stage. The ESA citizen-suit provision does not turn an undifferentiated demand for consultation into concrete injury, while procedural protections tied to a personal interest remain cognizable without certainty that the final agency choice will change. Preexisting Article III doctrine is applied and clarified in this foreign-project record; no new documentary prerequisite is imposed. The Court does not decide whether the Secretary's regulation binds absent agencies, whether particular funding changes would alter the projects, or whether §7(a)(2) reaches actions abroad. Part III-B has three votes, Stevens's statutory ground one, and neither controls. No Marks synthesis is necessary: five join each Court holding at the stated level, independently of judgment-only agreements. No earlier precedent is overruled.

#### Republic of Argentina v. Weltover, Inc. — 1992-06-12

Authority: `records/Republic_of_Argentina_v_Weltover_Inc_merits_1992-06-12.md`.

##### 1. Ordinary negotiable debt remains commercial despite a sovereign monetary purpose

**Holding and operative rule.** Under 28 U.S.C. §§1603(d) and 1605(a)(2), an activity is commercial when its nature is the kind of conduct a private participant in a market can undertake; the sovereign's public purpose does not control that characterization. Argentina and Banco Central acted commercially in issuing and rescheduling these negotiable, dollar-denominated debt instruments, even though the instruments served a foreign-exchange program and refinanced existing obligations.

**Authority.** Scalia's Opinion of the Court, joined by White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas, controls with eight votes. Stone-Zsela agrees with the statutory conclusion on his separately stated ground but joins no other writing.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that Congress made the character of the activity decisive. Verlinden B. V. v. Central Bank of Nigeria explains the Act's adoption of restrictive sovereign immunity and its exception-dependent jurisdictional structure. Section 1603(d) directs courts to examine nature rather than purpose. Argentina's currency difficulties explain why it acted, but they do not change the means it selected. The Bonods are negotiable promises to repay dollars, with interest tied to a market rate and payment available at specified financial centers. Private borrowers may issue and restructure such obligations. Argentina argues that no private person could administer its exchange program and that the instruments obtained no new money or goods. Those points concern the program's sovereign objective and the origin of the debt. Neither makes refinancing through ordinary negotiable promises an exclusively sovereign power. The Court decides the commercial character of these instruments and this challenged rescheduling. It does not classify every public borrowing, currency regulation or sovereign act affecting money as commercial.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Verlinden B. V. v. Central Bank of Nigeria, 461 U.S. 480 (1983): applied for the restrictive-immunity framework and statutory exception channel; no additional citizenship or commercial-purpose restriction is derived from it.

##### 2. Missing the contractually required New York payment has a direct domestic effect

**Holding and operative rule.** For the third clause of §1605(a)(2), an effect is direct when it follows as an immediate consequence of the defendant's activity; the statute adds no separate substantiality or foreseeability requirement, although wholly trivial effects remain outside the ordinary reach of the exception. Failure to make payment in New York, after the holders validly exercised the instruments' contractual payment option, produces the required direct effect in the United States even though the holders are foreign and their ultimate economic loss is abroad.

**Authority.** Scalia's Opinion of the Court, joined by White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas, controls with eight votes. Stone-Zsela separately agrees that the missed contractually required New York payment is direct and that no substantiality condition may be added; he is not counted for a separate general proposition concerning foreseeability.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the promised place of payment supplies the direct domestic consequence. The bond terms allowed the holders to choose New York, and they did so. Earlier payments credited their New York accounts. Rescheduling then prevented the dollars from arriving where the contract required performance. This consequence does not depend on treating foreign plaintiffs as residents or on locating all their financial injury here. Verlinden allows qualifying actions by foreign plaintiffs and supplies no contrary limitation. Argentina asks the Court to import substantiality and foreseeability conditions from sources that did not define this enacted clause. The statutory text does not contain those conditions. Ordinary limits exclude wholly trivial effects, but the promised payment here is neither remote nor trivial. New York's reputation as a financial center, relied upon below, is too remote to supply the necessary effect and is unnecessary to the judgment. The Court decides contractually required domestic performance, leaving other payment arrangements and merely incidental domestic financial connections for their own records.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Verlinden B. V. v. Central Bank of Nigeria, 461 U.S. 480 (1983): applied to permit jurisdiction over qualifying foreign-plaintiff actions; its general jurisdictional discussion is not a substantiality or foreseeability test for this clause.
- The Second Circuit's immediate-consequence formulation in the judgment below is sustained; its additional substantiality/foreseeability formulation is rejected, and its financial-center-reputation rationale supplies no controlling ground.

##### 3. The assumed constitutional objection supplies no reason to narrow this exception

**Holding and operative rule.** Assuming solely for Argentina's interpretive objection that a foreign state receives the relevant constitutional protection, Argentina's purposeful dollar debt and offered, selected and previously used New York payment arrangements provide sufficient transaction-related contacts to defeat the asserted constitutional doubt. Constitutional avoidance therefore does not justify narrowing the statutory direct-effect clause in this case; the Court decides neither foreign-sovereign constitutional personhood nor an independent constitutional personal-jurisdiction claim.

**Authority.** Scalia's Opinion of the Court, joined by White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas, controls with eight votes on this bounded rejection of a preserved interpretive contention. Stone-Zsela independently states the same assumed-personhood limitation in his concurrence. This is not an alternative basis for subject-matter jurisdiction.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the asserted constitutional concern does not require a different reading of the Act. International Shoe Co. v. Washington evaluates the quality and nature of a defendant's contacts and their connection to the suit when constitutional protection applies. Argentina did more than foresee that a foreign creditor might route money through an American bank. It issued dollar obligations offering New York as an agreed place of payment, honored that selection in earlier payments, and then failed to perform the disputed obligation there. Those purposeful arrangements connect this contract dispute to the United States. Assuming the constitutional premise therefore leaves no persuasive doubt that would support Argentina's proposed statutory restriction on these facts. Argentina expressly presents the constitutional point only as an interpretive argument, not an independent personal-jurisdiction claim. The Court respects that boundary. It neither decides whether a foreign state is a constitutional person nor supplies a general test for jurisdiction over unrelated sovereign conduct. The holding answers the avoidance objection and leaves the ordinary merits and asset-immunity questions untouched.

**Precedent treatment.**

- International Shoe Co. v. Washington, 326 U.S. 310 (1945): applied on an express assumption of constitutional protection, for purposeful contacts related to this dispute; no holding extends constitutional personhood to foreign states.

**Law after decision:** Effective June 12, 1992, the FSIA commercial-activity exception covers this ordinary negotiable-debt activity and its missed contractually required New York payment. The eight-Justice opinion supplies each controlling proposition without a fractured-opinion synthesis. Foreign-sovereign constitutional personhood, contractual defenses, liability, attachment and execution remain undecided. No categorical rule makes all sovereign financial activity commercial or every dollar payment a direct domestic effect.

#### FTC v. Ticor Title Insurance Co. — 1992-06-12

Authority: `records/FTC_v_Ticor_Title_Insurance_Co_merits_1992-06-12.md`.

##### 1. Private rate fixing requires actual substantive state supervision

**Holding and operative rule.** Private parties claiming state-action antitrust immunity for collectively fixed rates must establish both a clearly articulated state policy and actual state supervision of the particular restraint. When private rates take effect unless vetoed, the claimant must show that state officials exercised sufficient independent judgment and control to make the rate scheme a product of deliberate state intervention; an available veto, operating regulatory machinery or nominal approval alone is insufficient.

**Authority.** Kennedy's Opinion of the Court, Parts II and III, joined by White, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia and Souter, controls with six votes. Stone-Zsela concurs separately in the seven-Justice reversal on his approved substantive-examination ground and joins no writing. O'Connor and Thomas dissent.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that immunity for private restraints depends on the State's actual responsibility for them. Parker v. Brown protects sovereign regulatory policy. Midcal requires both authorization and supervision when private parties fix prices, and Patrick requires officials to exercise substantive control over the challenged conduct. A clear policy permitting collective rates cannot answer whether these particular rates implement state judgment. The insurers' proposed reliance on staffed agencies, available vetoes and enforceable duties ends the inquiry too soon. Those features establish capacity; they do not establish the required exercise. This inquiry concerns whose economic decision governs, not whether federal judges consider state regulation efficient or wise. Southern Motor Carriers decided that state compulsion is unnecessary for clear articulation; active supervision was conceded there, so it does not make every negative-option system immune. The Court requires no prescribed hearing, paperwork or number of rejected rates. It leaves materially different comprehensive supervision, infrequent lapses and unilateral governmental decisions outside this private price-fixing record unresolved.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Parker v. Brown, 317 U.S. 341 (1943): applied for the sovereign-policy basis of state-action immunity; mere state permission for private restraints is not enough.
- California Retail Liquor Dealers Assn. v. Midcal Aluminum, Inc., 445 U.S. 97 (1980): applied and specified; its clear-articulation and active-supervision requirements remain independently necessary for this private rate fixing.
- Patrick v. Burget, 486 U.S. 94 (1988): applied; officials must possess and exercise substantive control, and limited judicial review does not substitute for it.
- Southern Motor Carriers Rate Conference, Inc. v. United States, 471 U.S. 48 (1985): distinguished; its rejection of a compulsion requirement concerns clear articulation, and its conceded supervision establishes no automatic rule for negative-option systems.
- Lafayette v. Louisiana Power & Light Co., 435 U.S. 389 (1978), and Cantor v. Detroit Edison Co., 428 U.S. 579 (1976): applied for refusing to infer immunity merely from regulation; limited state ends do not silently authorize all associated restraints.
- Columbia v. Omni Outdoor Advertising, Inc., 499 U.S. 365 (1991), Fisher v. Berkeley, 475 U.S. 260 (1986), and 324 Liquor Corp. v. Duffy, 479 U.S. 335 (1987): left intact; local zoning, unilateral governmental decisions and different methods of comprehensive rate supervision are not resolved by this record.

##### 2. Wisconsin and Montana did not actually supervise these private rates

**Holding and operative rule.** The supported findings that Wisconsin and Montana performed at most arithmetic checks, left some filings unchecked and allowed rates to remain effective despite missing requested information establish no actual substantive state supervision of these challenged private rate agreements. The agreements therefore receive no state-action antitrust immunity in those two States, notwithstanding the legal effect that state law assigns to official inaction.

**Authority.** Part III of Kennedy's Opinion of the Court, joined by White, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia and Souter, controls with six votes. Stone-Zsela separately agrees with the no-immunity disposition on these supported findings. O'Connor and Thomas would sustain immunity.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the actual findings defeat the immunity claim in Wisconsin and Montana. The Commission adopted findings that review extended at most to mathematical accuracy and sometimes did not occur. Montana allowed a filing to become effective although requested supporting information was never shown to have arrived. In Wisconsin, requested information followed seven years after a filing became effective. These are failures to exercise substantive control, not findings that the States chose economically mistaken rates. Patrick requires actual review of the private decision, and Midcal does not permit a State to confer immunity simply by leaving private prices in place. The insurers' argument that inaction legally signifies approval cannot overcome the established absence of the supervision federal immunity requires. Limited state judicial review cannot supply the missing agency role. The Court decides the claimed immunity, not the ultimate validity of every title-insurance charge. Other preserved statutory and petitioning defenses remain for the proceedings below, and no rate or monetary award is set here.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Patrick v. Burget, 486 U.S. 94 (1988): applied to the supported record; limited judicial review cannot cure the absence of substantive agency supervision.
- California Retail Liquor Dealers Assn. v. Midcal Aluminum, Inc., 445 U.S. 97 (1980): applied to defeat immunity where particular private prices lack actual state control.
- FTC v. Superior Court Trial Lawyers Assn., 493 U.S. 411 (1990): applied for the gravity of horizontal price fixing; this case does not expand the category of challenged conduct beyond the private rate agreements before it.

##### 3. Connecticut and Arizona require renewed review under the correct standard

**Holding and operative rule.** The Third Circuit must reconsider its Connecticut and Arizona active-supervision determinations under the substantive-supervision rule stated here and the statutory requirement that supported Commission fact findings are conclusive. The Court does not now resolve the disputed appellate treatment of those findings or finally decide immunity in either State.

**Authority.** Part IV of Kennedy's Opinion of the Court, joined by White, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia and Souter, supplies six votes for this remand. Stone-Zsela separately concurs in the same remedial direction; O'Connor and Thomas would affirm the existing judgment. Section 5(c), 15 U.S.C. §45(c), supplies the existing fact-review obligation; the Court establishes no new general administrative-review test.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court returns the Connecticut and Arizona questions for appellate reconsideration because the governing legal standard has changed the inquiry the Third Circuit must perform. Patrick and Midcal require actual substantive supervision of the private restraint, rather than the regulatory capacity that the court below treated as sufficient. The Commission also challenges that court's treatment of the findings concerning these two States. Section 5(c) makes supported Commission findings conclusive, while leaving the immunity rule for independent legal review. A correction of law therefore neither authorizes replacement of supported agency facts nor proves that every factual disagreement has already been resolved against the insurers. The appellate court should address those matters first under the proper rule. The Commission's request for review does not warrant a new Supreme Court finding about each regulatory act. The Court leaves the ultimate Connecticut and Arizona immunity applications, and the insurers' other preserved defenses, for the remand; Wisconsin and Montana remain governed by the no-immunity determination already made.

**Precedent treatment.**

- California Retail Liquor Dealers Assn. v. Midcal Aluminum, Inc., 445 U.S. 97 (1980), and Patrick v. Burget, 486 U.S. 94 (1988): applied as the governing legal framework on remand; no appellate freedom to replace supported agency findings is derived from them.

**Law after decision:** Effective June 12, 1992, actual independent substantive state judgment is necessary for state-action immunity from the challenged private rate fixing. Regulatory capacity and the legal effect of nonobjection do not suffice by themselves. Supported Wisconsin/Montana findings defeat that immunity; Connecticut/Arizona return for proper legal and factual review. Clear articulation remains independently necessary but conceded for these four States. The Court imposes no mandatory regulatory form and does not decide distinct sovereign action, materially different comprehensive supervision, infrequent lapses, other insurance-law exemptions or a difference in FTC/Sherman Act immunity. The six-Justice Court opinion supports each holding; no Marks synthesis or aggregation of separate opinions is needed.

#### Burlington Northern Railroad Co. v. Ford — 1992-06-12

Authority: `records/Burlington_Northern_Railroad_Co_v_Ford_merits_1992-06-12.md`.

##### 1. Montana may preserve a resident corporation's home-county preference without extending it to Burlington

**Holding and operative rule.** Montana's venue distinction satisfies equal protection as applied to Burlington, which is incorporated outside Montana and has its actual headquarters in Texas: the State may give greater weight to a resident corporation's home-county convenience while allowing plaintiffs to choose a county against this nonresident corporation. Ordinary rational-basis review permits incorporation to approximate headquarters location when the challenger has not shown that the resulting overinclusion or underinclusion makes the venue rule irrational.

**Authority.** Souter's Opinion of the Court, joined by White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas, controls with eight votes. Stone-Zsela separately concurs in the unanimous judgment on his approved as-applied convenience ground, with no formal join. No suspect classification or fundamental right to a preferred county is involved.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that Montana may balance plaintiff choice against a defendant's convenience in defending at its actual home. Fritz permits reasonable legislative line drawing, and Bain Peanut recognizes latitude in arranging venue. A corporation's headquarters can matter more to litigation convenience than one local office of a corporation headquartered elsewhere. Montana could therefore protect a resident corporation's home county while leaving this nonresident subject to the plaintiff's county choice. Burlington's substantial local business does not erase that distinction. Incorporation is an imperfect proxy, but Dukes permits approximation, and an easily verified incorporation rule can avoid collateral litigation over headquarters. Burlington has not shown an irrational mismatch on this record. Power Manufacturing invalidated Arkansas's different classification between counties of business activity and unrestricted venue for authorized foreign corporations; it does not decide this distinction based on actual headquarters. The Court does not rely on FELA policy or hypothetical federal venue, and leaves a foreign corporation actually headquartered in Montana for another case.

**Precedent treatment.**

- United States Railroad Retirement Board v. Fritz, 449 U.S. 166 (1980): applied; reasonable legislative accommodation of competing interests need not be uniquely correct or perfectly precise.
- Bain Peanut Co. of Texas v. Pinson, 282 U.S. 499 (1931): applied for latitude in ordinary venue arrangements, subject to equal protection.
- New Orleans v. Dukes, 427 U.S. 297 (1976), Hughes v. Alexandria Scrap Corp., 426 U.S. 794 (1976), and Lindsley v. Natural Carbonic Gas Co., 220 U.S. 61 (1911): applied for permissible imprecision in rational classifications.
- United States v. Sperry Corp., 493 U.S. 52 (1989), and Dallas v. Stanglin, 490 U.S. 19 (1989): applied to ordinary rational-basis review where neither a suspect class nor a fundamental right is involved.
- Metropolitan Casualty Ins. Co. v. Brownell, 294 U.S. 580 (1935), and G. D. Searle & Co. v. Cohn, 455 U.S. 404 (1982): applied for reasonable corporate-location assumptions and the challenger's failure to show an irrational classification on this record.
- Power Manufacturing Co. v. Saunders, 274 U.S. 490 (1927): distinguished; Arkansas's materially different place-of-business classification does not invalidate Montana's more closely fitted headquarters approximation. Its equal-protection holding is not overruled.
- American Motorists Ins. Co. v. Starnes, 425 U.S. 637 (1976): its doubts about Power's continuing force are acknowledged without treating them as an overruling; it also supports reviewability of this final venue determination under §1257(a).
- Citizens & Southern National Bank v. Bougas, 434 U.S. 35 (1977), Denver & Rio Grande Western Railroad Co. v. Brotherhood of Railroad Trainmen, 387 U.S. 556 (1967), and Van Dusen v. Barrack, 376 U.S. 612 (1964): applied for venue's function of accommodating the parties' competing interests in the place of trial.

##### 2. Burlington cannot obtain relief for the hypothetical mismatch that would not affect its own venue

**Holding and operative rule.** Independently, Burlington cannot obtain relief on the objection that incorporation imperfectly substitutes for actual corporate headquarters, because a rule using actual headquarters would still treat this Texas-headquartered defendant as a nonresident. The Court does not decide the equal-protection claim of a foreign corporation whose actual headquarters is in Montana.

**Authority.** Souter's Opinion of the Court, joined by White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas, adopts this independently sufficient alternative with eight votes. Stone-Zsela reserves the hypothetical corporation but adds no independent claim-limitation ground; his concurrence supports only the approved as-applied venue rationale.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the suggested defect would not alter Burlington's treatment. Roberts & Schaefer requires a corporate challenger to connect the challenged distinction to its own claimed disadvantage, and Raines preserves the usual rule against invalidating an application by invoking hypothetical injury to others. Burlington's headquarters is in Fort Worth. Replacing incorporation with actual headquarters as the venue criterion would therefore leave it outside Montana's resident-corporation preference. Its Hill County office does not become the corporation's actual home merely because Burlington identifies that office as its principal Montana location. The contention about a foreign corporation genuinely headquartered in Montana concerns a materially different application and supplies Burlington no entitlement to transfer. This alternative does not insulate all incorporation classifications from review; it follows from the precise defect Burlington alleges and the fact that correcting that defect would not help it. The Court leaves that hypothetical application, separate commerce objections and the employees' FELA merits undecided.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Roberts & Schaefer Co. v. Emmerson, 271 U.S. 50 (1926): applied to bar relief based on a classification defect whose correction would not benefit this challenger.
- United States v. Raines, 362 U.S. 17 (1960): applied by analogy for the usual limit on invoking unconstitutional applications to others; no categorical exception or new facial-challenge rule is announced.

**Law after decision:** Effective June 12, 1992, Montana's corporate-venue rule is constitutional as applied to Burlington's established out-of-state headquarters. A State may weigh an actual resident home county more heavily than a nonresident corporation's local office, and rational approximation need not be exact. Independently, changing incorporation to actual headquarters would not aid Burlington. Power Manufacturing's distinct equal-protection holding remains intact. No preferred-county fundamental right, general permission to discriminate against foreign corporations, FELA merits rule, personal-jurisdiction rule or Commerce Clause holding is created. The eight-Justice Court opinion controls both holdings without a Marks synthesis or reliance on Stone's separate ground.

#### United States v. Alvarez-Machain — 1992-06-15

Authority: `records/United_States_v_Alvarez_Machain_merits_1992-06-15.md`.

##### This Treaty does not bar prosecution after the abduction

**Holding and operative rule.** The United States–Mexico Extradition Treaty does not prohibit this abduction or make extradition its exclusive means of obtaining a defendant’s presence. Because the Treaty supplies no restriction barring this prosecution, the Ker–Frisbie rule permits trial notwithstanding the DEA-sponsored forcible transfer and Mexico’s protest; the treaty-based dismissal and repatriation order must be reversed.

**Authority.** White’s Opinion of the Court, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Thomas; five votes support the treaty construction, its application, and resulting remand.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that this Treaty does not withdraw the court’s power to try respondent. Its text creates obligations to surrender persons in specified circumstances and prescribes procedures when extradition is invoked. Article9 reserves a decision within that process; it does not state that every acquisition of custody must occur through it. Rauscher enforces limits attached to an actual treaty surrender, and Cook enforces an established territorial seizure restriction. Neither requires courts to infer this different prohibition from an agreement that supplies none. Ker and Frisbie therefore govern the consequence for trial authority. Official sponsorship and Mexico’s protest make the conduct different from a private kidnapping, but do not themselves add a term to the Treaty. General international-law objections likewise cannot establish a particular extradition obligation without support in the compact or relevant extradition practice. The Court’s conclusion permits prosecution; it does not authorize abduction, adjudicate guilt, or resolve the conduct’s legality under other law. Diplomatic return outside this Treaty remains an Executive matter.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Ker v. Illinois, 119 U.S.436 (1886): applied to trial authority after this official international abduction because the Treaty supplies no contrary limitation; not treated as affirmative authorization to seize.
- Frisbie v. Collins, 342 U.S.519 (1952): retained and applied for the distinction between unlawful capture and the power to conduct a lawful trial.
- United States v. Rauscher, 119 U.S.407 (1886): retained; its enforceable treaty limitation follows actual extradition on specified terms and does not make this different Treaty exclusive.
- Cook v. United States, 288 U.S.102 (1933): distinguished; an established treaty restriction on governmental seizure controls there, and no comparable restriction is found here.
- Air France v. Saks, 470 U.S.392 (1985), and Valentine v. United States ex rel. Neidecker, 299 U.S.5 (1936): applied to begin with the Treaty’s terms and determine the adopted obligation rather than assume it.

**Law after decision:** The Treaty does not supply a prosecution bar on these facts; ordinary criminal proceedings may resume. Rauscher and Cook remain enforceable where a treaty supplies the relevant restriction, and Ker–Frisbie remains the default for trial authority after unlawful capture. The holding does not grant authority to abduct, settle general international legality, foreclose every other remedy, or impose a universal rule against individual treaty enforcement. Neither dissent changes controlling law.

#### Ankenbrandt v. Richards — 1992-06-15

Authority: `records/Ankenbrandt_v_Richards_merits_1992-06-15.md`.

##### Diversity jurisdiction and the limited domestic-relations exception

**Holding and operative rule.** Article III does not exclude domestic-relations disputes from the federal judicial power.
