Sited compacts may still refuse outside waste at the transition's end, so severance does not transfer New York's burden to their residents. Declaratory relief removes the compulsory ownership, possession, and liability mechanism. It neither drafts a federal substitute nor erases the otherwise lawful distributions and access choices.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Alaska Airlines, Inc. v. Brock, 480 U.S. 678 (1987): applied without changing the severability standard or treating silence as nonseverability.
- Reagan v. Farmers' Loan & Trust Co., 154 U.S. 362 (1894): applied; the failure of an enforcement device need not defeat the independently operative statutory purpose.
- United States v. Jackson, 390 U.S. 570 (1968): applied; an invalid enforcement measure may be severed while preserving an independently functioning scheme.

**Present operation.** The monetary and access mechanisms continue. The compulsory title, possession, and liability mechanism is invalid and severed. The general responsibility language does not independently compel regulation. Congress retains direct authority over the interstate disposal market, authorized conditional spending, and lawful cooperative arrangements; Garcia's generally applicable regulation of state activity is retained. This decision clarifies and extends Hodel's structural limitation to the unavoidable choice between state administration and compulsory ownership of private waste. Actual compact duties, direct regulation of State market conduct, and independently authorized state-court or judicial duties remain distinct. The Guarantee Clause claim fails, but general justiciability remains open because Stone-Zsela's affirmative reach is not the Court's rule. Alaska Airlines is applied without revision. No Marks extraction is needed: every controlling holding has at least five formal Court-opinion adherents.

#### Wisconsin Department of Revenue v. William Wrigley, Jr., Co. — 1992-06-19

Source: `records/Wisconsin_Department_of_Revenue_v_William_Wrigley_Jr_Co_merits_1992-06-19.md`.

### Solicitation includes its ancillary means, not independent business functions

**Holding and operative rule.** Under §381(a), solicitation includes express or implicit requests for purchases and activities serving no independent business function apart from requesting orders; customary assignment to salespeople, promotional usefulness or a connection with future sales does not by itself protect an independent service, delivery or inventory function. The statutory office distinction remains: maintaining an office attributable to the company exceeds solicitation even if its purpose is solicitation, subject to §381(c)’s express independent-contractor provision; ordinary use of a representative’s home or temporary meeting place is not automatically such an office.

**Authority.** Parts I–II of Scalia’s opinion, joined by White, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter and Thomas, supply six votes for the standard; Part III is joined by White, Stevens, Souter and Thomas, supplying five votes for its stated application. Stone-Zsela’s judgment concurrence is not a formal join and is not needed to establish authority.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that Congress protected the process of asking for business and the means serving that process. Heublein identifies the statute’s aim of establishing a clear minimum protection, but leaves this definition open. A request-only reading would exclude travel, samples and ordinary arrangements needed to solicit, while a customary-salesperson reading would let employers determine statutory immunity by allocating duties. Neither respects the enacted activity. Wrigley’s recruitment, training, evaluation, meetings and limited credit mediation facilitate solicitation without an independent function. Regular replacement of spoiled gum performs a service the company has reason to provide apart from requesting orders. Stock-check transactions supply gum for payment, and inventory maintained principally for these exchanges and sales supports those independent functions. Their usefulness in preserving customer relations does not change that conclusion. The statutory office provision separately controls a company office; the uncompensated home arrangements here do not establish one. The Court rejects an automatic before-sale/after-sale boundary and leaves materially different services to application of the stated rule.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Heublein v. South Carolina Tax Commission, 409 U.S. 275: applied for the enacted minimum-activity protection and distinguished from its separate state-regulation question; its reserved solicitation boundary is now defined.
- Northwestern States Portland Cement v. Minnesota, 358 U.S. 450: retained as the constitutional background to the later statute; constitutional tax power is not equated with statutory permission.
- FMC Corp. v. Holliday, 498 U.S. 52: applied for determining the fair scope of Congress’s express displacement of state law; no additional presumption narrows §381’s conceded subject.
- Sorenson v. Secretary of Treasury, 475 U.S. 851: applied for consistent meaning within the statute, preserving the office distinction in §381(c).
- Clairol, Inc. v. Kingsley, 402 U.S. 902: the summary disposition does not establish a request-only definition; its judgment is undisturbed.
- Anderson v. Celebrezze, 460 U.S. 780: applied to distinguish a summary disposition from adoption of every lower-court rationale.

### Truly trivial departures do not destroy protection; Wrigley’s combined functions are not trivial

**Holding and operative rule.** Activities outside protected solicitation do not defeat §381 protection when they are genuinely de minimis—too slight to create a nontrivial additional connection with the taxing State—but the inquiry considers the unprotected activities together and is not resolved by their small proportion of the company’s total sales. Wrigley’s recurring stale-gum exchanges, stock-check sales and supporting inventory together create that additional connection, making §381 protection unavailable for the assessed years.

**Authority.** Part II of Scalia’s opinion, joined by White, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter and Thomas, establishes the six-vote qualification. Part III, joined by White, Stevens, Souter and Thomas, supplies five votes for the combined-activity application. O’Connor independently finds inventory and stock-check sales sufficient without counting stale-gum replacement; her separate alternative is not a Court holding.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the statute does not make a genuinely trivial departure destroy an otherwise protected business arrangement. The ordinary principle that law disregards trifles remains part of the background of enacted rules; Abbott Laboratories illustrates that principle without supplying a fixed numerical threshold. Section 381’s word only limits the protected categories, but does not compel taxing all interstate income because of an isolated insignificant act. The qualification must serve this statute’s purpose: the question is whether additional activity creates a nontrivial connection with the State. Here the sales force regularly replaced stale gum under company policy, held stock for those exchanges, and used that stock for occasional paid deliveries. Considered together, those ongoing functions are substantial enough to remove the statutory protection, even though their size is small beside Wrigley’s overall Wisconsin sales. The Court need not decide whether any single activity would be trivial alone. No fixed dollar line, sales-percentage safe harbor or decision about a rare isolated act follows.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Abbott Laboratories v. Portland Retail Druggists Association, 425 U.S. 1: its recognition of the ordinary triviality principle supports a narrow statutory qualification, not a new numerical safe harbor.
- Heublein v. South Carolina Tax Commission, 409 U.S. 275: the statutory minimum-activity objective determines the relevant connection; its independent local-transfer holding remains intact.
- Northwestern States Portland Cement v. Minnesota, 358 U.S. 450: Congress’s statutory protection remains distinct from the constitutional minimum; no constitutional nexus ruling follows here.

**Present operation.** Effective June 19, 1992, §381 solicitation is governed by the entirely-ancillary/no-independent-business-function boundary and the nontrivial-additional-connection qualification. These define previously open statutory components. The out-of-state approval and fulfillment requirements, statutory office/independent-contractor distinction, and separate constitutional tax rules remain. No fixed amount, sales ratio, automatic postsale exclusion, tax assessment amount, or broader constitutional rule is adopted. The independent sufficiency of each unprotected activity remains undecided.

#### American National Red Cross v. S. G. — 1992-06-19

Source: `records/American_National_Red_Cross_v_S_G_merits_1992-06-19.md`.

### The express federal-court clause grants original jurisdiction

**Holding and operative rule.** The Red Cross charter’s authorization to sue and be sued in courts of law and equity, State or Federal, confers original federal jurisdiction over actions to which the Red Cross is a party. In construing a congressional charter’s sue-and-be-sued clause, a specific mention of federal courts permits a jurisdictional reading that general corporate-capacity language does not; the charter grant here operates independently of federal incorporation alone.

**Authority.** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, joined by White, Blackmun, Stevens and Thomas; five of nine participating Justices adopt the charter construction at this level of generality. Stone-Zsela agrees on his separate charter-specific ground but supplies no formal join.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that Congress gave the Red Cross access to a federal forum through the charter itself. Deveaux denied jurisdiction under general language granting capacity to sue. Osborn distinguished that language from an express reference to federal courts, and Bankers Trust preserved the distinction when construing another general capacity clause. D’Oench identified materially identical State-or-Federal language as a jurisdictional source. Congress added the federal reference to the Red Cross charter five years later, against that legal background. The existence of additional jurisdictional language in D’Oench does not erase the Court’s treatment of the clause it identified. Nor does the clause’s place among corporate powers prevent it from conferring both capacity and jurisdiction. The reference to state courts preserves the concurrent forum rather than compelling a capacity-only reading. Section1349 restricts jurisdiction resting merely on incorporation; this specific grant supplies the additional statutory authority. The Court does not decide the independent question of §1349’s application to nonstock corporations or create a rule for materially different charter provisions.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Bank of the United States v. Deveaux, 9 U.S.61: distinguished; its general-capacity clause remains insufficient to grant federal jurisdiction.
- Osborn v. Bank of the United States, 22 U.S.738: applied for the jurisdictional effect of express federal-forum language.
- Bankers Trust Co. v. Texas & Pacific Railway, 241 U.S.295: distinguished; its generalized court language remains a capacity provision.
- D’Oench, Duhme & Co. v. FDIC, 315 U.S.447: applied for its stated jurisdictional use of materially comparable State-or-Federal language; no FDIC substantive rule is imported.
- Cannon v. University of Chicago, 441 U.S.677: applied for Congress’s use of language with an established judicial meaning.
- United States v. Merriam, 263 U.S.179: applied for interpreting statutory words against their established judicial meaning.

### The special grant supports removal without a §1331 complaint

**Holding and operative rule.** Because the charter gives original federal jurisdiction over this Red Cross action, §1441 permits its removal even though the complaint alleges state-law torts. The well-pleaded-complaint limitation on statutory arising-under jurisdiction under §1331 does not eliminate this separate charter grant.

**Authority.** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, joined by White, Blackmun, Stevens and Thomas; the same five Justices adopt this necessary answer to the alternative removal objection.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that a state-law complaint may be removed when Congress independently grants original federal jurisdiction over the action. The complaint rule ordinarily requires the federal question to appear in the plaintiff’s own claim when jurisdiction depends on §1331. Verlinden distinguishes that statutory inquiry from jurisdiction granted on a separate basis. Here the Red Cross relies on its charter, and §1441 supplies the removal mechanism once original jurisdiction exists. Requiring the plaintiffs to plead a federal tort would silently reduce the special grant to ordinary federal-question jurisdiction and deprive the charter language of its distinct work. The plaintiffs’ reliance on the state-law character of their claims therefore does not defeat removal. This conclusion changes the available forum, not the governing elements of the tort claims. The Court does not establish diversity as an alternative basis, determine whether additional parties should be joined, or resolve a separately available abstention argument. Those questions retain their own legal requirements in further proceedings.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Verlinden B. V. v. Central Bank of Nigeria, 461 U.S.480: applied to distinguish §1331’s statutory arising-under limits from a separate original-jurisdiction grant.
- Osborn v. Bank of the United States, 22 U.S.738: the special charter basis remains distinct from the substantive law governing the controversy.
- D’Oench, Duhme & Co. v. FDIC, 315 U.S.447: its charter jurisdiction is not merged into the substantive law governing a particular controversy.

### Article III permits the special corporate-party grant

**Holding and operative rule.** Article III’s arising-under judicial power permits Congress to confer original federal jurisdiction over actions involving a federally chartered corporation such as the Red Cross, although the immediate claim arises under state law. The charter’s specific jurisdictional grant therefore lies within Congress’s constitutional authority.

**Authority.** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, joined by White, Blackmun, Stevens and Thomas; five Justices decide the constitutional objection. The dissent does not reach it, and Stone-Zsela supplies only his separate approved ground.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the statutory grant stays within Article III. Osborn established that Congress may authorize federal adjudication of cases involving a federally created corporation because federal law supplies its legal existence and powers. The presence of other questions governed by state law does not remove that federal foundation from the case. Bankers Trust retained the constitutional principle even while recognizing statutory restrictions on jurisdiction, and Verlinden likewise distinguishes the breadth of Article III from narrower general jurisdictional statutes. The plaintiffs’ reliance on the ordinary statutory complaint rule therefore does not show a constitutional defect in this different grant. Section1349 addresses Congress’s statutory choice; it does not narrow the Constitution’s judicial power or prevent Congress from making the additional specific grant at issue. The Court applies the established charter principle and has no occasion to reconsider its outer limits. It decides jurisdiction over this Red Cross action, leaving substantive duties, liability, defenses and relief to the ordinary proceedings.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Osborn v. Bank of the United States, 22 U.S.738: applied and retained as the constitutional basis for corporate-party federal jurisdiction.
- Bankers Trust Co. v. Texas & Pacific Railway, 241 U.S.295: its constitutional account remains distinct from its restrictive statutory result.
- Verlinden B. V. v. Central Bank of Nigeria, 461 U.S.480: applied for the distinction between ArticleIII judicial power and §1331’s statutory scope.

**Present operation.** Effective June19,1992, the charter itself provides original federal jurisdiction and a §1441 removal basis for this Red Cross action; a §1331 federal claim is not required. ArticleIII permits that specific grant. Section1349’s general incorporation limitation and ordinary capacity-only precedents remain, with the nonstock question unresolved. Federal jurisdiction does not create federal substantive tort law, immunity or liability. Other charter language, diversity, joinder, and independently available abstention are reserved.

#### Wright v. West — 1992-06-19

Source: `records/Wright_v_West_merits_1992-06-19.md`.

### The trial record permits a rational finding of larceny under Jackson

**Holding and operative rule.** On the trial record here, the quantity and recency of West’s possession of distinctive items from the same theft, considered with the jury’s permissible rejection of his incomplete account, allow a rational trier of fact to find the elements of larceny beyond a reasonable doubt; the exhausted Jackson insufficiency claim therefore warrants no habeas relief. The reviewing court must assess the evidence and permissible credibility choices favorably to the prosecution, without retrying guilt, shifting the State’s burden or treating possession as an invariably sufficient substitute for proof of theft.

**Authority.** Thomas and Scalia expressly adopt the Jackson application in the principal opinion; White expressly endorses that sufficiency demonstration in his judgment concurrence; O’Connor, Blackmun and Stevens adopt the same record-specific sufficiency conclusion in their concurrence; Kennedy expressly adopts it in his concurrence; and Stone-Zsela independently adopts it in his approved judgment concurrence. These eight Justices support this same operative proposition through their own writings. These are express proposition commitments, not inferred formal joins. Souter is excluded because his ground is Teague-only. No complete opinion commands a majority, and no broader holding is synthesized under Marks.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the evidence satisfies Jackson’s requirement of a rational finding beyond a reasonable doubt. Jackson requires favorable consideration of the prosecution’s evidence and the jury’s permissible resolution of conflicting inferences; Winship leaves proof of every element with the State. Numerous distinctive items from the same theft were found in West’s possession soon afterward. The jury could evaluate the gaps and confusion in his account rather than accept his description of flea-market purchases as resolving the case. The absence of direct evidence placing him at the house, and the possibility that goods passed through other hands, do not compel a finding that every inference of theft was irrational. The circuit’s reassessment gave insufficient effect to the jury’s permitted credibility choices. This is a conclusion about the affirmative possession evidence together with the actual explanation, not a rule that disbelief alone proves a missing element. The Court does not decide the separate new-evidence claim, the facial validity of every possession inference or an unnecessary new habeas-review standard.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Jackson v. Virginia, 443 U.S.307: applied without change to the actual trial record; rational jury inferences receive their prescribed weight.
- In re Winship, 397 U.S.358: retained; the State must prove each element beyond reasonable doubt and no burden shifts to West.
- Barnes v. United States, 412 U.S.837: its permissive-inference principle is consistent background, not a rule automatically equating any possession with theft.
- Cosby v. Jones, 682 F.2d1373 (11th Cir.1982): its contextual considerations do not replace Jackson or support the circuit’s insufficiency conclusion on this record; no general five-factor constitutional test is adopted.

**Present operation.** The existing Jackson standard is applied without a new burden, proof quantum, presumption, corroboration requirement or categorical possession-to-theft rule. The narrow sufficiency application has eight express supporters, although no complete opinion has five joins. Teague and its existing exceptions, Miller’s legal/fact distinction, and the then-existing §2254(d) remain; the Court adopts no general reasonable-state-decision rule. The separate writings disagree over Teague sequencing and its application here. Souter’s new-rule ground has no controlling coalition. O’Connor, Blackmun, Stevens and Kennedy’s broader independent-review discussion is not converted into a new general holding by Stone’s narrower approved position. Marks supplies no common rationale combining a Teague bar with a merits denial. The alternative Elkins claim remains undecided.

#### United States v. Salerno — 1992-06-19

Source: `records/United_States_v_Salerno_merits_1992-06-19.md`.

### Every condition of the former-testimony exception remains necessary

**Holding and operative rule.** A party offering an unavailable witness's former testimony under Federal Rule of Evidence 804(b)(1) must establish that the opposing party had both an opportunity and a similar motive to develop that testimony; the Government's power to immunize the witness at trial and an appeal to adversarial fairness do not dispense with either requirement. Similar motive must be considered on the actual proceedings and arguments; this decision neither finds that condition satisfied here nor establishes a categorical exclusion of grand-jury testimony.

**Authority.** Thomas's Opinion of the Court, joined in full by White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter; seven of nine participating Justices adopt this rule and explanation. Stone concurs in the judgment on his separately stated compatible rule and qualifications; he is not a formal opinion joiner. Stevens dissents from the remand on actual-motive application, not on an exemption from the Rule.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the former-testimony exception requires the showing Congress specified. Rule 804(b)(1) joins opportunity and similar motive as separate conditions, and neither favorable testimony nor the opposing party's power to obtain live testimony removes those words. Green v. Bock Laundry Machine Co. supplies the obligation to respect enacted evidentiary restrictions rather than revise them for a preferred result. Dennis v. United States permits access to grand-jury materials under the applicable disclosure rule; it does not make those materials admissible for their truth. Here the court of appeals made similar motive unnecessary because the Government could immunize the witnesses. It therefore did not fully consider the parties' opposing accounts of the actual examination and investigative purposes. The Government's assertion that the trial judge made a conclusive factual finding does not require this Court to decide that unresolved dispute first. The case returns for consideration under the correct Rule. Compelled immunity, other exceptions and an independently preserved constitutional ground remain outside this holding.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Green v. Bock Laundry Machine Co., 490 U.S. 504 (1989): applied for enforcing the enacted evidentiary rule rather than altering it for a preferred litigation result; no revision of Green's separate impeachment rule.
- Dennis v. United States, 384 U.S. 855 (1966): distinguished as a disclosure decision under Rule 6(e); its protection of access to relevant grand-jury material remains fully operative without creating automatic trial admissibility.
- United States v. Abel, 469 U.S. 45 (1984): its respect for the enacted evidentiary scheme remains consistent with this application; its distinct bias-impeachment rule is unchanged.

### Contradictory independent evidence does not itself forfeit the objection

**Holding and operative rule.** When the Government offers independent evidence contradicting unavailable witnesses' grand-jury accounts, but does not present any version of those accounts to the trial jury, that act alone does not forfeit its Rule 804(b)(1) objection. Even assuming a privilege-forfeiture analogy is available, mere contradiction is not disclosure of the protected communication; this decision does not establish that every hearsay objection is a testimonial privilege.

**Authority.** Thomas's Opinion of the Court, joined by White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter; seven Justices expressly reject the asserted forfeiture ground at this limited level of generality. This is a distinct controlling answer to the defendants' alternative admissibility theory, not a finding of ultimate inadmissibility.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the defendants' forfeiture argument fails on its own terms. They rely on the principle that a party cannot disclose its version of a privileged communication and then prevent use of the communication needed to test that version. United States v. Miller, the cited Fifth Circuit decision, concerns that form of selective disclosure; it does not treat every contradictory litigation position as a disclosure. Here the Government used contractors, recordings and documents to establish Cedar Park's participation. It did not tell the jury what DeMatteis or Bruno had said to the grand jury. The contrary theory would replace a showing of disclosure with the ordinary fact that opposing evidence conflicts. The Court does not decide that the hearsay rule is generally equivalent to a testimonial privilege, and it does not decide the consequences of an actual selective presentation of former testimony. The defendants remain free on remand to establish the actual similar motive that the enacted exception requires.

**Precedent treatment.**

- United States v. Miller, 600 F.2d 498 (5th Cir. 1979): distinguished on the selective-disclosure premise; the Court rejects the proposed analogy without adopting a general rule equating hearsay with testimonial privilege.

### Application and questions not reached

The Second Circuit's elimination of similar motive is reversed as to the seven respondents before this Court. The parties' actual-motive arguments and resulting admissibility and judgment consequences return for consideration. No finding of actual similar motive, perjury, ultimate guilt, immunity entitlement or constitutional admissibility is made. Other preserved appellate grounds remain; the separate relief of Ianniello, who is not a respondent here, is outside this writ. There is no fractured controlling rationale requiring Marks analysis.

**Present operation.** Effective June 19, Rule 804(b)(1)'s opportunity and similar-motive requirements apply without the court of appeals' immunity-based fairness exception. Contradiction through independent evidence alone does not forfeit the objection. Before this event no current decision resolved these precise trial-admissibility questions. White v. Illinois (January 15) remains an exception-sensitive Confrontation Clause decision; United States v. Williams (May 4) retains its confined grand-jury disclosure duty and qualified dismissal remedy. Neither supplies automatic trial admission, and neither is overruled or narrowed. The actual motive, other preserved evidentiary/constitutional grounds and their judgment consequences remain unresolved.

#### Reynolds v. International Amateur Athletic Federation — 1992-06-20

Source: `records/Reynolds_v_International_Amateur_Athletic_Federation_application_for_stay_1992-06-20.md`.

The unexplained interim order creates no controlling substantive holding or reusable stay, jurisdiction, exhaustion, sports-access, or third-party-harm rule. Its binding effect is the specific relief in Judgment & Remedy. No rationale is extracted from the vote or from a Justice's unpublished ground; no Marks analysis applies.

**Present operation.** No controlling doctrine changes on June20. The Court's ordinary appellate channel and existing equitable principles remain in force. The only new operative legal consequence is the trials-specific suspension of the appellate stay.

#### Sawyer v. Whitley — 1992-06-22

Source: `records/Sawyer_v_Whitley_merits_1992-06-22.md`.

### Capital innocence as a gateway to otherwise barred claims

**Holding and operative rule.** A prisoner seeking review of an otherwise successive, abusive or procedurally defaulted capital habeas claim without the ordinarily required cause and prejudice must show by clear and convincing evidence that, absent constitutional error, no reasonable juror would find the prisoner eligible for death under applicable state law. The inquiry includes the offense, required aggravating facts and other conditions of death eligibility; additional mitigation bearing only on the discretionary choice of punishment does not satisfy this gateway.

**Authority.** Kennedy's Opinion of the Court, joined in full by White, Scalia, Souter and Thomas; all five adopt the precise eligibility rule and clear-and-convincing quantum. The four judgment concurrences do not join that rule. Five of nine participating Justices make it controlling without Marks.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that an exceptional gateway must remain distinct from the constitutional claim it permits a court to hear. Murray v. Carrier preserves review when constitutional error probably convicted an actually innocent person; Smith v. Murray recognizes a sentencing application while distinguishing factual from merely legal innocence. McCleskey v. Zant preserves the narrow exception alongside cause and prejudice. Zant v. Stephens and Lowenfield v. Phelps distinguish the facts authorizing death from the subsequent individualized choice. The gateway therefore reaches all necessary eligibility conditions, rather than only elements of the capital crime. It does not extend to additional mitigation affecting only that discretionary choice. Lockett and Eddings continue to require constitutionally adequate consideration of mitigation on an available claim; they do not remove the separate obstacle to reopening a barred claim. Treating ordinary sentencing prejudice as innocence would eliminate the cause requirement. The clear-and-convincing showing protects the exception's extraordinary character. The Court leaves independently applicable exhaustion, retroactivity and constitutional merits requirements intact and decides no freestanding innocence claim.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Murray v. Carrier, 477 U.S. 478 (1986): preserved for the ordinary cause-and-prejudice requirement and miscarriage exception; this decision specifies the capital-eligibility branch without changing the crime-innocence standard.
- Smith v. Murray, 477 U.S. 527 (1986): its open capital-sentencing application is specified through eligibility, not discretionary mitigation reweighing.
- McCleskey v. Zant, 499 U.S. 467 (1991): applied; abuse of the writ remains a separate obstacle unless cause and prejudice or the applicable exceptional gateway is established.
- Kuhlmann v. Wilson, 477 U.S. 436 (1986): its plurality's factual-innocence discussion informs the successive-claim setting; no plurality statement is retrospectively converted into a majority holding.
- Dugger v. Adams, 489 U.S. 401 (1989): applied; an error capable of affecting sentencing accuracy does not itself demonstrate the exceptional innocence showing.
- Zant v. Stephens, 462 U.S. 862 (1983), and Lowenfield v. Phelps, 484 U.S. 231 (1988): relied on for the distinction between narrowing eligibility and selecting punishment; their distinct capital holdings are unchanged.
- Lockett v. Ohio, 438 U.S. 586 (1978), and Eddings v. Oklahoma, 455 U.S. 104 (1982): preserved for mitigation on the merits; their protection does not independently reopen a barred claim.
- Strickland v. Washington, 466 U.S. 668 (1984), and United States v. Bagley, 473 U.S. 667 (1985): distinguished; their reasonable-probability merits standards are not the clear-and-convincing gateway.

### Sawyer's additional evidence does not satisfy the gateway

**Holding and operative rule.** On the whole record, Sawyer's offered psychiatric and family evidence concerns mitigation, and his impeachment affidavits and caregiver account do not clearly and convincingly establish that no reasonable juror would find the surviving grounds of death eligibility. The Brady and successive sentencing-ineffectiveness claims therefore remain barred; denial of further merits review supplies no new finding that every allegation is false or that death is mandatory.

**Authority.** Kennedy's Opinion of the Court, joined by White, Scalia, Souter and Thomas; five adopt this application of the controlling gateway. Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens and O'Connor independently support affirmance on their broader grounds and do not supply joins in the Court's application rationale.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that these submissions leave a reasonable factual basis for death eligibility. Louisiana requires a valid aggravating circumstance, and its law of principals reaches participation in aggravated arson without requiring that Sawyer personally ignite the victim. The caregiver's account therefore does not erase the significance of the other evidence, including Sawyer's participation and the lighter-fluid evidence. The preceding beating and scalding also support the surviving cruelty aggravator independently of who applied the flame. The affidavits attacking Shano's credibility do not establish that every reasonable juror would reject the core account. This is an assessment of these submissions, not a categorical rule excluding later impeachment evidence. The psychiatric records and childhood evidence may support mitigation but do not negate the necessary eligibility facts presented here. Brady and Strickland supply the underlying constitutional inquiries; the Court does not decide those merits after the gateway fails. Nor does it reopen the accepted procedural classifications and cause determination or direct a new factual hearing, sentence or release.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Brady v. Maryland, 373 U.S. 83 (1963): the suppressed-evidence claim remains subject to the independent procedural gateway; no Brady merits adjudication occurs.
- Strickland v. Washington, 466 U.S. 668 (1984): the previously rejected sentencing-assistance claim is not reopened; the added evidence does not replace the gateway with ordinary prejudice.
- Sawyer v. Smith, 497 U.S. 227 (1990): its distinct Caldwell retroactivity disposition remains intact; this event does not revive that earlier claim.

### Scope and questions not reached

The Court does not determine the merits of the two barred constitutional claims, find the new evidence false in every respect, hold death mandatory, decide a freestanding innocence claim, revisit the prior Caldwell ruling, or resolve a new challenge to the wording of a Louisiana aggravator. The distinct competency claim discussed below was not among the two claims presented in this Supreme Court petition. Its omission creates no new competency holding. The gateway does not itself grant relief even when satisfied.

**Present operation.** Effective June 22, 1992, the capital miscarriage gateway for barred claims requires clear and convincing evidence of the absence of every lawful death-eligibility basis under the no-reasonable-juror inquiry. Before this event, Carrier, Smith and McCleskey left the precise capital formulation open; the proof quantum and eligibility-only scope are now specified. Ordinary merits prejudice, cause, exhaustion, retroactivity and properly available factual-development rules remain distinct. The broader sentencing-selection tests in the concurrences are noncontrolling. No Marks synthesis is necessary because five directly join the Court’s rule.

#### R. A. V. v. City of St. Paul — 1992-06-22

Source: `records/R_A_V_v_City_of_St_Paul_merits_1992-06-22.md`.

### Substantial overbreadth despite the state-law construction

**Holding and operative rule.** A criminal prohibition is facially invalid when, even under its authoritative state-law construction, it reaches substantial protected expression relative to its legitimate sweep and that defect cannot be cured by a permissible limiting construction or partial invalidation. St. Paul's ordinance fails that rule because its injury and resentment coverage extends beyond personally abusive provocation of an immediate breach of the peace; the ordinance charge must be dismissed, while independently valid charges remain available.

**Authority.** Chief Justice Stone-Zsela's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Blackmun, Stevens and O'Connor, supplies five of nine votes for this exact overbreadth rule and application. Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas concur in the judgment on a distinct content-discrimination ground. No Marks synthesis is needed or permitted to enlarge the five-Justice holding.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that causing resentment does not by itself turn expression into fighting words. Chaplinsky permits a narrowly confined prohibition, while Cohen, Gooding and Lewis establish that offensive ideas and generalized verbal injury do not satisfy its constitutional boundary. Broadrick requires substantial protected coverage before a court invalidates a law on its face. That requirement is met here: religious criticism and hostile debate over race or gender may cause deep anger without personal provocation of immediate violence. The Minnesota Supreme Court's construction binds the Court as a matter of state law, but its repeated injury formulation leaves that protected coverage in place. Calling the coverage fighting words cannot settle the federal question. Nor does its additional reference to Brandenburg's incitement category eliminate the broader branch. The grave alleged invasion of this family's yard does not cure the ordinance's ordinary unconstitutional applications. The Court preserves prosecution under independently valid laws and reserves whether a properly confined prohibition may select particularly harmful instances of genuinely unprotected expression.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Chaplinsky v. New Hampshire,315 U.S.568 (1942): applied within its narrow constitutional scope; generalized emotional injury is not a freestanding fighting-words category.
- Cohen v. California,403 U.S.15 (1971): applied; offensive expression is distinct from personally directed fighting words.
- Gooding v. Wilson,405 U.S.518 (1972): applied; an authoritative construction must actually confine a criminal prohibition to unprotected expression.
- Lewis v. City of New Orleans,415 U.S.130 (1974): applied; a state-law narrowing label does not cure remaining protected coverage.
- Broadrick v. Oklahoma,413 U.S.601 (1973): applied; overbreadth must be real and substantial relative to legitimate sweep, with permissible narrowing considered first.
- Brandenburg v. Ohio,395 U.S.444 (1969): retained; advocacy must be directed to and likely to produce imminent lawless action to fall within its incitement rule, and that category does not cure the ordinance's separate broad injury coverage.
- New York v. Ferber,458 U.S.747 (1982), and Brockett v. Spokane Arcades,Inc.,472 U.S.491 (1985): applied for authoritative narrowing, facial challenge and available partial invalidation; no judicial rewriting of this offense is ordered.

**Limits and questions not reached.** The Court does not decide the broader content-discrimination question concerning distinctions entirely within unprotected categories, the separate vagueness theory, guilt on count one, a new threat mental-state standard, sentencing enhancements, or the constitutionality of antidiscrimination laws not before it. Reversal removes count two under this ordinance; it does not immunize independently punishable conduct.

**Present operation.** Effective June22,St.Paul's ordinance cannot support the reinstated charge because it remains substantially overbroad as authoritatively construed. The existing narrow fighting-words,incitement and substantial-overbreadth doctrines are applied; emotional injury alone supplies no new unprotected category. The Court adopts no general new system governing distinctions within unprotected speech. Content/context theories in separate writings remain noncontrolling. No precedent is overruled.

#### Medina v. California — 1992-06-22

Source: `records/Medina_v_California_merits_1992-06-22.md`.

### Defendant's preponderance burden at a competency hearing

**Holding and operative rule.** Due process permits California to require a defendant claiming incompetence to prove it by a preponderance of the evidence at an adequate competency hearing with counsel and access to psychiatric evidence. The allocation decides a case in which the evidence is evenly balanced; it does not authorize trial of a defendant established to be incompetent or excuse the hearing when constitutionally sufficient doubt arises.

**Authority.** Kennedy, White, Scalia and Thomas expressly adopt this proposition in the principal opinion; O'Connor and Souter expressly adopt the same California-specific burden rule and adequacy conclusion in O'Connor's concurrence in the judgment. These six express proposition commitments control. No whole opinion has five joins; no categorical exclusion of Mathews from criminal procedure is controlling, and no broader rationale is synthesized under Marks.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that California's full competency hearing provides constitutionally adequate protection despite assigning the defendant the preponderance burden. Pate v. Robinson and Drope v. Missouri require a hearing when sufficient doubt arises and forbid trial while incompetent; they do not themselves settle the hearing's burden. The defendant has counsel, and psychiatric evidence bears directly on present capacity. An inability to assist counsel is itself relevant evidence, while requiring the defense to produce available information can aid an informed determination. The competing concern is serious: the challenged capacity may impair assistance in proving incapacity. The six Justices supporting this rule nevertheless conclude that the existing hearing safeguards make this allocation permissible. A State need not select the procedure most favorable to the accused merely because that alternative is possible. This decision preserves Dusky's functional definition and does not treat competence as an offense element or equate present competence with sanity when the crimes occurred. It decides no higher proof quantum and makes no finding that Medina's jury actually faced equally balanced evidence.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Pate v. Robinson, 383 U.S. 375 (1966): preserved; a possibly incompetent defendant cannot waive the necessary competency inquiry, but the hearing burden is a distinct question.
- Drope v. Missouri, 420 U.S. 162 (1975): applied to require adequate protection of the prohibition against trial while incompetent; that prohibition and the hearing threshold remain intact.
- Dusky v. United States, 362 U.S. 402 (1960): unchanged; the functional standard requires rational consultation with counsel and rational and factual understanding of the proceedings.
- Patterson v. New York, 432 U.S. 197 (1977): its allowance for state procedural choice is consistent with the narrow result; the principal opinion's exclusive-framework extension commands only four votes.
- Ake v. Oklahoma, 470 U.S. 68 (1985): its protection of necessary psychiatric assistance remains in force; the Court adopts no general restriction of its method.
- Leland v. Oregon, 343 U.S. 790 (1952): distinguished; allocating an insanity defense does not itself settle allocation of present trial competence.
- In re Winship, 397 U.S. 358 (1970): unchanged; the prosecution retains proof of every offense element beyond reasonable doubt.

### The associated presumption states the same permitted burden

**Holding and operative rule.** California may retain its statutory presumption of competence at the competency hearing insofar as the presumption means that the party asserting incompetence must prove it by a preponderance. The existence of sufficient doubt to require inquiry does not itself establish incompetence or make that burden-allocating presumption unconstitutional.

**Authority.** Kennedy, White, Scalia and Thomas expressly adopt this interpretation and conclusion in the principal opinion; O'Connor and Souter adopt the same operative allocation and affirmance of the associated presumption in their concurrence. Six support the exact burden-allocating proposition. No broader factual presumption or evidentiary weight is inferred from the judgment.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the presumption adds no independent evidentiary command to the permitted allocation. California's supreme court construed section 1369(f) to place persuasion on the party contesting competence; it did not treat the statutory presumption as psychiatric proof. Pate's threshold protects the right to an inquiry when the court has sufficient doubt. The hearing then resolves a different question under Dusky's functional definition, with counsel and expert evidence available. Requiring that inquiry therefore does not necessarily abolish every presumption governing its outcome. Medina argues that doubt and restrictions on his ordinary decisionmaking make a continuing presumption irrational. Those precautions instead protect the inquiry while capacity remains unresolved; they do not establish its factual answer. Because the six supporting Justices permit the underlying preponderance allocation, they also permit its restatement as this presumption. The Court decides no irrebuttable presumption, separate evidentiary weight, increased burden of proof or authority to proceed despite an actual finding of incompetence. Ordinary constitutional hearing and trial-capacity protections remain operative.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Pate v. Robinson, 383 U.S. 375 (1966): its inquiry requirement is preserved and distinguished from the burden governing the resulting hearing.
- Dusky v. United States, 362 U.S. 402 (1960): the presumption does not amend or replace the functional competence standard.

### Authority limits and questions not reached

The judgment is an affirmance supported by six Justices, but no whole opinion speaks for the Court. The broad proposition that Mathews is categorically the wrong framework for state criminal procedures receives only Kennedy, White, Scalia and Thomas; it is not controlling. O'Connor and Souter retain Mathews as useful guidance. Their method and the principal opinion's historical approach are not logical subsets from which a broader Marks rule can be extracted. The two narrow operative propositions above have six express supporters and need no such synthesis. No determination of actual incompetence, actual equipoise, harmlessness of an invalid burden, retrospective feasibility, insanity burden, higher proof quantum or other trial error is made.

**Present operation.** Effective June 22, 1992, California’s defendant-borne preponderance allocation and its equivalent competence presumption are constitutionally permissible when the required adequate hearing and protections exist. The previously open allocation question is resolved at that scope. Dusky’s definition, the threshold duty to inquire, the prohibition against trial while incompetent, offense-element proof and separate insanity questions retain their existing operation. No exclusive criminal-procedure due-process method receives five votes; no broader Marks rationale is controlling.

#### Estate of Cowart v. Nicklos Drilling Co. — 1992-06-22

Source: `records/Estate_of_Cowart_v_Nicklos_Drilling_Co_merits_1992-06-22.md`.

### Vested entitlement exists before payment or an award

**Holding and operative rule.** A worker whose substantive compensation right has vested is a person entitled to compensation under LHWCA section 33(g), even if the employer disputes liability, makes no current payments and is not subject to an award. When the covered third-party settlement is less than the statutory compensation entitlement, section 33(g)(1) requires prescribed written approval from employer and carrier before execution and filing within thirty days; failure to obtain and file required approval terminates compensation and medical benefits under subsection (g)(2), while judgments and settlements at least equal to compensation entitlement require notification rather than that prior approval.

**Authority.** Kennedy's Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, White, Scalia, Souter and Thomas. Six of nine Justices adopt the entitlement interpretation, the necessary relationship between the subsections and the application. No independently sufficient alternative ground or Marks inquiry is needed.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that entitlement concerns an existing legal right, rather than its later acknowledgment or adjudication. Demarest v. Manspeaker requires enforcement of clear statutory language; Congress's addition of subsection (g)(2) expressly makes prior payment or acknowledgment immaterial. Retention of one phrase does not adopt every earlier administrative limitation when the same amendment supplies contrary context. The uses of entitlement in sections 14(h) and 33(f) reinforce that reading: disputed claims can prompt investigation, and third-party recovery can reduce liability before payments begin. Sullivan v. Stroop and Sorenson v. Secretary of Treasury support consistent meaning in that setting. The estate's reading would separate those linked protections. Banks v. Chicago Grain Trimmers Association explains why approval guards against a settlement leaving excessive liability with the employer. Notification still serves independent purposes for judgments and sufficiently large settlements, so that requirement is not redundant. Cowart's vested right brings him within the condition despite the missing award and current payments. The Court does not decide whether employer participation independently satisfies or excuses approval, the gross-versus-net comparison, or the effect on final awards to other workers.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Demarest v. Manspeaker, 498 U.S. 184 (1991): applied; contrary administrative practice and later retention of words cannot overcome clear enacted meaning.
- Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. Natural Resources Defense Council, Inc., 467 U.S. 837 (1984), and K mart Corp. v. Cartier,Inc., 486 U.S. 281 (1988): preserved; no deference may override unambiguous statutory text. No new rule of deference to a changed litigation position is announced.
- Sullivan v. Stroop, 496 U.S. 478 (1990), and Sorenson v. Secretary of Treasury, 475 U.S. 851 (1986): applied; the recurring statutory phrase is read consistently where surrounding provisions reinforce that meaning.
- Banks v. Chicago Grain Trimmers Association, Inc., 390 U.S. 459 (1968): relied on for approval's protection against an inadequate third-party settlement; the statutory function remains operative before acknowledgment of liability.
- Northeast Marine Terminal Co. v. Caputo, 432 U.S. 249 (1977), and Voris v. Eikel, 346 U.S. 328 (1953): their remedial-construction principles remain available but do not authorize an exception contrary to this provision's text and structure.
- Potomac Electric Power Co. v. Director, OWCP, 449 U.S. 268 (1980): preserved concerning the Board's adjudicative rather than policymaking role; no special deference to the Board supplies a contrary construction.

### Application, limits and questions not reached

The estate cannot recover on its asserted payment-or-award prerequisite, and the Fifth Circuit judgment stands. The holding does not mean that every injured person has a vested compensation right or that all settlements require prior written approval. It decides no independently preserved participation, alternative approval, waiver or gross-versus-net calculation issue; employer involvement is not held incapable of mattering in a properly presented case. The Court neither fixes new liability nor decides retroactive effect, reopening of other final awards or a constitutional challenge. Difficult questions concerning deference to a newly formulated litigation position are unnecessary because the statutory meaning resolves the question.

**Present operation.** Effective June 22, 1992, section 33(g)’s entitlement condition attaches when the substantive compensation right vests, before acknowledgment, payment or award. The statutory conditions for low settlements, prescribed approval and filing, and notification for judgments or sufficiently large settlements remain distinct. The previously disputed payment-or-award limitation is rejected. No equitable exception, new compensation category, new deference method or ruling on employer participation is created.

#### Cipollone v. Liggett Group, Inc. — 1992-06-24

Source: `records/Cipollone_v_Liggett_Group_Inc_merits_1992-06-24.md`.

### The 1965 Act does not displace these common-law damages claims

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 5 of the 1965 Federal Cigarette Labeling and Advertising Act bars positive enactments requiring particular cautionary statements on cigarette packages or in advertising; it does not preempt the common-law damages claims presented here. The required federal package warning does not itself establish immunity from those claims.

**Authority:** Stevens's opinion on the 1965 Act, joined by White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Kennedy and Souter, and by Stone-Zsela as to this statutory construction and its application: seven of nine. Scalia and Thomas oppose restoration of warning claims under this Act but agree that liability for voluntarily made warranty and fraudulent statements is not barred by its statement requirement.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Congress's original prohibition concerned required cautionary statements, not every damages obligation connected with cigarettes. Rice supplies the presumption that traditional state police authority survives unless Congress clearly displaces it. The repeated reference to statements in sections 4 and 5 links the prohibition to prescribed warnings. The regulatory setting likewise concerned competing legislative and administrative warnings. English requires attention to the particular federal operation, and Gade rejects automatic displacement from regulatory overlap alone. A federal warning and a state damages remedy therefore do not inherently conflict. The manufacturers answer that damages can influence conduct. Garmon establishes that possibility, but it does not decide what this narrower cigarette provision covers. Congress could distinguish a compulsory statement from compensation for a private wrong, and this text does so. The Court rejects the asserted uniformity defense to these claims. It decides neither design-defect liability nor the operation of later amendments, and adopts no universal immunity from independently established actual conflict.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Rice v. Santa Fe Elevator Corp., 331 U.S. 218 — applied to retain traditional state remedies absent clear statutory displacement.
- English v. General Electric Co., 496 U.S. 72 — applied; additional tort consequences do not establish displacement without the necessary statutory or conflict basis.
- San Diego Building Trades Council v. Garmon, 359 U.S. 236 — its recognition that damages may regulate is preserved; the labor statute does not determine these cigarette clauses.
- Gade v. National Solid Wastes Management Association (June 18, 1992) — applied only for its shared negative overlap rule; its noncontrolling broader theories are not adopted.

### The successor clause reaches covered warning and warning-neutralization duties

**Holding and operative rule:** For cigarettes whose packages comply with the federal labeling requirements, amended section 5(b) reaches common-law duties and preempts liability requiring additional or clearer smoking-health warnings in advertising or promotion, including a duty forbidding advertising because it neutralizes the mandated warning. Preemption turns on the duty necessary to liability, so a warning claim cannot avoid the clause merely by being pleaded as fraud.

**Authority:** Stevens's amended-Act analysis, joined by Stone-Zsela, White and O'Connor, and Scalia's separate express-preemption analysis, joined by Thomas, expressly support these duties' inclusion and displacement: six of nine at this operative scope. The shared statutory proposition controls directly; it does not depend on treating Scalia's broader preemption position as a join to Stevens's whole opinion.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the amended words include duties enforced through damages. Garmon explains why a legal obligation may regulate conduct even when a court enforces it by compensation. Congress replaced the original reference to statements with requirements or prohibitions imposed under state law and extended the activity description to advertising or promotion. Those words cover the asserted obligation to add health warnings to promotional material. A prohibition on material because it neutralizes the required warning imposes the same kind of obligation from the other direction. Calling that claim intentional fraud does not change the showing on which liability depends. Morales confirms that an express clause must receive its enacted reach, but its airline language does not supply a cigarette-wide immunity. The Court decides only the covered duty and preserves the federal-label compliance condition. State-law liability, causation and damages remain for the lower courts, and genuinely independent duties are addressed separately.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Garmon, 359 U.S. 236 — applied to common-law duties as legal requirements; no labor-specific exclusivity rule is imported.
- Morales v. Trans World Airlines, Inc. (June 1, 1992) — distinguished by statutory language; its recognition of enforceable express boundaries remains consistent with this construction.

### Voluntarily undertaken express warranties survive

**Holding and operative rule:** Amended section 5(b) does not preempt an otherwise valid claim enforcing a manufacturer's voluntarily undertaken express warranty, because the substantive promise is supplied by the manufacturer rather than imposed by a state smoking-health command. The presence of the promise in an advertisement does not itself change that result.

**Authority:** Stevens's analysis, joined by Stone-Zsela, White and O'Connor, supplies the narrow controlling ground under Marks for this component. Blackmun, Kennedy and Souter also vote to restore the warranty claim on their broader ground that the amended Act reaches no common-law damages claims. The voluntary-warranty category is a true subset of that broader nonpreemption rule, with the same result whenever it applies; the two judgment-supporting groups total seven. The broader exemption is not adopted.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that enforcing a promise does not prescribe its substantive content. The warranty claim requires proof that the manufacturer made an assurance that became binding under state law and then failed to honor it. Its terms measure the obligation. State law supplies enforceability, but that does not transform every freely assumed promise into a smoking-health requirement imposed by the State. Norfolk & Western explains that contracts depend on law for their force; its broader exemption from all other law does not erase the narrower words Congress chose here. An assurance printed in advertising is therefore treated like the same assurance made elsewhere. This conclusion neither finds a warranty nor supplies reliance, breach, causation or damages. Those questions remain open under New Jersey law and the existing appellate posture. Marks permits the claim-specific ground shared by the judgment's narrower and broader nonpreemption positions to control without converting the broader position into law.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Norfolk & Western Railway Co. v. American Train Dispatchers Association, 499 U.S. 117 — distinguished; an exemption from all other law does not define this prohibition of state-imposed substantive requirements.
- Marks v. United States, 430 U.S. 188 — applied component by component to the narrow warranty ground; no broader common-law exemption follows.

### Independently actionable nonpromotional conduct remains outside the clause

**Holding and operative rule:** Amended section 5(b) does not preempt claims resting solely on testing, research or other conduct unrelated to advertising or promotion, or concealment claims resting on an independently actionable duty to disclose through nonpromotional channels. This limit does not preserve a disguised demand for additional advertising warnings or establish that any asserted state-law duty actually exists.

**Authority:** Stevens's analysis, joined by Stone-Zsela, White and O'Connor, supplies the narrow component ground under Marks, together with Blackmun, Kennedy and Souter's broader nonpreemption position: seven judgment supporters. The rule concerning claims independently outside the activity boundary is a logical subset of their broader common-law exemption. Scalia and Thomas disagree with restoring the presented consumer-warning theories merely by assigning communication to other channels, while reserving duties genuinely unrelated to advertising.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the clause's activity boundary must remain effective. The relevant inquiry is what the plaintiff must establish as the defendant's legal duty. A claim that rests solely on research or testing practices unrelated to advertising does not impose an advertising requirement. Likewise, an independently existing obligation to disclose facts through a nonpromotional channel does not become promotional because the facts concern smoking. English requires examination of the actual state duty rather than its general association with a regulated industry. Morales does not replace this statute's particular activity words with a boundless effects inquiry. The manufacturers' practical-compulsion objection remains relevant to identifying a disguised advertising duty, but it cannot erase every independent nonpromotional obligation. The Court finds no particular duty to report to an agency and no breach of one. The lower courts must determine the viable allegations and state-law elements, preserving the distinction between the duty asserted and advertising offered as evidence of another wrong.

**Precedent treatment:**

- English, 496 U.S. 72 — applied to examine the particular duty and its actual relationship to the federally regulated activity.
- Morales (June 1, 1992) — distinguished; its airline relation clause and specific fare effects do not abolish this statute's activity limitation.
- Marks, 430 U.S. 188 — applied to the surviving nonpromotional category only; no complete nonpreemption theory is extracted.

### General intentional falsehood and surviving concealment conspiracies are not barred

**Holding and operative rule:** Amended section 5(b) does not preempt liability for intentional material falsehood under the general duty not to deceive, even when the false statement appears in advertising, if liability does not depend on prescribing additional warning content or punishing warning neutralization. Conspiracy to misrepresent or conceal material smoking-health facts survives only to the extent its predicate deceptive duty survives; a conspiracy label creates no exemption of its own.

**Authority:** Stevens's claim-specific analysis, joined by Stone-Zsela, White and O'Connor, is the narrow controlling ground under Marks for these components, with Blackmun, Kennedy and Souter supporting the same restoration on the broader ground that no common-law damages claim is preempted. The protected falsehood and surviving-concealment categories are subsets of that broader rule, giving seven judgment supporters; Scalia and Thomas dissent on the presented amended-Act claims.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the subject of a falsehood does not invariably identify the legal basis of the duty it violates. A claim requiring proof of a knowingly false material representation enforces the general obligation not to deceive. It does not necessarily ask a jury to choose different warning content. The retained federal authority over deceptive advertising supports reading the statute as a warning boundary rather than permission to make false statements. Garmon establishes that damages can regulate, but it does not eliminate the statutory distinction between a smoking-health warning command and ordinary intentional deception. The contrary approach would convert the clause into protection for wrongdoing Congress did not identify. At the same time, relabeling the warning-neutralization theory as fraud cannot revive it. A conspiracy claim follows its predicate: surviving intentional deception or independently actionable concealment remains available, while a prohibited warning duty does not. The Court leaves every state-law element and the merits of the allegations to further proceedings.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Garmon, 359 U.S. 236 — its regulatory insight is preserved without making every damages claim a covered smoking-health requirement.
- Marks, 430 U.S. 188 — applied separately to these surviving claim categories; Blackmun's complete exclusion of common law remains noncontrolling.

**Present operation.** Effective June 24, 1992, the original Act leaves these common-law damages claims available, while the amended Act bars covered promotional warning/neutralization duties and preserves the specified warranty, falsehood and independent nonpromotional categories. Each preserved category remains subject to state-law elements. The amended-Act holdings have the component authority stated above; no complete amended-Act opinion commands five formal joins. The claim-specific Marks rules do not make Blackmun's exemption of all common law controlling, and Scalia's wider displacement does not become law. Gade's negative overlap holding and Morales's distinct airline construction remain in force. No generic prohibition of independently established impossibility preemption, field immunity for cigarettes, constitutional advertising rule, design-defect ruling or construction of later amendments is adopted. Before this decision there was no simulated cigarette-specific construction; the Acts' separate boundaries now govern the presented defense.

#### City of Burlington v. Dague — 1992-06-24

Source: `records/City_of_Burlington_v_Dague_merits_1992-06-24.md`.

### Contingency does not justify an enhanced statutory fee

**Holding and operative rule:** A reasonable attorney's fee under 42 U.S.C. §6972(e) and 33 U.S.C. §1365(d) may not be enhanced above a properly determined lodestar to compensate for the risk of nonpayment when the lawyer's compensation depends on success. The 25% contingency addition is removed; the unreviewed lodestar, fee eligibility and underlying environmental relief remain intact.

**Authority:** Scalia's Opinion of the Court, joined in full by White, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas: five of nine. The fee-statute rule is an ordinary majority holding; the dissenters' alternative market approach supplies no controlling component.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that these statutes compensate reasonable legal services in successful litigation without adding insurance against losing. Hensley supplies the calculation of reasonable hours multiplied by a reasonable rate and keeps fee disputes from becoming a second major litigation. Blum and Delaware Valley I establish the strong presumption that this lodestar is sufficient and forbid counting difficulty and skill twice. White's opinion in Delaware Valley II identified the incompatibility between risk enhancement and the statutes' allocation of fees to prevailing parties; the Court now adopts that position. Dague answers that contingent payment differs from guaranteed payment. The statutory fee need not reproduce every feature of private compensation. Blanchard makes the lodestar independent of the private contingent bargain. A market-access exception would still require uncertain risk comparisons and would not reliably isolate an uncompensated service. The prohibition concerns contingency, not adjustment for delay or correction of an unreasonable underlying rate. Those questions and the environmental judgment remain outside this review.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Hensley v. Eckerhart, 461 U.S. 424 — applied for the lodestar, compensation for successful litigation and avoidance of extensive collateral fee litigation.
- Blum v. Stenson, 465 U.S. 886 — applied for the strong lodestar presumption, applicant's burden and prohibition on double counting; its reserved contingency question is answered here.
- Pennsylvania v. Delaware Valley Citizens' Council for Clean Air, 478 U.S. 546 (Delaware Valley I) — applied for lodestar primacy; no new rule concerning delay or deficient rates follows.
- Pennsylvania v. Delaware Valley Citizens' Council for Clean Air, 483 U.S. 711 (Delaware Valley II) — White's previously noncontrolling categorical position is adopted for these statutes; O'Connor's limited permission and Blackmun's broader permission remain noncontrolling.
- Blanchard v. Bergeron, 489 U.S. 87 — applied; the private contingent agreement is not the governing measure of a statutory fee.

**Present operation.** Effective June 24, 1992, contingency risk cannot support an enhancement under the two reviewed fee provisions. The previously unresolved permission question is resolved categorically; the lodestar method, reasonable-hours/rates inquiry, applicant's ordinary burden and prohibition of double counting remain. The Court applies the same reasonable-fee principles used for similarly worded prevailing-party statutes but adjudicates this award under §6972(e) and §1365(d). It does not rule on a differently worded compensation scheme, delay, an incorrectly calculated lodestar or common-fund fees. Ardestani's administrative-eligibility rule remains distinct, and Department of Energy v. Ohio supplies no federal-immunity premise for this municipal award.

#### Lee v. Weisman — 1992-06-24

Source: `records/Lee_v_Weisman_merits_1992-06-24.md`.

#### Continued exposure supplies a live controversy

**Holding and operative rule:** A student who remains in the same public-school system and faces the challenged official prayer policy at her forthcoming high-school graduation has a live claim for prospective protection even though her earlier graduation is over. Deborah Weisman's continued exposure permits review and preservation of the injunction without deciding her father's taxpayer standing.

**Authority:** Part I of Blackmun's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy and Souter; six Justices expressly agree at this threshold level.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the claim remains live because the challenged policy continues to bear directly on Deborah's own education. She attended the middle-school ceremony after temporary relief was denied and now attends Classical High School within the same system. The stipulated record makes a clergy-led invocation and benediction at her later graduation likely. Her request therefore concerns an identified future application of the policy, not merely disagreement with a completed ceremony. Engel and Schempp supply the school-prayer interest for which prospective protection is sought; deciding that interest requires no conclusion about an unrelated taxpayer's right to sue. The officials' merits argument that attendance is optional does not erase the event's expected occurrence or Deborah's concrete relation to it. The Court preserves review of this ongoing practice and leaves taxpayer standing unresolved. It announces no exception permitting advisory decisions whenever an expired school dispute might recur.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Engel v. Vitale, 370 U.S. 421, and School District of Abington Township v. Schempp, 374 U.S. 203 — supply the substantive school-prayer interest; neither is expanded into a new taxpayer-standing rule.

#### Officially organized graduation worship is forbidden

**Holding and operative rule:** Public secondary-school officials violate the Establishment Clause when they select clergy, request invocations and benedictions, guide the prayers' religious content, and incorporate that worship into the official graduation program. Neither nonsectarian wording nor formally optional attendance cures that official sponsorship; no separate showing of punishment or quantified social pressure is required to invalidate this practice.

**Authority:** Part II of Blackmun's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor and Souter; five Justices adopt this independently sufficient ground. Kennedy does not join Part II.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the school may not organize worship as part of its own graduation ceremony. Engel forbids governmental composition and sponsorship of school prayer even when the words are denominationally neutral and observance is voluntary. Schempp likewise rejects excusal from school devotional exercises as a cure. Lemon's prohibition on a primary effect advancing religion applies directly: the principal selected a rabbi, requested prayer, supplied civic-prayer guidance and placed the exercise in the official program. Asking that the prayer be broadly acceptable still makes religious presentation an undertaking of the State. The officials' interest in solemnity does not authorize that undertaking; a secular ceremony can mark the achievement without selecting an approved devotional expression. Marsh concerns legislative prayer in a distinct institutional and historical setting. Mergens protects private religious activity under equal access, not school-directed worship. The Court preserves those distinctions, leaves general accommodation doctrine intact, and decides no question about funding, denominational aid or genuinely private religious speech.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Engel v. Vitale, 370 U.S. 421 — applied to official school-organized worship at graduation; denominational neutrality and voluntary observance do not cure sponsorship.
- School District of Abington Township v. Schempp, 374 U.S. 203 — applied: permission to abstain does not validate the official devotional exercise.
- Lemon v. Kurtzman, 403 U.S. 602 — applied through its prohibition on a primary effect advancing religion; no replacement test and no separate decision under its purpose or entanglement prongs.
- Marsh v. Chambers, 463 U.S. 783 — distinguished: legislative prayer does not authorize this school-directed graduation exercise.
- Board of Education of Westside Community Schools v. Mergens, 496 U.S. 226 — distinguished: private religious activity under equal access is materially different from official sponsorship.

#### The school-directed ceremony also imposes impermissible religious pressure

**Holding and operative rule:** The Establishment Clause independently forbids school-directed clergy prayer at a public secondary-school graduation when the school's control and the ceremony's importance place objecting students under substantial pressure to participate, appear to participate, or forgo the occasion. On this record, formally optional attendance and an opportunity to stand or remain silent do not remove that unconstitutional pressure; mere offense alone is insufficient.

**Authority:** Kennedy's concurrence in part and in the judgment, joined by Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor and Souter; five Justices expressly adopt this independently sufficient rationale. It controls by direct majority support, without a Marks inference. Stone-Zsela does not join this writing.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court also holds that the school may not use the importance and discipline of graduation to press students into a religious exercise. Engel and Schempp recognize the distinctive constitutional dangers of prayer organized for schoolchildren. Here officials selected the religious participant and controlled the ceremony in which classmates stood together. An objecting adolescent could reasonably understand standing or silence during the collective prayer as her participation or approval. Requiring her instead to protest publicly or miss a singular educational occasion does not preserve a meaningful choice. The absence of a disciplinary penalty therefore does not answer the constitutional injury. Marsh rests on an adult legislative setting where attendees can enter and leave without the same educational and social consequences. The Court does not equate every disagreeable message or ordinary demand for courtesy with religious coercion. It leaves the treatment of mature adult audiences and other religious accommodations open; the holding concerns this State-directed exercise and the pressures attending public secondary-school graduation.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Engel v. Vitale, 370 U.S. 421, and School District of Abington Township v. Schempp, 374 U.S. 203 — applied to indirect religious pressure in the secondary-school graduation setting; formal excusal is not dispositive.
- Marsh v. Chambers, 463 U.S. 783 — distinguished by the adult legislative setting and freedom to leave without the same consequences.
- Lynch v. Donnelly, 465 U.S. 668 — its minimum prohibition on coerced religious support or participation is applied, without extending its display holding.
- County of Allegheny v. ACLU, 492 U.S. 573 — the present decision does not reopen the display judgments or settle every dispute about accommodation.

**Present operation.** Effective June 24, 1992, official organization of the identified secondary-school graduation worship violates the Establishment Clause independently of a separate showing of coercion, and the school's indirect pressure supplies a second independently sufficient ground. Before this decision Engel, Schempp and Lemon governed school worship, but their precise application to this graduation practice was unresolved in the simulated term. Afterward both stated applications control, each with five Justices. No general replacement establishment test, rule against mere offense, quantitative pressure scale, funding rule or universal accommodation rule is adopted. Lemon remains in force; its purpose and entanglement prongs are not independently adjudicated. Adult legislative prayer and genuinely private religious speech remain governed by their own authorities. No Marks extraction is necessary because each controlling ground has express majority support.

#### Doggett v. United States — 1992-06-24

Source: `records/Doggett_v_United_States_merits_1992-06-24.md`.

#### An outstanding indictment protects fair adjudication before arrest
