**Holding and operative rule:** Once an indictment or other official accusation triggers the Sixth Amendment, the speedy-trial inquiry must consider impairment of the defense even if the accused remains free and unaware of the charge. Absence of incarceration or anxiety eliminates those possible injuries on this record, but it does not exclude defense impairment from the Barker balance.

**Authority:** Part II of Souter's opinion for the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens and Kennedy; six Justices adopt the scope rule.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that an outstanding indictment brings the fairness of the eventual trial within the speedy-trial guarantee. Barker identifies defense impairment as a protected interest distinct from detention and anxiety, and Moore rejects a rigid insistence upon affirmative proof of trial prejudice. The Government's narrower account would remove that protection whenever an accused remained unaware and unrestricted, although the pending prosecution could still erode his ability to defend himself. Marion addresses delay before formal accusation; MacDonald and Loud Hawk address periods outside an operative prosecution and the consequences of justified proceedings. Those decisions do not erase Barker's protection after accusation. Doggett's indictment remained pending throughout the delay, so his defense interest must enter the balance even though two other kinds of injury are absent. The Court does not constitutionalize a limitations period running from the offense, decide an independent right to repose, or withdraw due process protection against unfair delay before or after accusation.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Barker v. Wingo, 407 U.S. 514 — applied: fair adjudication is an independent protected interest in the existing four-factor inquiry.
- Moore v. Arizona, 414 U.S. 25 — applied: affirmative proof of trial prejudice is not indispensable in every claim.
- United States v. Marion, 404 U.S. 307 — distinguished as to preaccusation delay; its accusation boundary remains effective.
- United States v. MacDonald, 456 U.S. 1 — distinguished as to periods after dismissal; its separate due process protection is preserved.
- United States v. Loud Hawk, 474 U.S. 302 — distinguished as to absence of an operative prosecution and justified interlocutory delay; no categorical restriction to custody or anxiety is adopted.

#### Extraordinary negligent delay and unrebutted impairment require dismissal here

**Holding and operative rule:** Under Barker, extraordinary post-indictment delay caused by sustained governmental negligence can violate the Sixth Amendment without identified trial prejudice when the accused did not knowingly acquiesce and the resulting inference of defense impairment is not persuasively rebutted. The eight-and-one-half-year delay here, including approximately six avoidable years while Doggett lived openly, establishes that violation; Strunk requires dismissal of the indictment and vacatur of the conditional conviction, not a sentence adjustment.

**Authority:** Part III of Souter's opinion for the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens and Kennedy; six Justices adopt the rule, application and dismissal consequence.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the Government's prolonged neglect cannot be excused merely because Doggett cannot identify everything time has destroyed. Barker first requires delay sufficient to prompt examination and then weighs its duration, reasons, assertion of the right and prejudice together. Its warning that forgotten evidence can rarely be identified explains why presumptive impairment matters beyond the initial threshold. Negligence weighs less heavily than deliberate obstruction, but its weight grows when it persists for years without adequate justification. The accepted findings establish that ordinary investigation could have located Doggett while he lived openly, and that he did not know of the indictment before arrest. His prompt assertion therefore supplies no knowing acquiescence. Moore forecloses treating particularized prejudice as an extra mandatory element. The Government's answers to specific allegations of harm do not persuasively rebut the accumulated inference here. Diligent pursuit, justified obstacles, evasion or materially shorter neglect may yield another result. Strunk supplies dismissal once this constitutional violation is established; no fixed deadline or general transfer of the ultimate constitutional burden is announced.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Barker v. Wingo, 407 U.S. 514 — applied and specified for extraordinary negligent delay; the threshold inquiry remains distinct from the ultimate balance and knowing acquiescence remains relevant.
- Moore v. Arizona, 414 U.S. 25 — applied against a categorical actual-prejudice prerequisite.
- United States v. Loud Hawk, 474 U.S. 302 — preserved for the significance of justified delay and the insufficiency of possible prejudice considered alone.
- Strunk v. United States, 412 U.S. 434 — applied: dismissal and vacatur, rather than sentence credit or a new trial, implement an established speedy-trial violation.

#### The express conditional plea preserves effective review

**Holding and operative rule:** A guilty plea entered under Federal Rule of Criminal Procedure 11(a)(2), with the Government's express consent to reservation of the adverse speedy-trial ruling, preserves that claim and its defense-impairment argument for appellate review. Doggett's plea does not waive the claimed consequence of unconstitutional delay merely because he ultimately admitted guilt.

**Authority:** Part I of Souter's opinion for the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens and Kennedy; six Justices adopt this threshold application.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the Government must honor the appellate reservation it accepted. Rule 11(a)(2) permits a conditional plea preserving review of an adverse pretrial ruling. Doggett's agreement expressly reserved the denial of his motion to dismiss for post-indictment constitutional delay, and the Government consented to that reservation. The impairment claim was part of that motion. Broce addresses the consequences of an ordinary guilty plea; it does not transform an expressly conditional plea into an unconditional surrender of the very issue reserved. Accepting the Government's position would permit an appeal in name while disabling the principal argument supporting it. The Court therefore reviews the preserved speedy-trial issue as effectively as it could have been reviewed after trial. This application does not excuse an unreserved objection or reopen the factual admissions establishing guilt. Relief follows because the preserved constitutional claim succeeds and Strunk requires the conviction to be set aside with dismissal of the prosecution.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Federal Rule of Criminal Procedure 11(a)(2) — applied according to the express reservation and prosecutorial consent; no new exception to plea waiver.
- United States v. Broce, 488 U.S. 563 — distinguished: an unconditional plea's consequences do not defeat this expressly preserved issue.
- Strunk v. United States, 412 U.S. 434 — applied to the consequence of success on the preserved constitutional claim.

**Present operation.** Effective June 24, 1992, Barker's existing four-factor balance expressly reaches extraordinary negligent post-indictment delay with unrebutted presumptive defense impairment despite an accused's freedom and ignorance. The threshold delay needed to trigger inquiry is distinct from the growing weight of presumed impairment in the ultimate balance. Negligence remains less culpable than deliberate obstruction but weighs increasingly against the Government as unjustified delay persists. No fixed time limit, automatic presumption of constitutional violation, universal prosecutorial burden transfer, rule governing preaccusation delay or independent right of repose is created. Strunk's dismissal consequence and Rule 11(a)(2)'s express reservation operate without change. Six Justices support the Court's entire opinion; no fractured-decision or Marks issue arises.

#### International Society for Krishna Consciousness, Inc. v. Lee — 1992-06-26

Source: `records/International_Society_for_Krishna_Consciousness_Inc_v_Lee_merits_1992-06-26.md`.

### Judgment without a controlling validity rationale

**Judgment and authority.** Six Justices—Stone, White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas—affirm the denial of relief against continuous or repetitive solicitation and immediate personal receipt of funds in the Authority's terminal interiors. The judgment binds these parties within that construction. White's nonpublic-forum reasonableness opinion has four joins including its author. Stone and Kennedy separately apply the more protective public-forum time, place and manner standard to the immediate-payment rule; they do not join the nonpublic classification. No five Justices adopt the same validity rationale or forum classification.

**Fractured-decision boundary.** Marks v. United States, 430 U.S. 188, permits identification of an actual narrower judgment-supporting ground; it does not combine votes for different grounds into an opinion. Here the nonpublic rationale depends on a different legal classification and demands reasonableness, whereas the other two writings apply Ward's significant-interest, tailoring and alternatives requirements. Their justification predicates and rules are not demonstrated logical subsets simply because one standard is more demanding. Stone also expressly reserves classification, while Kennedy classifies the common areas as public. The limited affirmance does not establish either classification or a general public-forum permission to prohibit immediate collections. No controlling alternative merits holding is extracted.

**Limits and issues not reached by a controlling Court rationale.** Neither the general status of airports nor the constitutionality of broader donation appeals, literature distribution, literature sales, airline-leased areas, or secure operational areas is decided by a controlling rationale in this docket. The companion decides its own distribution relief. Religious solicitation remains protected expression under existing law; no new category of unprotected speech is created.

**Present operation.** The limited solicitation-and-immediate-receipt restriction survives for these parties from June 26, 1992. No reusable airport-classification rule or unified constitutional-validity rationale is added. Existing forum doctrine, viewpoint neutrality and protected-solicitation law remain. The four-Justice nonpublic analysis and the two Ward applications are separate noncontrolling grounds. Same-day distribution relief is separately established in Lee v. ISKCON.

#### United States v. Fordice; Ayers v. Fordice — 1992-06-26

Source: `records/United_States_v_Fordice_and_Ayers_v_Fordice_merits_1992-06-26.md`.

### 1. The continuing duty in a formerly dual university system

**Holding and operative rule.** A State that maintained a legally segregated university system does not discharge its affirmative dismantlement duty merely by adopting good-faith, facially neutral policies. It must eliminate policies traceable to that system that continue to foster segregation when their effects can practicably be removed consistently with sound educational practices; the established duty does not require fresh discriminatory intent for each such remnant, and current racial imbalance alone is insufficient.

**Authority.** White's opinion, joined by Stone, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas, eight of nine Justices, controls the Equal Protection rule and the related Title VI disposition in these cases.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that opening a university's doors does not necessarily remove the State's old barriers to educational choice. Brown establishes the duty to end legally imposed educational separation. Green requires effective dismantlement rather than reliance on the name given a freedom-of-choice policy. Dowell and the Court's decision in Freeman preserve practical limits and require identification of effects attributable to the established violation. University attendance is voluntary, and differing institutional missions may serve legitimate educational purposes. But the alternatives among which a student chooses remain shaped by admissions requirements, available programs and State-assigned institutional roles. Bazemore does not make those arrangements private choices: its voluntary clubs presented findings that discriminatory practices had ended and choice was unfettered. Mississippi's good faith therefore does not answer whether its inherited machinery still fosters segregation. The Title VI claim follows the same established discrimination duty here. The Court leaves new policies without roots in the dual system to ordinary equal-protection analysis and does not require proportional enrollment or decide unrelated statutory theories.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Brown v. Board of Education, 347 U.S. 483, and Brown II, 349 U.S. 294: applied; university systems share the affirmative duty to dismantle established legal segregation.
- Green v. School Board of New Kent County, 391 U.S. 430: applied to effective dismantlement, with voluntary university attendance and legitimate institutional differences preserved.
- Board of Education v. Dowell, 498 U.S. 237: applied; good faith and practicable removal of vestiges remain distinct requirements.
- Freeman v. Pitts, March 31, 1992 simulated decision: applied within its actual causal and practicability limits; its school-decree holding is not replaced or mechanically transplanted.
- Bazemore v. Friday, 478 U.S. 385: distinguished; genuinely unfettered voluntary club choice does not establish removal of inherited university constraints.
- Arlington Heights v. Metropolitan Housing Development Corp., 429 U.S. 252: preserved for a new violation involving policies not rooted in the established dual system.

### 2. The State's burden and assessment of interacting policies

**Holding and operative rule.** The State bears the burden of demonstrating that it has dismantled its established dual system; a court may not shift that burden to plaintiffs by demanding that they disprove discharge. The court must examine the current effects and practical alternatives of challenged inherited policies, including their interaction, and must tie any remedy to the violation rather than infer liability or relief from racial identifiability alone.

**Authority.** The same eight-Justice opinion of the Court adopts the burden, combined-effects inquiry and violation-linked remedial boundary.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that a finding of current good intentions cannot substitute for proof of completed dismantlement. Brown II places responsibility for overcoming the established unlawful system on the State. Green requires examination of whether the chosen measures actually remove its operation, and Swann ties remedial authority to the constitutional wrong. Here the District Court identified substantial unnecessary duplication yet relied on an asserted absence of plaintiffs' proof that changing it would affect enrollment. That approach reverses the burden and isolates a policy that may reinforce admissions and mission differences. The Court's Freeman decision likewise requires attention to causal and remedial connections rather than treating a finding on one question as an answer to all others. Undisturbed historical findings remain evidence; applying the wrong compliance standard does not itself require retrying every fact. On remand the courts must examine the affected policies together where their effects interact, assess educational justification and practical change, and state the findings supporting relief. Neither general racial balance nor unlimited judicial management follows.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Brown II, 349 U.S. 294: applied; the State retains the burden of showing discharge of its established duty.
- Green, 391 U.S. 430: applied; effectiveness is assessed in actual operation, not by formal policy labels.
- Swann v. Charlotte-Mecklenburg Board of Education, 402 U.S. 1: applied; the scope of the violation bounds equitable relief.
- Freeman v. Pitts, March 31, 1992 simulated decision: applied; causal burdens, practicable compliance and remedial interdependence are retained.
- Milliken v. Bradley, 418 U.S. 717, and 433 U.S. 267: preserved; remediation must address the proven violation and its consequences.

### 3. Differential and ACT-only admissions requirements require further justification

**Holding and operative rule.** Where present admissions requirements derive from deliberately exclusionary criteria and continue segregative effects, the State cannot establish dismantlement merely by attributing their later retention to neutral educational purposes. On this record it must adequately justify the differential thresholds and ACT-only method under the continuing-duty standard or replace the unjustified, practicably removable remnants; the Court does not invalidate every use of the ACT or prescribe a substitute score.

**Authority.** White's eight-Justice Court opinion controls this application. Scalia supplies a ninth vote for further admissions review only on his distinct intentional-admission-barrier ground; he does not join the broader rule.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that changing a policy's stated purpose does not by itself remove its discriminatory inheritance. Brown and Green require the State to dismantle the exclusionary system actually established. The ACT requirements began as a means of excluding black applicants from historically white institutions, and the lower findings show continuing differences in automatic admission opportunities. The asserted concern about unprepared students does not adequately explain all differences between institutions with comparable undergraduate missions or establish why different missions require these precise thresholds. The refusal to consider school performance alongside test scores also requires more than the general invocation of uneven grading. The record presents an available predictor of success and materially different effects that the courts below did not adequately assess under the State's burden. This conclusion does not replace university admissions expertise with a judicial formula. It requires an explanation adequate to the established remedial duty and examination of practicable alternatives. The State may maintain sound admissions standards that satisfy that duty; no universal ACT prohibition is announced.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Brown and Green: applied; inherited discriminatory admission barriers require effective dismantlement rather than a new neutral label.
- Dowell and the simulated Freeman: applied; practical removal and causal attribution govern the continuing compliance inquiry.
- Florida ex rel. Hawkins v. Board of Control, 350 U.S. 413: applied to equal access to higher education; no new test-score formula follows.

### 4. Remand for the full structure without a predetermined institutional remedy

**Holding and operative rule.** The corrected inquiry must examine unnecessary program duplication, inherited mission assignments, continued institutional organization, and other properly challenged features including affected funding, with their combined segregative effects and educational justifications. Neither racial identifiability nor fiscal inefficiency alone requires closing or merging a university, and improved funding alone cannot establish dismantlement while unlawful barriers remain.

**Authority.** White's opinion, joined by the same seven Justices, controls the full remand in both dockets; Scalia dissents from this breadth.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the State must examine the structure through which its universities offer educational opportunity. Green supplies the demand for effective removal of the former system, and Swann and Milliken limit remedies to its established consequences. Unnecessary duplication can preserve separate pathways, and mission assignments that carry forward the former institutions' unequal roles may reinforce admissions barriers. Maintaining all eight institutions must also be examined where that organization perpetuates the dual system; expense alone does not resolve the constitutional issue. The present record does not establish that closing any school is required, and changes to admissions or programs may make closure unnecessary. Historically black institutions' educational value and students' interests remain relevant to sound remedial design. The Court rejects an entitlement to additional funding merely to maintain publicly financed separate enclaves, but leaves funding needed for actual dismantlement for remand. The Alabama State Teachers Association summary affirmance concerned an educationally justified new campus; it did not approve every inherited policy. Concrete remedies must follow the corrected findings, not precede them.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Green and Swann: applied to effective, violation-linked structural relief rather than a numerical enrollment command.
- Milliken, 418 U.S. 717, and 433 U.S. 267: applied to connect practical educational relief to proven consequences.
- Alabama State Teachers Association v. Alabama Public School and College Authority, 393 U.S. 400: confined to the issues necessarily affirmed concerning the justified new campus; no blanket university-compliance safe harbor is recognized.
- Rufo v. Inmates of Suffolk County Jail, January 15, 1992 simulated decision: left intact in its consent-decree-modification setting; it supplies no different liability or automatic termination standard here.

**Present operation.** Effective June 26, 1992, Mississippi must establish actual dismantlement under the traceability, current segregative effect, educational justification and practicable-cure inquiry. The rule clarifies the established affirmative duty in higher education: neutral wording and good faith are insufficient by themselves. The State's existing burden continues; the law does not turn every racially identifiable university or every difference in funding, programs or admissions into a violation. The full range of properly challenged inherited policies must be reassessed with relevant interactions, and remedies remain subject to causation and educational practicality. Equal Protection and the related Title VI disposition share that established duty here. No specific closure, merger, funding award or racial assignment rule enters law.

#### Two Pesos, Inc. v. Taco Cabana, Inc. — 1992-06-26

Source: `records/Two_Pesos_v_Taco_Cabana_merits_1992-06-26.md`.

### Inherent distinctiveness suffices without acquired secondary meaning

**Holding and operative rule.** Under the pre-1988 Lanham Act §43(a), nonfunctional restaurant trade dress that is inherently distinctive may receive protection without proof of acquired secondary meaning, provided the remaining infringement requirements, including likelihood of confusion, are met. Protection does not expire after an unspecified period merely because secondary meaning has not developed.

**Authority.** White's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter, supplies seven votes at this level of generality. Stevens and Thomas agree with the judgment on separate statutory accounts; the Court's rule does not depend on combining their writings.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that a source identifier need not acquire distinctiveness twice. Inwood distinguishes acquired public association from functionality and recognizes the broader protection available under §43(a). Park 'N Fly explains the statutory distinction between descriptive marks requiring acquired meaning and inherently distinctive marks. The same source-identifying distinction applies to the nonfunctional restaurant presentation accepted here. The jury found inherent distinctiveness and likely confusion, yet found no acquired secondary meaning; those findings concern different propositions and are not contradictory. Chevron Chemical properly applies the familiar Abercrombie categories to trade dress, whereas a universal secondary-meaning requirement has no basis in the operative statute. Requiring an initial period of unprotected copying would obstruct source identification and goodwill. The concern that protection might exhaust useful designs is answered by the independent requirements of distinctiveness, nonfunctionality and confusion. A temporary exemption followed by an unwritten deadline is equally unsupported. The Court accepts the findings for this limited review and leaves the excluded functionality challenge and other forms of product design undecided.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Inwood Laboratories, Inc. v. Ives Laboratories, Inc., 456 U.S. 844 (1982): applied for secondary meaning, functionality and §43(a)'s protection beyond registered marks; its distinct contributory-infringement holding is unchanged.
- Park 'N Fly, Inc. v. Dollar Park & Fly, Inc., 469 U.S. 189 (1985): applied for the statutory distinction between inherent and acquired distinctiveness and protection of source-identifying goodwill; no registration requirement is imported into §43(a).
- Kellogg Co. v. National Biscuit Co., 305 U.S. 111 (1938): its acquired-source-significance concept is preserved; it does not impose secondary meaning on accepted inherently distinctive restaurant dress.
- Abercrombie & Fitch Co. v. Hunting World, Inc., 537 F.2d 4 (2d Cir. 1976): its classification is used to distinguish inherent from acquired distinctiveness; this circuit authority is not represented as an earlier Supreme Court trade-dress holding.
- Chevron Chemical Co. v. Voluntary Purchasing Groups, Inc., 659 F.2d 695 (5th Cir. 1981): its treatment of inherently distinctive trade dress is approved on the question presented.
- Vibrant Sales, Inc. v. New Body Boutique, Inc., 652 F.2d 299 (2d Cir. 1981), and the contrary categorical reading of the Second Circuit trade-dress cases: rejected insofar as they require acquired secondary meaning despite accepted inherent distinctiveness; their distinct functionality applications are not decided.

**Limits and questions not reached.** The accepted jury findings are not retried. No ownership of Mexican cuisine, a general decorative style, useful designs or generic matter follows. The Court decides no later categorical product-design rule, no excluded functionality issue, no new burden of proof and no damages amount. No Supreme Court precedent is overruled.

**Present operation.** Effective June 26, 1992, inherent distinctiveness supplies an alternative to acquired secondary meaning for this nonfunctional restaurant trade dress under the applicable §43(a). Previously the circuits disagreed; the Court now rejects a categorical additional acquired-meaning requirement and an unwritten expiration period. The ordinary civil infringement burden, independent confusion requirement, functionality limitation and remedies not under review are unchanged.

#### Franklin v. Massachusetts — 1992-06-26

Source: `records/Franklin_v_Massachusetts_merits_1992-06-26.md`.

### Standing for the continuing apportionment injury

**Holding and operative rule.** A State and its voters have standing to contest inclusion of an identified population in the operative apportionment when that inclusion costs the State a seat and authoritative relief concerning the responsible census determination would likely correct the allocation through the prescribed statutory sequence. Likely redress here does not require an injunction against the President or a general assumption that persons outside the litigation follow judicial advice.

**Authority.** Parts I–III of Stone-Zsela's Opinion of the Court, joined by all eight Associate Justices, control. This holding concerns the proved inclusion injury; it does not find that any particular alternative data source would independently give Massachusetts a seat.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that this continuing representational injury can be judicially redressed. Massachusetts would receive an additional seat if the challenged overseas population were excluded. The injury therefore rests on the operative allocation, not an abstract objection to governmental procedure. This Court's Montana decision establishes that authoritative declaratory adjudication can probably correct an ongoing apportionment through the prescribed governmental sequence. Lujan requires a concrete injury, causal connection and likely relief at the pertinent evidentiary stage; its absent-agency application did not command a majority and does not supply a categorical bar here. The statutory sequence and the Government's litigation position support the likelihood of correction following an authoritative determination concerning the census. The Court need not presume a power to command presidential discretion. Nor does it assume that a different residence dataset would necessarily produce another seat: that separate causal proposition has not been established. The inclusion injury and its connected legal challenges suffice for the review undertaken here.

**Precedent treatment.**

- United States Department of Commerce v. Montana, March 31, 1992, this Court: applied for continuing representational injury and likely declaratory redress through the allocation process; the express unanimous holding remains controlling and its reservation of coercive presidential relief remains intact.
- Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife, June 12, 1992, this Court: applied for injury, causation, likely redress and evidentiary demands; the noncontrolling application concerning absent funding agencies is not promoted into law. Its rule for procedure connected to concrete injury does not require certainty that a new method changes the result.

### The Secretary's completed census determination is reviewable

**Holding and operative rule.** The Secretary's completed census determination supplies final agency action under APA §704 even though the President later transmits the resulting apportionment statement. The census statutes neither preclude review nor commit the counting decision entirely to unreviewable discretion: the duty to count the proper population supplies law to apply, while leaving substantial methodological choice to the Secretary.

**Authority.** Part IV of Stone-Zsela's Opinion of the Court, joined by Blackmun, Stevens, Kennedy and Souter, controls with five votes. White, O'Connor, Scalia and Thomas disagree with this rule while agreeing with reversal on their own reviewability ground.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the official responsible for the count cannot escape review merely because another official transmits its apportionment consequence. Section 141 assigns the census and its completed population figures to the Secretary. Section 2a directs transmission of numbers ascertained under that census and application of the prescribed formula; it does not confer a new presidential choice of the population to count. Abbott Laboratories supports review of a completed administrative determination producing concrete consequences. Waterman involved a statutory presidential judgment over foreign air routes; that independent decision differs from the prescribed transmission here. Possible correction and general executive supervision do not make every completed agency decision tentative. Japan Whaling confirms that broad administrative discretion remains judicially reviewable when governing statutory commands supply a legal standard. The Secretary retains latitude over census form and method, but must count the proper population under the governing law. Review of that completed administrative choice is sufficient. The Court therefore leaves the President's own APA status and susceptibility to coercive relief undecided.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Abbott Laboratories v. Gardner, 387 U.S. 136 (1967): applied for reviewability and practical completion of an agency determination with concrete consequences; the statutory transmittal here does not postpone finality.
- Chicago & Southern Air Lines, Inc. v. Waterman Steamship Corp., 333 U.S. 103 (1948): distinguished because Congress there required an independent presidential foreign-affairs judgment; its rule for that decision structure is preserved.
- Japan Whaling Assn. v. American Cetacean Society, 478 U.S. 221 (1986): applied for review against statutory limits despite broad administrative judgment; methodological latitude is not absence of law to apply.

### Temporarily absent residents may be included in the constitutional count

**Holding and operative rule.** The constitutional command to count persons in each State permits inclusion of federal personnel temporarily overseas whose enduring ties support residence in a particular State. It does not require a literal census-day physical presence rule, and it does not authorize attribution to a State without a supportable residence connection.

**Authority.** Part III of Stone-Zsela's Opinion of the Court, joined by all eight Associate Justices, controls with nine votes. The constitutional rule does not itself resolve the separate statutory finality or APA explanation questions.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that temporary government service abroad does not necessarily sever residence in a State. The constitutional text requires an enumeration of the population belonging to each State, but does not define residence exclusively by bodily presence on census day. The first census legislation treated persons temporarily absent from their usual abode as belonging there. That early implementation gives concrete content to the counting command. The challenged personnel retain identified state connections, and the Secretary may reasonably treat their absence on federal assignment as temporary for this purpose. Wesberry and Karcher enforce equality in drawing districts within a State; they do not dictate this answer to who belongs in an interstate population count. Montana likewise recognizes that interstate apportionment must respect the constitutional structure rather than import every intrastate rule unchanged. Home-of-record information is imperfect, but that does not make every supported attribution constitutionally forbidden. The Court decides this record and rejects mandatory exclusion; unsupported connections and different methodological records remain open.

**Precedent treatment.**

- United States Department of Commerce v. Montana, March 31, 1992, this Court: applied for the constitutional structure of interstate apportionment and the distinction from intrastate districting; no new tolerance for unequal districts is adopted.
- Wesberry v. Sanders, 376 U.S. 1 (1964): distinguished as an intrastate congressional district-equality rule; its equal-population command remains effective in its own setting.
- Karcher v. Daggett, 462 U.S. 725 (1983): distinguished on the same interstate/intrastate ground; the present residence decision does not excuse avoidable inequality among a State's districts.

### The statutory and administrative objections fail on this record

**Holding and operative rule.** Section 141 permits the supported inclusion of temporarily overseas personnel with enduring state ties, and the Secretary's comparative choice of supplemented home-of-record information is not arbitrary or capricious on this record. The agency must supply a contemporaneous, reasonably discernible explanation connecting its available information and residence objective to its choice; imperfection alone does not require rejection of every available dataset.

**Authority.** Part V of Stone-Zsela's Opinion of the Court, joined by Blackmun, Stevens, Kennedy and Souter, controls with five votes. The other four Justices do not join the statutory or APA merits determination.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the Secretary made a permissible comparative choice and adequately explained it. State Farm requires examination of relevant considerations and a rational connection between the record and the decision. Bowman Transportation permits an explanation whose path is reasonably discernible even if its expression is imperfect. Cancellation of the proposed survey is a serious objection because improved residence information helped motivate the change. But the contemporaneous record also contains assurances of supplementary personnel information and continuing reasons to recognize temporarily absent residents. Home of record can identify an enduring connection; legal residence may instead reflect taxation, and last duty station may identify employment rather than home. The Secretary could weigh those comparative defects under the statutory counting responsibility. The Court relies on the recorded reasons, not later declarations or a rationale invented by counsel. It does not demand a perfect dataset unavailable to the agency. Nor does it excuse persistence when an agency's only factual premise collapses. Different methodological challenges require their own records.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Motor Vehicle Manufacturers Assn. v. State Farm Mutual Automobile Insurance Co., 463 U.S. 29 (1983): applied for consideration of relevant factors, reasoned explanation and review on the agency's actual rationale; no exemption from review accompanies census discretion.
- Bowman Transportation, Inc. v. Arkansas-Best Freight System, Inc., 419 U.S. 281 (1974): applied for a reasonably discernible decisional path despite imperfect exposition; a court may not substitute a new rationale.

**Limits and questions not reached.** No replacement allocation, judicially selected dataset, injunction against the President, or general APA status for the President is decided. No claim that another residence method necessarily restores a seat is found proved. The statutory and administrative holdings are record-specific applications; no earlier Supreme Court precedent is overruled.

**Present operation.** Effective June 26, 1992, the completed Secretary census decision is reviewable under §704 despite subsequent presidential transmission; counting obligations supply law to apply. The constitutional count may include supported temporarily absent residents. The statutory and APA merits uphold this inclusion and home-of-record choice on its contemporaneous explanation. Earlier Montana declaratory-redress law is applied. No presidential immunity or presidential APA holding, new evidentiary burden, replacement formula or districting tolerance is created.

#### Lee v. International Society for Krishna Consciousness, Inc. — 1992-06-26

Source: `records/Lee_v_International_Society_for_Krishna_Consciousness_Inc_merits_1992-06-26.md`.

### Judgment without a controlling invalidity rationale

**Judgment and authority.** Stone, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy and Souter affirm the invalidation of the Authority's complete literature-distribution prohibition. Stone applies nonpublic-forum reasonableness on an assumption; O'Connor reaches unreasonableness after classifying the terminals as nonpublic. Kennedy, Blackmun, Stevens and Souter apply public-forum protections. The declaration and injunction bind these parties, but neither forum classification nor one constitutional invalidity rationale receives five joins.

**Fractured-decision boundary.** Under Marks v. United States, 430 U.S. 188, a common result is not enough to create a controlling opinion. The two contextual-reasonableness writings cannot be mechanically combined with the four public-forum votes: a restriction can fail the more protective public-forum test while remaining reasonable in nonpublic property. Conversely, the forum predicates of the published writings are different. No adopted logical-subset rule with majority support is established here. The per curiam expressly preserves the judgment without a unified rationale rather than treating stronger scrutiny as an implicit join in a different test. There is no controlling alternative holding.

**Limits.** The judgment preserves relief against the complete distribution prohibition in the common terminal areas actually at issue. It supplies no categorical license to sell merchandise, solicit immediate personal payment, obstruct circulation, or enter private or secure operational space. Particular viewpoint-neutral conduct and location regulations await their own assessment. No majority doctrine governing sales of literature is established.

**Present operation.** From June 26, 1992 the Port Authority remains subject to the declaration and injunction against the challenged complete distribution prohibition. No controlling airport classification or reusable constitutional invalidity rationale emerges from the fractured decision. Existing forum, reasonableness, viewpoint-neutrality and tailoring law remain operative. The companion's distinct limited solicitation judgment does not itself validate or invalidate distribution rules.

#### Planned Parenthood of Southeastern Pennsylvania v. Casey — 1992-06-29

Source: `records/Planned_Parenthood_of_Southeastern_Pennsylvania_v_Casey_merits_1992-06-29.md`.

### 1. Protected choice and the boundary of the decision

**Holding and operative rule.** The Fourteenth Amendment protects a woman’s choice to obtain an abortion before viability against prohibition and substantial state interference. Roe’s protection after viability remains subject to the State’s authority to restrict abortion with an exception when necessary to preserve the woman’s life or health; the State has legitimate health and potential-life interests throughout pregnancy.

**Authority.** Joint opinion Part I of O’Connor, Kennedy and Souter, joined by Stone, Blackmun and Stevens: six Justices adopt these propositions. This majority does not adopt a single general standard for all regulation of abortion.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court retains the central constitutional protection recognized in Roe. Griswold, Eisenstadt and Carey protect personal reproductive decisions and refute the argument that liberty extends only to activities individually named in the Constitution. Roe also recognizes the State’s interests in maternal health and potential life. Those interests do not authorize prohibition of the protected previability choice. The central boundary remains workable, and neither subsequent doctrine nor the record’s medical facts have removed its constitutional foundation. Reliance includes decisions about family life and personal futures; it is not confined to commercial arrangements. Webster did not overrule this protection. Disagreement with a prior decision, or a change in the Court’s membership, is not itself a sufficient reason to withdraw it. The Court decides the specified regulations and preserves the existing viability and life-and-health boundary. It announces no constitutional fetal-personhood rule and adjudicates no unchallenged postviability prohibition.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Roe v. Wade, 410 U.S. 113 — core choice, viability and necessary life/health protection retained; specific regulatory treatment appears below.
- Doe v. Bolton, 410 U.S. 179 — life-and-health safeguard retained.
- Griswold v. Connecticut, 381 U.S. 479 — applied to reproductive liberty.
- Eisenstadt v. Baird, 405 U.S. 438 — applied to individual reproductive choice.
- Carey v. Population Services International, 431 U.S. 678 — applied to reproductive liberty, without mechanically resolving abortion regulation.
- Webster v. Reproductive Health Services, 492 U.S. 490 — its fragmentation did not overrule Roe’s core.

### 2. The protective emergency construction

**Holding and operative rule.** Section 3203 is sustained as construed to permit a physician exercising good-faith clinical judgment to proceed without compliance with the Act’s consent and notice requirements when compliance would significantly threaten the woman’s life or health, including the three conditions disputed here. The physician need not await the threatened serious injury before acting; contrary enforcement remains challengeable.

**Authority.** Joint opinion Part II-A, joined by all nine Justices. This common construction does not adopt Stone’s additional mental-health discussion as a nine-Justice holding.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court evaluates the provision as the Court of Appeals construed it. Brockett supports respect for a lower federal court’s supportable construction of state law. The construction here prevents compliance with the abortion regulations from producing a significant threat to life or health. It covers preeclampsia, inevitable abortion and prematurely ruptured membranes, the three conditions on which the facial objection rests. A rule directed to serious risk must permit treatment before the anticipated injury occurs. The Court therefore rejects the argument that compliance must continue until the threatened harm becomes irreversible. This reading resolves the claimed exclusion of the disputed conditions without supplying hypothetical findings or a judicial list of further emergencies. The disclosure-specific defense in §3205(c) does not replace the emergency exception. Neither this judgment nor the adopted construction approves enforcement requiring harmful delay in an actual case.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Brockett v. Spokane Arcades, Inc., 472 U.S. 491 — applied to respect a supportable state-law construction.
- Doe v. Bolton, 410 U.S. 179 — its protection of medical judgment remains; no exhaustive emergency list is adopted.

### 3. Facial vagueness of the clinical standard

**Holding and operative rule.** Section 3203’s familiar medical judgments and subjective good-faith clinical standard provide constitutionally adequate warning on this facial record. The provision is not sustained on an assumed power to impose hindsight objective liability contrary to that construction.

**Authority.** Joint opinion Part II-B, O’Connor, Kennedy and Souter joined by White, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia and Thomas: eight ground votes. Stone sustains the emergency provision on his approved protective construction and does not join this additional ground.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court rejects the contention that the emergency definition gives physicians constitutionally inadequate notice. Vuitch and Doe recognize that physicians routinely determine whether a procedure is required to preserve a patient’s life or health. Those ordinary medical judgments do not become impermissibly vague merely because their application requires clinical evaluation. The provision here expressly uses the physician’s good-faith judgment, and the adopted construction protects decisions made under that subjective standard. Colautti presented uncertainty about the governing viability trigger and whether the physician faced an objective or subjective obligation. That uncertainty does not control this different text. Together with the life-and-health construction, the good-faith standard gives fair warning on the present facial record. The Court does not approve an enforcement practice that disregards that standard, and it does not determine a physician’s liability in any actual prosecution or disciplinary proceeding.

**Precedent treatment.**

- United States v. Vuitch, 402 U.S. 62 — applied to ordinary medical judgments.
- Doe v. Bolton, 410 U.S. 179 — applied to clinical judgment.
- Colautti v. Franklin, 439 U.S. 379 — distinguished because of its different uncertain viability trigger.

### 4. Ordinary medical information and voluntary written consent

**Holding and operative rule.** The State may require voluntary written informed consent and accurate, medically relevant disclosure of the procedure, material risks and alternatives, and childbirth risks. Accurate gestational-age information is also permissible informed-consent content; these content holdings do not themselves decide who must speak or how long the patient must wait.

**Authority.** Joint opinion Part IV-A has all nine Justices on the ordinary written-consent and medical-risk proposition; Stevens alone does not join sustaining compulsory gestational-age content. Stone and Blackmun’s content joins do not approve the separately disputed physician and timing conditions.

**Controlling explanation.** Danforth permits written informed consent because a patient should understand the nature and consequences of the procedure she chooses. The disclosures concerning the abortion procedure, material risks and alternatives, and the medical risks of carrying the pregnancy to term address that purpose directly. Their content enables a medical choice rather than presuming that the patient has made the wrong choice. Accurate gestational age also describes the pregnancy to which that decision relates. The content holding remains distinct from the conditions under which information must be supplied. Accepting truthful medical information does not by itself approve compulsory state persuasion, exclusive physician delivery or a fixed waiting period. Those restrictions receive separate judgments and grounds below. Nor does this holding convert an optional printed publication into mandatory reading. It preserves ordinary medical advice and a written record of lawful consent while leaving the disputed conditions to their own analysis.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Danforth, 428 U.S. 52 — applied to voluntary informed written consent.
- Akron, 462 U.S. 416 — the permissibility of relevant medical information is preserved; its delivery and timing restrictions receive separate treatment below.

### 5. Prescribed information about alternatives, assistance and support

**Holding and operative rule.** The specified truthful and nonmisleading disclosures about available state materials, assistance and paternal support may be required on this facial record, with receipt of printed materials left to the woman’s choice. Under the narrow judgment-supporting rationale, the legitimate informed-choice and potential-life purposes may be served because these requirements do not establish a purpose or effect creating a substantial obstacle; certification may enforce the requirements sustained in this decision.

**Authority.** Joint opinion Part IV-B. O’Connor, Kennedy and Souter’s joint opinion supplies this narrow judgment-supporting permission; White, Scalia and Thomas concur under broader rational review. Under Marks, the permission is a true logical subset of that broader rationale, and the six writers support this same judgment component. Stone, Blackmun and Stevens dissent. This is not a majority adoption of the general Part III undue-burden formula.

**Controlling explanation.** Danforth’s informed-consent principle permits a State to ensure that a patient has relevant information before choosing. The disclosures here tell the woman that printed materials and assistance may be available and that the father bears support obligations, subject to the stated rape qualification. Their truth and nonmisleading character matter. The woman need not accept or read the printed materials. The State may hope that information leads her to choose childbirth; that preference alone does not transform accurate advice into an unconstitutional obstacle under the narrower rationale supporting this judgment. The facial record does not establish the necessary obstruction from these disclosures. Thornburgh and Akron require a different result insofar as they prohibit such truthful prescribed information because it favors childbirth. Their contrary rules are displaced to this extent. This decision does not permit misinformation, ideological assent or materially different coercive disclosures, and written certification remains tied to duties actually sustained.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Danforth — informed-consent principle applied.
- Thornburgh — contrary prescribed-information holding overruled to the extent described.
- Akron — contrary prescribed-information holding overruled to the extent described.
- Marks, 430 U.S. 188 — the narrower judgment-supporting permission controls this component.

### 6. Physician delivery of medical information

**Holding and operative rule.** Pennsylvania’s requirement that the performing or referring physician deliver the specified medical information survives this facial challenge. Under the narrow judgment-supporting rationale, the requirement reasonably serves informed medical choice and the record does not establish a purpose or effect creating a substantial obstacle; no categorical permission for all physician-exclusive counseling follows.

**Authority.** Joint opinion Part IV-B. O’Connor, Kennedy and Souter’s joint opinion supplies this narrow judgment-supporting permission; White, Scalia and Thomas concur under broader rational review. Under Marks, the permission is a true logical subset of that broader rationale, and the six writers support this same judgment component. Stone, Blackmun and Stevens dissent. This is not a majority adoption of the general Part III undue-burden formula.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court separates the qualifications of the speaker from the information’s content. The requirement concerns medical facts about the procedure, its risks and alternatives, the pregnancy and childbirth. A State may reasonably conclude that a physician can explain those subjects and answer resulting medical questions. The record shows that qualified counselors can convey information and that physician delivery may increase cost. Those facts support the opposing view, but they do not establish a substantial obstacle under the narrower rationale supporting the judgment. The physician requirement is therefore sustained on this facial record. Akron’s rule invalidating this restriction notwithstanding that showing cannot remain controlling to the contrary extent. The holding does not require a physician to perform every counseling task or establish that every physician-only requirement is valid. It also does not alter the physician’s responsibility for appropriate medical judgment or decide a later challenge supported by materially different access burdens.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Akron — its contrary physician-delivery holding overruled to the extent it requires invalidation under the rule applied here.
- Marks — the narrower judgment-supporting permission controls the physician-delivery component.

### 7. The 24-hour interval

**Holding and operative rule.** The 24-hour interval between the required disclosures and abortion survives this facial record under the narrow judgment-supporting rule permitting reasonable informed-choice measures that do not have the purpose or effect of creating a substantial obstacle. The emergency exception continues to protect prompt care, and materially different demonstrated burdens remain open.

**Authority.** Joint opinion Part IV-B. O’Connor, Kennedy and Souter’s joint opinion supplies this narrow judgment-supporting permission; White, Scalia and Thomas concur under broader rational review. Under Marks, the permission is a true logical subset of that broader rationale, and the six writers support this same judgment component. Stone, Blackmun and Stevens dissent. This is not a majority adoption of the general Part III undue-burden formula.

**Controlling explanation.** The 24-hour condition is assessed independently of the information that begins the interval. The record establishes added travel, expense, repeat visits and delays that may exceed a single day, particularly for women with limited resources. The narrower rationale supporting the judgment does not deny those burdens. It concludes that the record does not establish a substantial obstacle and that time for considered choice reasonably advances the State’s legitimate interests. Maternal health is not the State’s only permissible concern; the decision also concerns potential life. The emergency construction prevents required waiting when compliance would significantly threaten life or health. Akron’s contrary mandatory-delay holding is overruled to the extent it requires invalidation of this rule. This is a facial judgment on the existing evidence, not a finding that every woman can bear every delay. A different actual application or materially different proof remains subject to constitutional review.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Akron — contrary mandatory-waiting holding overruled to the stated extent.
- Marks — applied to this particular sustaining judgment, without adopting the joint general test as a majority opinion.

### 8. The providers’ distinct speech claim

**Holding and operative rule.** The First Amendment does not invalidate the truthful, nonmisleading factual disclosures at issue as reasonable regulation of the provision of medical services. This holding authorizes no compelled ideological endorsement and does not classify all professional speech as commercial speech.

**Authority.** Joint opinion Part IV-C, joined on this independent provider-speech proposition by White, Scalia and Thomas in addition to O’Connor, Kennedy and Souter: six ground votes. Stone joins rejection of the separate providers’ First Amendment claim by the approved silent-component fallback and adds no ground; his contrary abortion-liberty judgment is unchanged. Blackmun and Stevens approve ordinary medical disclosure but do not reach an additional speech ground against the persuasion commands they independently invalidate.

**Controlling explanation.** The providers assert their own First Amendment interests in addition to the patients’ protected abortion choice. The challenged factual disclosures do not require a physician to affirm that abortion is morally wrong or to profess agreement with the State’s preference. They require accurate information in providing a regulated medical service. The Court’s conclusion depends on that limited character. The State’s power to regulate informed consent would be defeated if every factual disclosure requirement necessarily infringed the provider’s freedom of speech. Conversely, describing a medical service as commercial does not authorize unrelated ideological commands. The Court rejects the providers’ facial claim on the truthful and nonmisleading disclosures presented here. This conclusion neither establishes the validity of every possible professional-speech restriction nor supplies the rule governing the distinct burdens on the patient’s abortion decision. Those burdens receive their separate constitutional analysis, and the Justices’ different votes on that analysis remain distinct.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Danforth — its recognition of informed-consent regulation supplies the medical-service setting, not a universal First Amendment exemption. The judgment below’s broader reliance on a commercial-speech classification is not adopted as a general rule.

### 9. The independent parental bypass

**Holding and operative rule.** When a State conditions an unemancipated minor’s abortion on parental consent, it must provide an effective independent route to authorization alternatively on maturity and informed choice or on the minor’s best interests, with confidentiality and expedition and without requiring a prior parental request. Pennsylvania’s basic one-parent/bypass structure satisfies those requirements on this facial record.

**Authority.** Joint opinion Part IV-D’s constitutional bypass rule, O’Connor, Kennedy and Souter joined by Stone, Blackmun and Stevens: six votes. White, Scalia and Thomas sustain the basic procedure under broader rational review without joining the constitutionally necessary safeguards; the basic-structure judgment is nine to zero. Incorporated counseling, timing and delivery conditions are separately addressed.

**Controlling explanation.** Danforth forbids giving a parent an absolute veto over the minor’s protected decision. Bellotti II and Ashcroft require an effective independent route when parental consent is imposed, and Pennsylvania supplies that alternative. A mature and informed minor may obtain authorization on her own choice, and an immature minor may obtain it when abortion is in her best interests. She need not first seek a parent’s consent, so access to court does not depend on a parent’s willingness to cooperate. Counsel and confidentiality protect the proceeding. The trial court must act within three business days and the appellate court within five more business days. Ohio v. Akron Center and Hodgson support evaluating those practical safeguards without assuming that they will fail. Emergencies require prompt care. The Court sustains the basic structure while preserving review of an actually unavailable bypass and separately deciding the disputed informed-parent conditions.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Danforth — prohibition of an absolute parental veto applied.
- Bellotti v. Baird, 443 U.S. 622 — alternative maturity or best-interests route, confidentiality and expedition applied.
- Ashcroft, 462 U.S. 476 — sufficient bypass applied.
- Ohio v. Akron Center, 497 U.S. 502 — practical confidentiality and expedition applied.
- Hodgson, 497 U.S. 417 — effective independent alternative applied.

### 10. Incorporated informed-parental requirements

**Holding and operative rule.** The actual informed-parental-consent requirement survives this facial challenge because its incorporated disclosures and related conditions survive the separate judgments in this decision and the independent bypass remains available. The narrower sustaining rule does not permit an invalid substantive condition to be revived by requiring a parent’s signature.

**Authority.** Joint opinion Part IV-D. O’Connor, Kennedy and Souter’s joint opinion supplies this narrow judgment-supporting permission; White, Scalia and Thomas concur under broader rational review. Under Marks, the permission is a true logical subset of that broader rationale, and the six writers support this same judgment component. Stone, Blackmun and Stevens dissent. This is not a majority adoption of the general Part III undue-burden formula.

**Controlling explanation.** A valid bypass does not answer every objection to the information required for parental consent. The Court therefore considers the incorporation separately. The challenged disclosures and conditions have been sustained on this facial record, and asking that the participating parent be informed does not itself establish an additional substantial obstacle under the narrower judgment-supporting rationale. The independent judicial procedure remains available when the minor elects not to obtain parental consent. Bellotti II and Ashcroft support that route; neither permits a parent to exercise an absolute veto. The opposing Justices would remove the incorporated commands because they would invalidate those commands in the adult informed-consent provision. The controlling judgments reject that premise for the requirements sustained here. No rule authorizes incorporation of an unconstitutional duty, delay that defeats the bypass or failure to provide emergency care. Severability and implementation must conform to the actual component holdings.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Bellotti II — independent route applied without authorization to incorporate an invalid duty.
- Ashcroft — sufficient bypass applied without authorization to incorporate an invalid duty.
- Marks — applied only to the narrow sustaining rationale for the complete informed-parent component.

### 11. The operation of spousal notice

**Holding and operative rule.** Section 3209’s compulsory spousal notice, certification and dependent enforcement are unconstitutional on this record because they transfer practical control of the protected abortion decision through coercion that the statutory exceptions do not adequately prevent. A formal distinction between notice and a signature veto does not save that operation.

**Authority.** Joint opinion Part V-A, O’Connor, Kennedy and Souter joined by Stone, Blackmun and Stevens: six votes. The distinct facial denominator rule is addressed next; Stone does not join that further formulation.

**Controlling explanation.** Danforth prevents the State from transferring control of a woman’s abortion decision to her spouse. The formal absence of a spouse’s consent requirement does not resolve the practical operation of this notice rule. The trial record establishes that compulsory disclosure can produce violence, economic pressure and other coercion preventing a woman from obtaining an abortion. The exceptions do not cover all serious coercion or threatened harm to others, and requiring an assault report may itself defeat the claimed protection. A spouse’s interest in family life does not authorize making such disclosure a legal condition of treatment of another competent adult. The parent-minor cases concern different legal responsibilities and an independent bypass; they do not support this adult-spouse condition. The Court therefore preserves injunctive relief against §3209 and its dependent enforcement. Voluntary consultation remains unrestricted, and the Court does not adjudicate every conceivable notice arrangement.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Danforth — protection against transferred spousal control applied to notice’s actual operation.
- Hodgson — distinguished as a parent-minor procedure with different authority and safeguards.
- Ohio v. Akron Center — distinguished as a parent-minor procedure with different authority and safeguards.

### 12. The class relevant to this facial challenge

**Holding and operative rule.** For the §3209 facial challenge, the relevant class consists of women for whom the law imposes a restriction, including married women who would not otherwise notify and lack an applicable exception. The rule is invalid because a large fraction of that class faces a substantial obstacle; voluntary notifiers and other women outside the restriction do not supply the denominator that defeats the claim.

**Authority.** Joint opinion Part V-B, O’Connor, Kennedy and Souter joined by Blackmun and Stevens: five votes. Stone’s additional notice-invalidity vote adopts neither a percentage threshold nor this further formulation.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court assesses the notice requirement where it actually restricts conduct. Most married women’s voluntary consultation says little about the effect of compelling those who would not give notice and lack an applicable exception. The record identifies serious coercive obstacles within that constrained group. For a large fraction of those women, the rule prevents the protected decision from being exercised freely. Counting every woman who seeks an abortion would dilute that constitutional inquiry with persons upon whom the challenged command imposes no restriction. Salerno’s broad facial-challenge language does not require that result in this abortion challenge. Danforth and the abortion cases examine the operation of categorical burdens on the protected choice. The Court therefore rejects the proposed all-patients denominator. It does not announce a fixed numerical percentage, determine a universal denominator for all statutes or replace facial-review doctrine in unrelated constitutional settings.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Danforth — categorical protection of abortion choice applied.
- Salerno, 481 U.S. 739 — not applied to defeat this abortion claim merely because other persons voluntarily do what the challenged law commands; general facial-review doctrine otherwise reserved.

### 13. The notice-dependent report

**Holding and operative rule.** Section 3214(a)(12)’s reporting of marital and notice status and reasons for failing to notify is invalid as part of the unconstitutional notice machinery. A report cannot preserve the invalid condition by demanding the information that the condition unlawfully requires; remand must address severability and dependent enforcement.

**Authority.** Joint opinion Part V-A’s dependent-report proposition, joined by Stone, Blackmun and Stevens with O’Connor, Kennedy and Souter: six votes. Its rationale follows the notice invalidation rather than approving a general ban on marital or medical data.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court considers the notice paragraph separately from the ordinary medical reports. Section 3214(a)(12) asks whether the patient is married, whether her spouse received notice and why notice was not given. Those questions serve the notice arrangement the Court holds unconstitutional. The State cannot retain the same disclosure machinery merely by placing its demands on a report sent by the provider. Danforth permits legitimate health records while forbidding their use to accomplish an unconstitutional restriction, and that limit resolves this dependency. The report’s confidentiality does not cure its connection to the invalid condition. This conclusion does not prohibit independently justified health information or decide that every possible inquiry into marital status is unlawful. It invalidates this notice-linked paragraph on the actual statutory arrangement. The courts on remand must conform the injunction and determine Pennsylvania-law severability while preserving independently functioning valid provisions.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Danforth — the limit against using reporting to accomplish an unconstitutional restriction applied.
- The ruling on §3209 — controls its reporting dependency within this coordinated decision.

### 14. Confidential medical-judgment and referral reports

**Holding and operative rule.** The challenged non-notice procedure reports, including the specified medical-judgment bases and referring-physician identification, are sustained under Danforth because they have concrete health and lawful compliance purposes, protect patient confidentiality and are not shown to use recordkeeping detail to accomplish an unconstitutional restriction. This native reporting rule does not adopt a new general substantial-obstacle threshold.

**Authority.** Joint opinion Part VI-A, O’Connor, Kennedy and Souter joined by Stone and Stevens: five votes at this specified level. White, Scalia and Thomas concur in the judgment under rational review; Blackmun dissents.

**Controlling explanation.** Danforth permits confidential health reporting and forbids using recordkeeping detail to accomplish an otherwise unconstitutional restriction. These reports identify procedures, gestation, complications and the medical judgments supporting lawful exceptions. Referring physicians can perform consent and gestational-age functions, making their identity relevant to those responsibilities. The established confidentiality differs from the threatened patient exposure addressed in Thornburgh. The recorded expense and possible reluctance of referring physicians do not establish the use of these particular reports to restrict protected care contrary to Danforth. The Court relies on those limited health-purpose, confidentiality and anti-restriction grounds, not on agreement about a general threshold governing every abortion regulation. This conclusion approves neither patient identification nor materially different burdens. Reports also cannot enforce an invalid duty: the notice paragraph is excluded for that reason. Independently operating valid health records remain subject to the severability proceedings required by the remand.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Danforth — confidential health reporting and its anti-restriction limit applied.
- Thornburgh — patient-exposure rule distinguished on the established confidentiality; no general patient-identification permission is created.

### 15. Public facility and aggregate reports

**Holding and operative rule.** The specified public facility-identity, affiliation and aggregate reports survive this facial challenge when state appropriations trigger public access because they concern institutional accountability, do not identify patients and lack an established connection to an additional unconstitutional restriction on protected care. The ruling does not approve materially different disclosures, reprisals or reporting burdens.

**Authority.** Joint opinion Part VI-A’s public-report application, O’Connor, Kennedy and Souter joined by Stone and Stevens: five votes. White, Scalia and Thomas concur in the judgment; Blackmun dissents. The independent funds-condition objection is separately resolved below.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court separates public institutional information from private patient information. Sections 3207(b) and 3214(f) make specified facility and aggregate reports public when the statutory state-funding conditions apply. Those reports identify institutions, affiliations and activity, rather than individual patients. Public accountability supplies a concrete reason for access to information concerning the use of appropriations. The record describes demonstrations and concern about increased harassment, but it does not establish that these particular disclosures cause an additional unconstitutional restriction on protected care. Thornburgh’s patient-exposure problem is therefore absent from this record. This conclusion does not disregard a demonstrated risk of identification or reprisals in a different case. It sustains only the particular disclosures challenged here, with their funding and institutional-information limits. The underlying filing duties were not themselves the target of this public-access objection, and no new duty to disclose patient identity follows.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Thornburgh — distinguished because the present public reports do not expose individual patients.
- Danforth — its anti-restriction principle remains; confidential medical reporting does not itself authorize every public disclosure.

### 16. The distinct public-funds condition objection

**Holding and operative rule.** Making the specified institutional and aggregate reports of a state-funded facility public does not, on this record, impose an unconstitutional condition on a benefit: no surrender of privately financed protected abortion activity or demonstrated additional access obstacle is established. The State receives no general authority to prohibit privately financed abortions as a condition of support.

**Authority.** Joint opinion Part VI-B, joined by White, Stevens, Scalia and Thomas with O’Connor, Kennedy and Souter: seven ground votes. Stone sustains the public-report component on his approved reporting/privacy ground without joining this additional theory. Blackmun opposes the challenged public disclosures.

**Controlling explanation.** The challenged condition makes limited information about institutions receiving state appropriations available to the public. It does not require a recipient to cease providing abortions with private resources, and the reports do not identify the patients. Public accountability is a legitimate reason to disclose the institutions and aggregate services supported by public money. The record establishes concern about demonstrations but does not establish the necessary connection between this disclosure and a new barrier to protected care. The unconstitutional-conditions objection therefore supplies no independent ground to invalidate these provisions. The Court does not infer from the State’s power to define a funded program that it may suppress a recipient’s separately financed protected activity. That distinction limits the judgment. The underlying institutional filing duties were not themselves the target of this public-access challenge, and the Court announces no new rule compelling disclosure of patient identities or excusing a proven burden on access.

**Precedent treatment.**

- The distinction between a funded program and separately financed activity remains; no general funding doctrine is enlarged. The judgment does not adopt every step of the Court of Appeals’ unconstitutional-conditions analysis.

**Present operation.** Effective June 29, 1992, the core previability choice and viability/life-and-health boundary remain controlling by six votes. There is no single majority replacement framework for every abortion regulation. The general joint undue-burden formula has three joins; the narrow sustaining rules for the specified §3205 and incorporated §3206 requirements control through issue-specific Marks, and the contrary Akron/Thornburgh applications are displaced to the stated extent. The record therefore cannot be described as retaining those regulatory holdings unchanged.

The protective emergency construction and familiar-medical-judgment facial rejection control. Spousal notice and its report are invalid; the affected-class/large-fraction rule has five proposition-level votes, while Stone’s additional vote does not expand that rule. Confidential health/facility reporting now has a five-Justice joint rationale under Danforth’s health-purpose, confidentiality and anti-restriction principles, without importing the general substantial-obstacle threshold into Stone’s join. The limited provider-speech and funds-condition rulings have their express coalitions. Other Roe/Doe protections, distinct precedent holdings and supported future applications remain subject to the precise limits above. Broad equality, fetal personhood, all postviability prohibitions, all professional speech and general facial-review doctrine are not adjudicated.

#### Lucas v. South Carolina Coastal Council — 1992-06-29

Source: `records/Lucas_v_South_Carolina_Coastal_Council_merits_1992-06-29.md`.

### The original categorical prohibition presents a reviewable earlier-period claim

**Holding and operative rule.** A later discretionary permit procedure does not bar review of compensation for an earlier categorical building prohibition where permission under that prohibition was unavailable and the state supreme court finally rejected the compensation claim on its merits. Any compensable taking must be remedied for its actual duration; the later procedure and unresolved factual issues remain relevant to future use, duration and valuation, and the permanent-value award is not automatically reinstated.

**Authority.** Scalia's Opinion of the Court, Part II, joined by Stone-Zsela, White, O'Connor and Thomas, controls with five votes. Kennedy separately concurs in the judgment and agrees that the earlier interval is reviewable, but does not join the Court's writing. The holding rests on the express five-Justice opinion without combining separate rationales.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that a new opportunity to seek future permission cannot eliminate a completed deprivation from judicial review. Williamson County and MacDonald ordinarily require a final determination of permitted development so that a court knows the restriction it must assess. Here the Council stipulated that the original Act allowed no building permit, and the state supreme court rejected compensation on a categorical merits ground after the amendment existed. First English establishes that ending a restriction does not eliminate compensation for a period in which it effected a taking. The Council's request for further permitting therefore cannot dispose of the earlier interval.
