Yee preserves the difference between a distinct unpresented claim and an argument supporting the claim actually decided; the earlier deprivation belongs to the compensation dispute already presented. These conclusions do not establish that a taking occurred or fix its value. The Court leaves the new permit avenue, the extent and duration of actual loss, and the defenses the state supreme court did not decide for further proceedings.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Williamson County Regional Planning Commission v. Hamilton Bank, 473 U.S. 172 (1985): preserves ordinary finality requirements; distinguishes an absolute prohibition for an already elapsed period.
- MacDonald, Sommer & Frates v. Yolo County, 477 U.S. 340 (1986): preserves the need to determine permitted development; an unavailable original permit need not be sought to test the earlier prohibition.
- First English Evangelical Lutheran Church v. County of Los Angeles, 482 U.S. 304 (1987): applies compensation for an actual temporary taking; does not decide whether this regulation effected one or set the amount.
- Yee v. City of Escondido, simulated April 1, 1992: applies the distinction between a presented claim and a distinct unpresented taking theory; no new physical-taking rule follows.

### Complete economic deprivation requires compensation unless background ownership law already foreclosed the use

**Holding and operative rule.** A land-use regulation that removes all economically beneficial use of the relevant property effects a taking requiring just compensation unless the prohibited use was already outside the owner's title under background state property or nuisance law. After the owner establishes complete deprivation, the State must identify and substantiate an applicable background limitation that would independently prohibit the claimed use in the property's circumstances; a new legislative harm declaration alone does not suffice, and ordinary adjudication supplies the proof requirements without a special heightened quantum.

**Authority.** Scalia's Opinion of the Court, Part III, joined by Stone-Zsela, White, O'Connor and Thomas, supplies five votes for the complete rule and its explanation. Kennedy supports reversal through an expectations-sensitive analysis, without joining this categorical formulation. No Marks combination is required or used.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that public authority to restrict land and responsibility to pay for its complete economic loss are separate questions. Pennsylvania Coal recognizes that otherwise valid regulation can impose a compensable taking. Agins supplies the complete-use formulation, which the Court now specifies as a categorical rule with an ownership limitation. Penn Central continues to govern ordinary partial burdens. A purpose-only exemption would let the State describe the same sacrifice as preventing harm or conferring a benefit and thereby determine its own compensation duty. Mugler and the older noxious-use cases do not establish that exemption for proved elimination of all beneficial land use. Roth and Scranton explain why the owner's entitlement remains bounded by existing law: prohibiting an entitlement never held takes nothing. The trial finding supplies the premise of this review, but does not settle the Council's preserved factual objections. South Carolina must identify genuine background principles and their application on remand. Changed knowledge may affect nuisance law's application; a new legislative label cannot replace it. The Court decides no parcel-definition rule or percentage shortcut.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Pennsylvania Coal Co. v. Mahon, 260 U.S. 393 (1922): applies its recognition that regulation can go too far despite valid public purposes; does not attribute today's exact categorical rule to that decision.
- Agins v. City of Tiburon, 447 U.S. 255 (1980): specifies and extends its complete-economic-use formulation into the rule and necessary exception stated here; does not adjudicate its distinct legitimate-interest formulation.
- Penn Central Transportation Co. v. New York City, 438 U.S. 104 (1978): preserves contextual analysis for partial deprivation, including economic impact, expectations and the character of governmental action; no percentage automatically defeats or establishes compensation.
- Mugler v. Kansas, 123 U.S. 623 (1887), Hadacheck v. Sebastian, 239 U.S. 394 (1915), Miller v. Schoene, 276 U.S. 272 (1928), and Goldblatt v. Hempstead, 369 U.S. 590 (1962): limits a broad noxious-use reading that would make any legislative harm characterization conclusive despite complete beneficial-use deprivation; preserves their actual outcomes and lawful protective regulation.
- Keystone Bituminous Coal Association v. DeBenedictis, 480 U.S. 470 (1987): distinguishes its productive-enterprise and partial-interest setting; no reversal of its actual holding or adoption of a new denominator follows.
- Board of Regents v. Roth, 408 U.S. 564 (1972): applies existing rules and understandings to identify the protected property interest; does not decide a procedural due-process claim.
- Scranton v. Wheeler, 179 U.S. 141 (1900): applies its antecedent-title-limit principle; a previously existing servitude is distinct from a newly imposed public burden.
- Loretto v. Teleprompter Manhattan CATV Corp., 458 U.S. 419 (1982), and Kaiser Aetna v. United States, 444 U.S. 164 (1979): preserve physical-occupation and exclusion protections and illuminate the difference between existing limitations and a new imposed servitude; this decision concerns regulatory loss.
- Webb's Fabulous Pharmacies, Inc. v. Beckwith, 449 U.S. 155 (1980): applies the prohibition on converting private property into public property by governmental assertion alone.

**Limits and questions not reached.** The Court assumes the trial court's total-loss finding to decide the presented legal issue; it makes no new appraisal and does not resolve the Council's preserved challenge to that finding. No unconditional entitlement to the trial award, final application of state nuisance law, right to anticipated profits, new parcel rule, or disposition of post-amendment permission is adopted. Legitimate environmental purposes remain sufficient to justify regulation; compensation is a distinct consequence. The Court overrules no earlier decision.

**Present operation.** Effective June 29, 1992, proved complete deprivation of economically beneficial land use triggers a categorical compensation rule unless background state ownership law already forecloses the use. The State must identify and substantiate that exception under ordinary adjudication. This specifies the earlier complete-use formulation without creating a percentage rule, parcel definition or heightened proof quantum. The original absolute prohibition is reviewable despite later permitting; unresolved facts, duration, valuation and state-law defenses remain for remand. Partial-loss doctrine, public regulatory authority and ordinary finality outside this setting remain intact.

#### Espinosa v. Florida — 1992-06-29

Source: `records/Espinosa_v_Florida_summary_merits_1992-06-29.md`.

### A legally influential recommendation cannot rest on an inadequately narrowed aggravator

**Holding and operative rule.** When a weighing State requires its sentencing judge to give great weight to a jury's recommendation, an inadequately narrowed aggravating instruction to that jury can infect the final sentence even if the judge directly finds only valid aggravators. The especially wicked, evil, atrocious or cruel instruction given here lacks the guidance the Eighth Amendment requires; the judge's separate findings do not by themselves cure the recommendation's legal influence.

**Authority.** The per curiam Opinion of the Court is joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas, seven Justices, for this rule and its application. White's preference for argument is not a present merits vote. Scalia rejects the weighing premise in dissent. An express majority supplies authority without any Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that constitutional guidance must follow the actual allocation of sentencing responsibility. Godfrey, Maynard and Shell require an aggravating category to distinguish the cases to which it applies; the ordinary words given this jury supply no adequate boundary. Stringer and Sochor distinguish eligibility supported by valid factors from the later weighing that selects death. Florida law makes the jury's general recommendation consequential by requiring the judge to give it great weight, whether it recommends life or death. Mills does not permit an assumption that jurors ignored an authorized invalid category, and Walton's presumption that a judge follows the law means the recommendation received the weight state law required. The judge thus cannot be treated as wholly independent of the defective recommendation. Baldwin permits a different allocation of sentencing roles; it does not eliminate an actual duty of deference. The Court does not find that particular jurors selected particular aggravators. It decides the legal effect of the unguided recommendation and preserves the State's ability to structure a constitutionally guided sentencing process.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Godfrey v. Georgia, 446 U.S. 420 (1980), Maynard v. Cartwright, 486 U.S. 356 (1988), and Shell v. Mississippi, 498 U.S. 1 (1990): apply their requirement of meaningful guidance for a capital aggravating category; the instruction here is insufficiently narrowed.
- Stringer v. Black, simulated March 9, 1992: applies the distinction between valid eligibility and constitutionally defective weighing; surviving valid aggravators alone do not remove the error.
- Sochor v. Florida, simulated June 8, 1992: applies its weighing-error rule while distinguishing its independent state preservation bar to a different jury claim and its separate factual-invalidity issue.
- Mills v. Maryland, 486 U.S. 367 (1988): applies the refusal to presume that jurors disregard an instruction's constitutionally defective route; no factor-specific verdict is invented.
- Walton v. Arizona, 497 U.S. 639 (1990): preserves genuine judicial sentencing and the presumption that judges apply legal narrowing; distinguishes a system in which a separate unguided recommendation carries required weight.
- Baldwin v. Alabama, 472 U.S. 372 (1985), and Spaziano v. Florida, 468 U.S. 447 (1984): preserve States' latitude in allocating sentencing responsibility; that latitude does not validate influential unguided weighing.
- Tedder v. State, 322 So.2d 908 (Fla. 1975), Smith v. State, 515 So.2d 182 (Fla. 1987), and Grossman v. State, 525 So.2d 833 (Fla. 1988): supply the state-law requirement to give great weight to jury recommendations of life or death.
- Smalley v. State, 546 So.2d 720 (Fla. 1989): rejects its premise that the jury's advisory designation alone makes this vague instruction constitutionally immaterial.

### Correction must address the recommendation's influence on the death sentence

**Holding and operative rule.** Reversal of the rejection of this sentencing claim requires further proceedings that remove the influence of the invalid jury recommendation through lawful reweighing or harmless-error review satisfying Chapman beyond a reasonable doubt. If no lawful cure is available, a new sentencing proceeding is required; valid remaining aggravators alone do not establish a cure, and neither an automatic life sentence nor disturbance of the guilt judgment follows.

**Authority.** The same seven Justices join the per curiam opinion's remedy instruction. They expressly apply the existing Stringer, Sochor, Clemons and Chapman correction requirements to the two-stage defect found here. No agreement is attributed to White on this unargued merits question or to Scalia on the need for correction.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that correction must reach the error actually found. Clemons permits an authorized reviewing court to reweigh valid aggravation against mitigation or determine that an invalid factor was harmless; it does not treat a surviving majority of factors as an answer. Chapman supplies the beyond-a-reasonable-doubt harmlessness standard. Stringer and Sochor require an actual lawful cure of invalid weighing, and Parker requires attention to the sentencing record that genuinely existed. Here the jury's general recommendation carried legally required weight into the judge's decision. The judge's four valid aggravators and two mitigators therefore do not establish that this indirect error had no effect. Further review must account for that channel of influence, rather than repeat the premise that the judge alone imposed sentence. The Court does not undertake its own balancing, choose a sentence, or decide that Florida law authorizes every proposed appellate cure. It leaves those proceedings to the state court under the stated constitutional limits. The conviction and unrelated petition questions are outside this disposition.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Clemons v. Mississippi, 494 U.S. 738 (1990): applies lawful appellate reweighing or harmless-error correction; merely counting valid aggravators does not cure the error.
- Chapman v. California, 386 U.S. 18 (1967): applies harmlessness beyond a reasonable doubt; no lesser standard or burden on the defendant is created.
- Stringer v. Black, simulated March 9, 1992, and Sochor v. Florida, simulated June 8, 1992: apply actual cure requirements to the recommendation's transmitted influence; no unreasoned harmlessness label suffices.
- Parker v. Dugger, 498 U.S. 308 (1991): applies review grounded in the real sentencing record and mitigation, not an assumed alternative record.

**Limits and questions not reached.** No automatic reduction to life, retrial of guilt, adjudication of unrelated aggravators, factor-by-factor jury finding, or collateral-review gateway rule follows. The petition's severance and mitigation/time/funding/proportionality questions are not granted or adjudicated in this limited summary action; no separate denial of them is invented. No prior Supreme Court decision is overruled.

**Present operation.** Effective June 29, 1992, a jury recommendation that receives great legal weight is part of the constitutionally relevant weighing process. Its unguided aggravating category can contaminate the final sentence through that required influence even when the judge directly uses valid factors. Ordinary Stringer/Sochor correction must address this indirect route: lawful reweighing or Chapman review, and a new proceeding if no lawful cure exists. States retain latitude in assigning sentencing roles; no automatic life sentence, collateral-review rule or factor-specific jury presumption is created.

#### Benten v. Kessler — 1992-07-17

Source: `records/Benten_v_Kessler_A-40_application_to_vacate_stay_1992-07-17.md`.

### A substantial procedural challenge does not alone establish entitlement to mandatory return

**Holding and operative rule.** An applicant seeking to vacate an appellate stay of an order granting effectively final individual relief must show a substantial likelihood of entitlement to that ordered relief and a demonstrable error in the appellate stay. Where the asserted procedural defect would restore agency discretion rather than compel the requested substantive outcome, a strong procedural claim and serious irreparable injury do not alone establish entitlement to immediate judicially ordered return of the drug.

**Authority.** The per curiam opinion is joined by Stone-Zsela, White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas: seven Justices. The Court assumes the procedural challenge's strength for this remedial inquiry; it does not adopt Stone-Zsela's separate assessment that the then-operative FDA procedural regulation was likely violated.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the requested remedy requires a legal connection the applicants have not sufficiently shown. Western Airlines supplies the demanding review of an appellate stay; the Court must assess entitlement to the relief that would follow vacatur. Benten faces a serious imminent loss of her preferred noninvasive treatment, and the record supplies no ground to invent a health danger or an alternative placement. But the District Court acknowledged that the personal-use policy permits admission rather than compels it. Even assuming a strong challenge to automatic detention, removal of that policy restores a decision assigned to the agency. Ford Motor recognizes flexibility to make lawful agency proceedings effective; it does not clearly authorize a court to select the remaining discretionary result. Section 706(1) likewise permits relief requiring lawful action without automatically deciding its substance. Urgency and the individualized medical findings deserve weight, but do not close that remedial gap. The Court leaves the procedural merits for the proper proceedings and expresses no view on the unpresented constitutional access claim.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Western Airlines, Inc. v. Teamsters, 480 U.S. 1301 (1987) (in chambers): applied for the demanding standard governing intervention in an appellate stay; urgent injury does not eliminate the required merits-and-remedy showing.
- Ford Motor Co. v. NLRB, 305 U.S. 364 (1939): its flexibility to make lawful administrative action effective is preserved; it does not clearly establish entitlement to judicial selection of the discretionary admission decision on this application.
- United States v. Rutherford, 442 U.S. 544 (1979): the statutory approval framework remains in force; this application creates no judicial drug-approval exemption and makes no general safety finding.

**Limits and questions not reached.** The Court does not finally uphold the Import Alert, reject the asserted 1989 procedural undertaking, establish an across-the-board exhaustion command, decide §10.70 or arbitrary-or-capricious merits, or deny the possibility of prompt lawful agency-action relief. The constitutional claim discussed by Stevens was not addressed below or presented in the applicants' filings here and is not properly before the Court. Neither that claim nor the right to a particular abortion method is decided.

**Present operation.** Effective July 17, 1992, the Court denies this application because the showing of entitlement to mandatory return remains insufficient even assuming a strong procedural challenge. The remedial distinction controls at the appellate-stay stage. The Court establishes no final APA rule governing the Import Alert, no new exhaustion requirement, no substantive approval of RU-486 and no constitutional access rule. The pending appeal and otherwise proper agency-action channels retain their scope.

### 4. Material Published Noncontrolling Positions

- **Stone and Stevens — *Mireles*, October 21:** A genuinely separable command of gratuitous physical violence is operational rather than judicial, while warrants, contempt decisions, and attendance orders remain protected despite error. Noncontrolling concurrence in an equal-division disposition.
- **White, Blackmun, O'Connor, and Souter — *Mireles*, October 21:** The challenged conduct remains judicial at the general attendance-function level and is absolutely immune. Noncontrolling dissent from equal division.
- **Stone, Blackmun, and Stevens — *Campos*, October 29:** A court should not implement an expressly objected-to legislative plan for convenience where a nonobjected-to alternative exists; they would have stayed only Plan 2. Noncontrolling application dissent.
- **Stone, Blackmun, and Stevens — *Zatko*, November 4:** Filing history should not replace examination of the current submission, and a financial barrier risks excluding a later colorable indigent claim. Noncontrolling dissent.
- **Blackmun — *Griffin*, December 3:** The result follows from *Turner* without broad historical assumptions about jury competence. Noncontrolling concurrence in judgment.
- **O'Connor, Stone, and Stevens — *Estelle*, December 4:** The instruction created a reasonable likelihood of bypassing the required identity finding; they would remand for harmless-error review beyond a reasonable doubt. Noncontrolling.
- **Scalia — *Gibson*, December 4:** Position-specific objection followed by prompt escrow and arbitration is adequate because uncertain future lobbying need not be estimated; he joins the controlling panel-membership rule. Noncontrolling as to timing and objection scope.
- **Blackmun — *Simon & Schuster*, December 10:** Section 632-a is underinclusive as well as overinclusive. Noncontrolling elaboration.
- **Kennedy — *Simon & Schuster*, December 10:** A direct content-based burden on fully protected speech is forbidden here without compelling-interest balancing. Noncontrolling rationale.
- **Blackmun and Stevens — *Ardestani*, December 10:** “Under section 554” should functionally include statutorily required on-the-record adversary adjudications with government counsel. Noncontrolling dissent.
- **Scalia — *Wolas*, December 11:** The result follows from unambiguous text without legislative-history or policy analysis. Noncontrolling methodology; he joins the controlling statutory proposition and judgment.

- **Scalia and Kennedy — *Ray*, December 16:** Proposed derivative investigation is not itself a FOIA public benefit where the disclosed identifiers say nothing about agency performance. Noncontrolling join limitation.
- **Blackmun — *Hilton*, December 16:** Congress has broader power to subject state instrumentalities to FELA liability than the Court opinion needs to recognize. Noncontrolling rationale.
- **O'Connor and Scalia — *Hilton*, December 16:** A clearer statement is required and *Parden*'s coverage and waiver analyses should fall together. Dissent.
- **White and Scalia — *Hunter*, December 16:** The agents should receive immunity on the existing record. Noncontrolling remedy position. **Stevens:** denial should be affirmed rather than vacated. Noncontrolling remedy position.
- **Stevens and Blackmun — *Blodgett*, January 13:** The dispositive failure to seek modification does not justify broader criticism of the panel's coordination. Noncontrolling concurrence.
- **Scalia — *Smith*, January 14:** The Rule's express protection for informal form or title alone supplies the result. Noncontrolling methodology.
- **Blackmun — *County of Yakima*, January 14:** Later Indian legislation leaves ad valorem authorization insufficiently clear. Partial dissent.
- **Scalia — *Norman*, January 14:** Illinois may demand support for the county endeavor as a whole. Dissent.

- **INS v. Doherty, 1992-01-15:** White, joined by Blackmun and O'Connor, would sustain the denial of both applications under the regulation requiring material evidence previously unavailable; the prospect of a rejected destination did not become qualifying new evidence merely because it occurred. Kennedy would sustain both denials because the withdrawal was a tactical choice made with the destination risk known. Stone, joined by Stevens, Scalia and Souter, accepts denial of discretionary asylum but rejects using that discretion, or an imported asylum explanation requirement, to foreclose mandatory withholding. They would have the court of appeals review the administrative exclusions on the existing record and return the claim to the agency if those grounds fail. They neither award withholding nor treat the extradition decision as dispositive of the INA exclusions. **Status:** noncontrolling at the scope identified; no later treatment within this chunk. [Source record](records/INS_v_Doherty_merits_1992-01-15.md).
- **White v. Illinois, 1992-01-15:** Thomas, joined by Scalia, agrees with the exception-based judgment and its analysis but declines to reject the government's proposed definition of a witness. He would leave open reconsideration of whether confrontation particularly addresses formalized testimonial materials; that suggested reconstruction is not adopted law. **Status:** noncontrolling at the scope identified; no later treatment within this chunk. [Source record](records/White_v_Illinois_merits_1992-01-15.md).
- **Rufo v. Inmates of Suffolk County Jail; Rapone v. Inmates of Suffolk County Jail, 1992-01-15:** O'Connor concurs only in the judgment. She agrees that reconsideration is necessary because anticipation was treated as dispositive, relevant financial concerns were excluded, and any departure from single occupancy was treated as defeating the decree. She emphasizes the district judge's experience and discretion and does not compel administrative deference on whether modification is warranted. Stevens, joined by Blackmun, accepts flexibility but would affirm: the officials anticipated growth and reaffirmed single occupancy in 1985, and the trial court's alternative explanation adequately protects settlement reliance. **Status:** noncontrolling at the scope identified; no later treatment within this chunk. [Source record](records/Rufo_v_Inmates_of_Suffolk_County_Jail_merits_1992-01-15.md).
- **Dewsnup v. Timm, 1992-01-15:** Stone, joined by Scalia and Souter, would read the adjacent provisions consistently: allowance establishes the claim, while §506(a) supplies the amount secured for §506(d). They would preserve the established $39,000 secured portion and avoid only the unsupported excess, subject to preserved exceptions and estate-administration issues on remand. They distinguish Long and Johnson because survival through personal discharge does not defeat an affirmative statutory avoidance provision. Reliance interests cannot supply an unwritten exception to the enacted valuation language. **Status:** noncontrolling at the scope identified; no later treatment within this chunk. [Source record](records/Dewsnup_v_Timm_merits_1992-01-15.md).
- **Wyoming v. Oklahoma, 1992-01-22:** Scalia, joined by Thomas, would dismiss for lack of standing. He considers the inference from displaced purchases and unused capacity to lost taxable production insufficient on summary judgment and views the State's derivative tax interest as outside the protected commercial interests. Thomas, joined by Scalia, also would decline discretionary original jurisdiction even assuming standing, because the primary commercial injury could be litigated through ordinary private actions. Neither writing decides whether the quota would survive Commerce Clause review on the merits. **Status:** noncontrolling at the scope identified; no later treatment within this chunk. [Source record](records/Wyoming_v_Oklahoma_merits_1992-01-22.md).
- **INS v. Elias-Zacarias, 1992-01-22:** Stone agrees that the organization's agenda and the applicant's protected ground are different questions. He would remand for review of the Board's actual reopening decision, considering actual or imputed opinion and circumstantial motive without making new administrative findings or deciding that this record conclusively defeats the claim. Stevens, joined by Blackmun and O'Connor, would affirm the Ninth Circuit's relief: refusal to align with an armed political movement can express protected neutrality, and the coercive circumstances support that inference. Their position is an inference from the record, not an invented concession or an automatic award of asylum. **Status:** noncontrolling at the scope identified; no later treatment within this chunk. [Source record](records/INS_v_Elias_Zacarias_merits_1992-01-22.md).
- **Presley v. Etowah County Commission; Mack v. Russell County Commission, 1992-01-27:** Stevens, joined by White and Blackmun, would require preclearance of both resolutions. Allen's functional protection should reach substantial removal of the authority voters select an official to exercise; Etowah's change following the remedial electoral arrangement supplies an additional reason for coverage, without deciding discriminatory intent. Stone separately would cover substantial structural removal of a core, independently exercisable district-office function into appointment or materially different collective control. He distinguishes these changes from routine appropriations, individual hiring, or losses in ordinary policy votes, and would leave preclearance merits to the designated statutory channels. All four would reverse both appealed noncoverage judgments. **Status:** noncontrolling at the scope identified; no later treatment within this chunk. [Source record](records/Presley_v_Etowah_County_Commission_merits_1992-01-27.md).
- **Lechmere, Inc. v. NLRB, 1992-01-27:** White, joined by Blackmun, would uphold the Board's accommodation and its assessment of effective alternatives. He reads Babcock, Central Hardware and Hudgens as leaving broader room to balance and argues that modern administrative deference also supports Jean Country; he criticizes Babcock's original approach. Stevens agrees only with the accommodation and subsequent-precedent reasons. He defends Babcock's employee/nonemployee distinction and declines to join the claim that it was wrongly decided. All three would affirm enforcement. **Status:** noncontrolling at the scope identified; no later treatment within this chunk. [Source record](records/Lechmere_v_NLRB_merits_1992-01-27.md).

- **Hudson v. McMillian, 1992-02-25:** O’Connor/White/Kennedy/Souter, Part B, February 25: universal Whitley force standard; four-Justice noncontrolling proposition. Stevens, concurrence, same date: unnecessary-and-wanton pain suffices outside serious disturbances; higher standard met here. Blackmun, judgment concurrence, same date: opposes universal Whitley extension; constitutional pain may include substantial psychological harm, and docket reduction cannot define a substantive constitutional right. That psychological issue is unpresented and noncontrolling. Thomas/Scalia, dissent, same date: serious objective deprivation required, potentially nonphysical; State remedies and their constitutional adequacy remain a distinct route. No later treatment yet. [Source record](records/Hudson_v_McMillian_merits_1992-02-25.md).
- **United States v. Nordic Village, Inc., 1992-02-25:** Stevens and Blackmun, dissent, February 25: §106(c)’s express application-and-binding language reaches §550 monetary recovery; sovereign-immunity construction should not defeat that legislative command. They also criticize rigid judge-made immunity presumptions. Noncontrolling; no later treatment. [Source record](records/United_States_v_Nordic_Village_merits_1992-02-25.md).
- **Franklin v. Gwinnett County Public Schools, 1992-02-26:** Scalia and Thomas, concurrence in judgment, February 26: the 1986 remedies language supports Title IX damages; an implied cause does not automatically carry every remedy, and judicial limits on its remedial scope may be appropriate where Congress has not acknowledged them. Noncontrolling methodology; the Title IX result is shared. No later treatment. [Source record](records/Franklin_v_Gwinnett_County_Public_Schools_merits_1992-02-26.md).
- **Collins v. City of Harker Heights, 1992-02-26:** Stone, partial concurrence and judgment concurrence, February 26: known risk plus deliberate safety omissions do not supply the required custodial or affirmative-deprivation predicate; no coerced entry, bodily injury, blocked rescue or pleaded affirmative increase in danger exists here. Actual compulsion and properly pleaded affirmative danger remain distinct. This ground and reservation are noncontrolling as a separate writing; it announces no general affirmative-danger test. No later treatment. [Source record](records/Collins_v_City_of_Harker_Heights_merits_1992-02-26.md).
- **McCarthy v. Madigan, 1992-03-04:** Scalia and Thomas, concurrence in judgment, March 4: absence of monetary relief alone defeats exhaustion of this damages-only grievance; McNeese and Montana National Bank supply the adequate-remedy principle. They do not join the broader framework’s unmodified use for Bivens and reject reliance on reasonable filing deadlines with extensions. The no-compensation ground is shared with the Court; the contrary deadline and methodology positions are noncontrolling. No later treatment. [Source record](records/McCarthy_v_Madigan_merits_1992-03-04.md).

- **Dawson v. Delaware, 1992-03-09** — source: the separately named writings in `records/Dawson_v_Delaware_merits_1992-03-09.md`. Stone concurs in the relevance ruling and vacatur but would require Delaware to prove harmlessness beyond a reasonable doubt on the trial record, with new sentencing otherwise. Blackmun joins the Court and emphasizes that the permissibility of harmless-error treatment itself remains unresolved; this safeguard may protect against chilling association as well as an unreliable sentence. Thomas dissents: voluntary prison-gang membership can support a character and future-danger inference and rebut favorable character evidence. He would treat ordinary evidentiary fairness, rather than a categorical First Amendment insulation, as the inquiry. His inference is not a Court finding about this Delaware group. No later treatment is established; these positions do not change controlling law.

- **Williams v. United States, 1992-03-09** — source: the separately named writings in `records/Williams_v_United_States_merits_1992-03-09.md`. White, joined by Kennedy, reads the specific departure-review provision as controlling this sentence: once the departure is justified by sufficient lawful reasons and is reasonable, affirmance is permissible. He disputes the majority’s use of §3742(f)(1), not the prohibition on using an arrest record itself. The Court requires causal harmlessness in addition to reasonableness. No later treatment is established; these positions do not change controlling law.

- **Stringer v. Black, 1992-03-09** — source: the separately named writings in `records/Stringer_v_Black_merits_1992-03-09.md`. Souter, joined by Scalia and Thomas, dissents because Zant reserved the weighing question and Barclay did not clearly require the precise constitutional correction now demanded. In his view, reasonable jurists at finality could distinguish constitutionally protected conduct from a vague aggravator and regard the Mississippi application as unsettled. The majority holds that the statutory weighing role strengthens the old guidance requirement rather than making it new. No later treatment is established; these positions do not change controlling law.

- **Connecticut National Bank v. Germain, 1992-03-09** — source: the separately named writings in `records/Connecticut_National_Bank_v_Germain_merits_1992-03-09.md`. Stevens concurs in the judgment because both statutory language and legislative history support the available route. O’Connor, joined by White and Blackmun, accepts that redundancy is a real issue but finds the historical statutory structure insufficient to show withdrawal of interlocutory review. These four Justices agree with reversal without joining Thomas’s complete textual methodology. No later treatment is established; these positions do not change controlling law.

- **PFZ Properties, Inc. v. Rodriguez, 1992-03-09** — source: the separately named writings in `records/PFZ_Properties_v_Rodriguez_dismissal_of_writ_1992-03-09.md`. Stone would reach the granted question and affirm dismissal while assuming the asserted property entitlement. Favorably read allegations of delay and irregular administration do not, in his view, establish the further constitutionally oppressive or legally irrational deprivation required for the substantive claim. A party’s label of sham review is not itself a finding of improper purpose or the missing constitutional conclusion. He would not hold that available state review cures every substantive wrong, import the rejected pretrial enlargement, or decide procedural due process, equal protection, takings, damages, permits or unnecessary officer defenses. No later treatment is established; these positions do not change controlling law.

- **United States v. R. L. C., 1992-03-24** — source: the separately named writings in `records/United_States_v_RLC_merits_1992-03-24.md`. Souter, joined by White and Stevens, additionally relies on statutory development and legislative history and regards lenity as a last resort after that inquiry. Scalia, joined by Kennedy and Thomas, would not use legislative history to resolve textual punishment ambiguity against the accused. Thomas separately stresses that established legal rules of construction precede lenity. Stone relies on text and statutory context, uses residual lenity only to confirm narrower punishment, and preserves juvenile discretion below the ceiling. O’Connor, joined by Blackmun, dissents: in her view, the offense-statute maximum preserves the separate rehabilitative scheme, and calculating an adult range imports procedures Congress did not require. The Court adopts the cap without resolving those methodological disagreements. No later treatment is established; these positions do not change controlling law.

- **United States v. Wilson, 1992-03-24** — source: the separately named writings in `records/United_States_v_Wilson_merits_1992-03-24.md`. Stevens, joined by White, would permit the sentencing court to resolve a ripe credit entitlement when counsel and the record are available, leaving ministerial arithmetic or later adjustments to the appropriate official. He reads the passive wording and placement in the sentencing chapter as allowing shared responsibility and rejects the premise that occasional incomplete information bars every sentencing-stage determination. The Court instead adopts initial administrative computation after commencement. The dissent does not claim unreviewable executive discretion or discard the statutory no-double-credit language. No later treatment is established; these positions do not change controlling law.

#### Holmes correction insertion — published separate positions, March 24, 1992

O'Connor, joined by White and Stevens, agrees with the causal and SIPA grounds but would answer the purchaser-seller question. Section 1964(c) creates an express action for a person injured in business or property by reason of a RICO violation; Blue Chip Stamps shaped an implied securities action and does not add a purchaser-seller requirement to that enacted right. Their view does not remove the need to prove proximate cause, establish every alleged predicate or resolve the fraud merits. It is noncontrolling.

Scalia concurs only in the judgment. He treats proximate cause as an established background limit on civil recovery and agrees that the asserted relationship is insufficient. He also would reject the purchaser-seller requirement here because the policies used to define an implied securities action do not alone justify that categorical restriction under RICO's text. His statutory-scope account is separate from the Court's borrowed-antitrust explanation; it does not provide a Court holding on the reserved question.

Stone concurs while joining the Court. He adds that, insofar as SIPC seeks its own advances under §1964(c), its expenditure remains dependent on the broker-dealers' intervening losses and therefore fails the same proximate-cause requirement. This additional ground addresses the alternative characterization of SIPC's own claim; it is not a causal test for the independent SIPA provision and does not bar distinct trustee claims or lawful recovery through liquidation estates. No other Justice joins it, and it creates no additional controlling holding.

For continuity, Scalia also treats proximate cause and the protected class of statutory interests as distinct background limits that can vary with predicate offenses, including his qualification concerning multiple predicates. That account is noncontrolling and is not an operative Court test. O'Connor's broader discussion does not settle which securities violations qualify as predicates. Source for each position and its scope: `records/Holmes_v_Securities_Investor_Protection_Corp_merits_1992-03-24.md`, writings identified there; no later treatment is established.

#### Chunk 7 published separate positions

- **United States v. Felix, 1992-03-25:** Stevens, joined by Blackmun, agrees that the separate substantive crimes can be prosecuted and joins Part I. They also agree with the conspiracy judgment because the two overlapping acts did not establish the agreement, but decline a general exception to Grady for conspiracy or other complex crimes. Their narrower ground does not replace the seven-Justice Part II rule. Source: `records/United_States_v_Felix_merits_1992-03-25.md`. The positions are noncontrolling except where an express Court join is identified in §3.

- **Barnhill v. Johnson, 1992-03-25:** Stevens, joined by Blackmun, would treat delivery as a conditional transfer if honor follows within ten days, and use honor otherwise. They rely on the breadth of the statutory transfer definition, §547(e), ordinary commercial treatment and consistent language within §547. That view does not control §547(b), and neither writing decides a general §547(c) timing rule. Source: `records/Barnhill_v_Johnson_merits_1992-03-25.md`. The positions are noncontrolling except where an express Court join is identified in §3.

- **National Railroad Passenger Corp. v. Boston & Maine Corp., 1992-03-25:** White, joined by Blackmun and Thomas, accepts that the statute permits acquisition followed by reconveyance. They would require the ICC to explain and apply the service-need condition itself, treating counsel’s useful-or-appropriate construction as a later rationalization. They rely on State Farm, Chenery and Burlington Truck Lines and do not endorse the circuit’s categorical permanent-ownership rule. Their agreement about possible statutory power is not a join in the Court’s approval of this order. Source: `records/National_Railroad_Passenger_Corp_v_Boston_and_Maine_Corp_merits_1992-03-25.md`. The positions are noncontrolling except where an express Court join is identified in §3.

- **Freeman v. Pitts, 1992-03-31:** Kennedy, joined by White and Scalia, regards the existing demographic findings as credible and emphasizes Spangler’s limit on perpetual balancing, while accepting remand for unresolved connections and whole-decree good faith. Blackmun, joined by Stevens and O’Connor, urges examination of the district’s contribution to residential movement and racially identifiable schools; they do not regard initial neighborhood assignments or demographic change alone as proof of completed desegregation. Souter separately explains that past segregation can cause migration and that remaining violations can generate renewed imbalance in a released area; retained jurisdiction permits renewed control when justified. Scalia separately urges future reconsideration of the causation presumption as past violations recede. That proposed change is not adopted. All seven non-Stone Justices join the limited Court opinion; none is attributed agreement with every separate application. Source: `records/Freeman_v_Pitts_merits_1992-03-31.md`. The positions are noncontrolling except where an express Court join is identified in §3.

- **Yee v. City of Escondido, 1992-04-01:** Blackmun concurs in the judgment: no physical taking, and neither additional merits claim is properly presented. He declines the Court’s ripeness determination and suggestions about which allegations may bear on regulatory taking. Souter concurs in the judgment and would otherwise join except for the suggestions about regulatory relevance. Stone’s noted join is limited to I and II-D; no separate authored opinion adds a ground beyond those sections. The Court’s discussion of possible regulatory significance decides no regulatory liability. Source: `records/Yee_v_City_of_Escondido_merits_1992-04-01.md`. The positions are noncontrolling except where an express Court join is identified in §3.

- **Jacobson v. United States, 1992-04-06:** O’Connor, joined by Kennedy and by Scalia as to Part I, would defer to the jury’s inference from prompt illegal orders, requests for more and precautions about detection. They distinguish preliminary contact from inducement and fear that the Court’s reasoning will restrict legitimate undercover investigation. In Part II, joined only by Kennedy, O’Connor objects that the Court appears to require a predisposition knowingly to violate law. The Court expressly adopts no new knowledge-of-illegality element; Scalia does not join that criticism. No objective-police-misconduct theory controls. Source: `records/Jacobson_v_United_States_merits_1992-04-06.md`. The positions are noncontrolling except where an express Court join is identified in §3.

- **Trevino v. Texas, 1992-04-06:** Stone concurs, emphasizing that preservation alone cannot warrant a new trial; he would order one only if the completed inquiry establishes a violation or lawful corrective proceedings cannot resolve it. That additional remedial qualification is his separate position, not a direction adopted by the Court. There is no noted dissent and no public certiorari poll. Source: `records/Trevino_v_Texas_merits_1992-04-06.md`. The positions are noncontrolling except where an express Court join is identified in §3.

#### Chunk 7 corrections — published separate positions

- **Suter v. Artist M., March 25, 1992:** Blackmun, joined by Stevens and, on (15) only, Stone, would apply Wilder to the mandatory, individualized reasonable-efforts obligation. The absence of the essential caseworker permits a judicial inquiry without prescribing every welfare decision; administrative funding withdrawal does not establish incompatibility with §1983. The supported three-day remedy is confined to this record. Blackmun and Stevens also would sustain the reporting claim under §1983, while Stone leaves its enforceability and merits for provision-specific consideration. All three leave a redundant implied (15) action undecided. Stone's separate writing preserves that narrower position on (9) and the distinction between the existing (15) injunction and possible additional relief on remand. These positions do not control the rejected (15)/(9) claims. Source: [record](records/Suter_v_Artist_M_merits_1992-03-25.md). No later treatment within the reviewed committed events.

- **United States Department of Commerce v. Montana, March 31, 1992:** No separate writing or noted separate position is published. All nine Justices join the Opinion of the Court and both disposition components. Source: [record](records/United_States_Department_of_Commerce_v_Montana_merits_1992-03-31.md).

#### Chunk 8 published separate positions

**United States v. Alaska, April 21:** White writes the Court’s opinion. Stone joins Parts I–II and agrees in the judgment on Parts III–VII and denial of the broader injunction prayer. No separate writing or additional published noncontrolling proposition is supplied; no unjoined APA reasoning is attributed to Stone.

- **Barker v. Kansas, 1992-04-21** — [Source writings](records/Barker_v_Kansas_merits_1992-04-21.md): Stevens, joined by Thomas, concurrence, April 21, 1992: Davis controls this case, but a tax borne equally by federal retirees and the great majority of ordinary state taxpayers should not be treated as forbidden discrimination merely because the State benefits its own employees. Congress may correct that application of statutory intergovernmental tax immunity. This criticism is noncontrolling; both Justices join the Court’s application of Davis. Prudential Insurance Co. v. Benjamin, 328 U.S. 408, supplies their congressional-correction analogy, not a new majority rule.

- **United States Department of Energy v. Ohio, 1992-04-21** — [Source writings](records/United_States_Department_of_Energy_v_Ohio_merits_1992-04-21.md): White, joined by Blackmun and Stevens, concurrence in part and dissent in part, April 21, 1992: CWA facilities and both citizen-suit clauses expressly waive punitive penalties; a specific inclusion of the United States should govern the incorporated remedy and the CWA’s approved state enforcement partnership brings program penalties within its federal-law limitation. Waiver should be read as an integrated enactment rather than narrowed through artificial ambiguity. They agree that RCRA facilities alone lacks an unequivocal punitive waiver because it specifically concerns injunction enforcement. The three broader-waiver propositions are noncontrolling. Their cited Canadian Aviator/Block principle cautions against unduly narrowing an actual waiver; Nordic Village remains controlling law despite their disagreement about its application here.

- **Taylor v. Freeland & Kronz, 1992-04-21** — [Source writings](records/Taylor_v_Freeland_and_Kronz_merits_1992-04-21.md): Stevens dissents: §522(l)'s reference to subsection (b) should deny the deadline's protection to a claim with no arguable statutory basis. He would reverse and remand for the appropriate inquiry, protecting creditors without finding fraud or automatically reinstating every turnover theory. His statutory-context and equitable-administration account does not control.

- **Gomez v. United States District Court for the Northern District of California, 1992-04-21** — [Source writings](records/Gomez_v_United_States_District_Court_application_to_vacate_stay_1992-04-21.md): Stevens, joined by Blackmun, dissents: the medical and contemporary-legislative evidence persuades them that cyanide-gas execution inflicts unnecessary cruelty; they would preserve the stay for lower-court adjudication and regard timing as insufficient to permit an unconstitutional punishment. Stone separately dissents: he finds a supported possibility of relief and an insufficiently established channel bar, and concludes that the medical submissions, recent concrete account, irreversibility, and focused duration justify the existing stay despite independently serious delay. Recent evidence is no automatic excuse, irreversibility creates no automatic capital stay, and he makes no final method determination. All these propositions are noncontrolling.

- **Keeney v. Tamayo-Reyes, 1992-05-04** — [Source writings](records/Keeney_v_Tamayo_Reyes_merits_1992-05-04.md): White; joined by Scalia, Souter, Thomas, dissent: Would replace Townsend’s fifth-branch deliberate-bypass qualification with cause and actual prejudice plus a fundamental-miscarriage exception; would reverse/remand to permit that showing. Kennedy, concurrence; joins Court: Material factual inadequacy entails a realistic possibility that the hearing could affect the outcome; independent habeas gateways should not displace accurate factual adjudication once the claim is properly before the court. Noncontrolling supplemental explanation, not an added general prejudice requirement.

- **Denton v. Hernandez, 1992-05-04** — [Source writings](records/Denton_v_Hernandez_merits_1992-05-04.md): Stevens; joined by Blackmun, dissent: Agrees with the factual-frivolity standard but would affirm the appellate judgment because it already accords with that standard; disputes the additional remand, without a merits finding or categorical judicial-notice restriction.

- **United States v. Williams, 1992-05-04** — [Source writings](records/United_States_v_Williams_merits_1992-05-04.md): Stevens, joined by Blackmun and O’Connor, would dismiss the writ because the Government did not contest the circuit rule in this litigation. Thomas joins the dismissal preference and objection to favored treatment of the Government in Part III, without joining Part I’s fuller analysis. On the reached merits, those four recognize a duty to disclose personally known, substantial evidence directly negating guilt and identify evidence plainly precluding probable cause as a sufficient case for dismissal; they question whether the accounting evidence satisfies that formulation and do not resolve an evidentiary question outside the grant. Stone concurs in affirmance on a broader rule covering known evidence substantially undermining an essential element where omission materially distorts the presentation, and finds the established hearing findings and Bank of Nova Scotia prejudice sufficient. His broader application remains noncontrolling outside the narrow Marks rule. Scalia, joined by White, Kennedy and Souter, would reverse because supervisory remedies enforce independently established law and do not authorize courts to create this presentation duty for a separate accusatory institution.

- **Vasquez v. Harris, A-768, April 21, 1992** — [Stone's statement concurring in part and dissenting in part](records/Vasquez_v_Harris_A-768_application_to_vacate_stay_1992-04-21.md): Stone agrees that no materially new claim or record shown justifies the later stay effectively nullifying Gomez, but opposes categorically requiring this Court's permission for every further lower-federal-court stay. A genuinely new, jurisdictionally proper claim satisfying the governing stay standard must remain judicially available below; mere relitigation of Gomez does not warrant a stay. This qualification is noncontrolling and is no exception to the Court's actual permission condition. Blackmun and Stevens publish denial notations in both Harris applications without explanatory opinions; no additional legal theory is attributed to those notations.

#### Chunk 9 published separate positions

- **Foucha v. Louisiana, 1992-05-18** — [Source writings](records/Foucha_v_Louisiana_merits_1992-05-18.md): White, joined by Blackmun, Stevens and Souter, concludes in Part III that Louisiana also denies equal protection by withholding ordinary commitment safeguards from a sane acquittee while not permitting comparable indefinite dangerousness confinement for other persons whose original custody basis has ended. The four would require a particularly convincing justification for that disparity; this is a plurality position, not law.

O'Connor emphasizes that the holding concerns this indefinite psychiatric regime. She leaves open detention carefully limited in nature and duration to substantial safety concerns and questions extended confinement beyond what a conviction could have permitted, without deciding that question. No constitutional requirement that a State provide an insanity defense is adopted.

Stone-Zsela agrees that the psychiatric channel's ended predicate requires relief. He reserves independent equal protection, a new general scrutiny tier, universal proof rules and narrowly defined alternative regimes. Implementation cannot allow indefinite continuation while the State looks for a new justification; separate custody requires its own established authority and procedures.

Kennedy would affirm because the insanity adjudication follows proof of criminal conduct and belongs to a distinct legal channel. He views state definitions of criminal insanity and incapacitation as constitutionally relevant and rejects automatic transfer of ordinary civil-commitment rules to this setting.

Thomas, joined by Scalia, would affirm because acquittees differ from ordinary civil committees, the State may attach continued safety-based custody to that status, and periodic counseled review is adequate here. They reject an expansive new substantive right against this confinement and distinguish its lack of a fixed endpoint from a refusal ever to provide release review. Neither dissent controls.

- **Riggins v. Nevada, 1992-05-18** — [Source writings](records/Riggins_v_Nevada_merits_1992-05-18.md): Kennedy concurs in the judgment. He would require an extraordinary showing that involuntary medication for competency presents no significant risk of materially altering the accused's reactions, participation or assistance to counsel. He doubts that the contemporary medical evidence often permits that showing; if competence cannot be restored without such impairment, lawful civil commitment rather than trial may be the available route. This proposed standard is not the Court's rule.

Stone-Zsela joins the Court and separately applies Chapman v. California, 386 U.S. 18 (1967): the State bears the burden of showing constitutional trial error harmless beyond a reasonable doubt, and the medication record fails that burden. His concurrence supplies no general structural-error classification, categorical ban on competency treatment or order to stop medically necessary care. The Court's holding remains the record-specific unjustified-treatment and trial-risk rule.

Thomas, joined by Scalia except in Part II-A, would affirm. They regard expert testimony and the competence finding as adequate protection of the trial, distinguish a refusal-interest violation remediable in a civil action from an established basis to reverse a conviction, and reject reversal based on possible impairment alone. Thomas alone also disputes proof of involuntariness and whether a separate Harper liberty theory was preserved and included in the grant; Scalia does not join those additional objections.

The Court leaves the separate Fifth Amendment demeanor/self-incrimination theory and competency-waiver question undecided, all nine agreeing. The separate Eighth Amendment sentencing-display theory is also left undecided.

- **Wyatt v. Cole, 1992-05-18** — [Source writings](records/Wyatt_v_Cole_merits_1992-05-18.md): Kennedy, joined by Scalia, explains why preserving historical protection for reasonable private reliance does not justify Harlow immunity. The analogous torts attended to malice, probable cause and, when material, actual belief. A court may test deficient proof through ordinary summary judgment; the immunity ruling does not decide the elements or burdens of an eventual private defense. Souter, joined by Thomas, would affirm: the historical good-faith protection can support objective immunity, and citizens should be able to rely reasonably on unrepealed laws that officials continue to enforce. Both positions concern the distinction central to the Court's decision; neither creates an affirmative defense as controlling law.

- **Wade v. United States, 1992-05-18** — [Source writings](records/Wade_v_United_States_merits_1992-05-18.md): No institutional change, retained Supreme Court case, new remand or later external act results. Stone-Zsela, concurrence in Wade, May 18, 1992, reserves the discovery standard for a supported constitutional-purpose claim and rejects circular proof demands while agreeing that assistance alone warrants nothing. This is a published noncontrolling reservation; it does not displace the eight-Justice threshold. No other separate writing is issued.

- **Coleman v. Thompson, 1992-05-20** — [Source writings](records/Coleman_v_Thompson_stay_application_1992-05-20.md): Blackmun, dissent in Coleman, May 20, joined by Stone-Zsela and Souter, would preserve review where the lower court's possible substitution of conclusive innocence for a probability inquiry and its treatment of scientific criticism create sufficient Barefoot review/reversal prospects. The position acknowledges contrary evidence, confines relief to a stay, and resolves neither innocence nor hearing entitlement. Stevens's judgment-only concurrence announces no new doctrinal position or final petition action.

- **Burson v. Freeman, 1992-05-26** — [Source writings](records/Burson_v_Freeman_merits_1992-05-26.md): Blackmun's plurality, joined by Stone, White and Kennedy, concludes that the historical mechanism justifies this limited protection of the unavoidable approach to voting. Campaign agents could confront and observe voters in ways that enabled intimidation or fraudulent control. Starting protection only inside the door and punishing proved intimidation afterward do not equally prevent subtle or completed pressure. Campaign-specific coverage addresses the documented source of that harm; it does not prefer candidates or viewpoints. The plurality treats the 100-foot line as a confined implementation of protection while campaigning remains available beyond it. It does not demand an experiment exposing voters to coercion or proof that each foot is indispensable, and does not treat tradition alone as sufficient. This application has four votes and is noncontrolling.

Kennedy's concurrence explains that a law facially distinguishing content may call for examination of its actual justification. He regards this narrow accommodation of free voting as compatible with his objection to general censorship through compelling-interest balancing. His concurrence does not lower the standard joined in this case.

Scalia concurs only in the judgment. In his view, the longstanding exclusion of electioneering from functioning polling environs means that those areas are not traditional public forums during voting. He finds the restriction reasonable and viewpoint neutral and accepts the historical considerations as adequate for that standard. His forum theory has one vote.

Stevens, joined by O'Connor and Souter, agrees that strict scrutiny governs but concludes that Tennessee has not met it. The State's concrete disruption testimony concerns the interior, while the prohibition reaches peaceful campaign speech across public streets and sidewalks. A tradition adopted with secret ballots does not itself prove the continuing necessity of this outdoor exclusion. Existing laws against intimidation and more limited entrance protections are insufficiently answered; permitting other solicitation while banning campaign speech further weakens the asserted fit. The dissent would leave the federal invalidation in place.

- **United States v. Burke, 1992-05-26** — [Source writings](records/United_States_v_Burke_merits_1992-05-26.md): United States v. Burke, May 26, 1992: Blackmun, joined by White, Stevens and Kennedy, plurality — old Title VII’s backpay/equitable structure fails the tort-type personal-injury classification under §104(a)(2); remedies are material to the claim’s nature, and the holding would be confined to the old regime. Scalia, concurrence in judgment — §104’s personal injuries or sickness context concerns physical or mental health; backpay entitlement does not require such injury, and an inconsistent Treasury interpretation cannot override the statute; he expressly recognizes that the Government did not argue this ground. Souter, concurrence in judgment — no indispensable intangible-remedy requirement; tort and employment-contract analogies both bear weight, and an unclear exclusion fails under the narrow-construction canon. O’Connor, joined by Thomas, dissent — the statutory right against discrimination is tort-type irrespective of the available remedy’s limitation, and compensatory wage measurement does not negate personal injury. Stone, dissent — this identified intentional-discrimination settlement qualifies by the source and object of its payment, while ordinary unpaid wages, other non-tort payments, punitive damages and amended regimes remain outside his rule. All these rationales are noncontrolling; none receives five joins or an established narrowest-ground status.

- **Evans v. United States, 1992-05-26** — [Source writings](records/Evans_v_United_States_merits_1992-05-26.md): Evans v. United States, May 26, 1992: O’Connor, concurrence in part and judgment — joins Stevens I–II but reserves the unbriefed false-pretense issue as beyond the question fairly presented; she expresses no view whether that theory is correct. Kennedy, concurrence in part and judgment — joins III but reads induced as applying to official right, satisfied by an intended and understood corrupt exchange conveyed expressly or through words/conduct; a course of dealing must communicate that failure to pay means adverse or less favorable official treatment. He protects good-faith belief in lawful entitlement and regards exchange as necessary beyond campaign cases; these separate elaborations do not supply additional Court elements. Stone, concurrence in judgment — no first-speaker prerequisite for a knowingly accepted specific official undertaking, no automatic coercion from office, no congressional-silence-alone basis for an ambiguous crime; gratuities, ambiguous general support and absent undertakings remain reserved. Thomas, joined by Scalia, dissent — would require the distinct official-right claim and statutory inducement, preserve lenity and the federal-state criminal boundary, and reverse the Hobbs disposition; they leave the tax conviction undisturbed because its particular instruction objection was unpreserved. All additional propositions remain noncontrolling except the separately identified express joins in Holdings.

- **Quill Corp. v. North Dakota, 1992-05-26** — [Source writings](records/Quill_Corp_v_North_Dakota_merits_1992-05-26.md): Quill Corp. v. North Dakota, May 26, 1992: Stevens, joined by Blackmun, O’Connor and Souter, Part IV plurality — the Commerce collection boundary retains substantive value as a clear limit on cumulative national compliance burdens and can be distinguished from Spector’s merely verbal formalism; that complete policy analysis is noncontrolling, though its express precedent/adherence ground is part of the common holding. Scalia, joined by Kennedy and Thomas, concurrence — agrees with due-process revision through comparable reasoning without treating every adjudicative and legislative jurisdiction standard as identical; retains Commerce precedent solely through stare decisis, justified reliance and Congress’s corrective power, without revisiting its substantive merits. Stone, concurrence in judgment — the specific collection precedent remains within Complete Auto; insignificant software does not create local market-maintaining activity; technological change reduces calculation cost but does not establish disappearance of divergent collection duties; reliance is substantial but not permanent immunity and congressional coordination supports, not independently commands, adherence; due process is reserved. White, concurrence in part and dissent in part — joins due process but would overrule the Commerce rule, treating sustained economic activity and protected market access as sufficient nexus; regards the physical distinction as economically arbitrary and would not dismiss the software alternative without fuller consideration. None of these additional rationales changes current law beyond the exact express majority holdings.

#### Chunk 10 published separate positions

- **Chemical Waste Management, Inc. v. Hunt (1992-06-01):** Stone concurs while joining the Court in full. He adds that residents' general contributions to state revenue do not establish a corresponding substantially equivalent intrastate burden that would explain the differential. This is his additional noncontrolling ground. It does not turn the State's unpressed compensatory-tax theory into a holding of the Court.

- **Fort Gratiot Sanitary Landfill, Inc. v. Michigan Department of Natural Resources (1992-06-01):** Blackmun dissents. He would remand to allow proof that Michigan's comprehensive planning system serves legitimate environmental and safety objectives before invalidating the import restrictions. The system also imposes planning and disposal burdens on Michigan counties, which in his view may distinguish it from a simple local commercial preference. His position seeks an evidentiary opportunity; it neither declares the law valid nor supplies a controlling exception.

- **Morales v. Trans World Airlines, Inc. (1992-06-01):** Stone concurs in the judgment. He relies on the intrinsic relation of the binding fare-presentation, availability and mandatory-term requirements to rates, together with the actual enforcement threat. He joins no part of Scalia's opinion and no independent economic-effects ground. His concurrence gives savings a real role outside the displaced category and reserves actual fraud, voluntarily undertaken obligations, unrelated injury and constitutional speech claims for their own records; those reservations do not narrow the five-Justice Court holding.

Stevens, joined by Blackmun, dissents. They would reverse the fare-guideline protection as well because the aviation statute's structure and savings preserve ordinary truthfulness regulation. They also consider the claimed significant effects on fares insufficiently demonstrated. They oppose the excessive decree too, but join none of the Court's reasoning.

- **Burdick v. Takushi (1992-06-08):** Kennedy, joined by Blackmun and Stevens, would reverse and remand. They accept contextual scrutiny and reject the ballot-as-general-expression theory, but consider the total prohibition a substantial burden in the actual nomination system. Low signature requirements do not answer the need to select one party or nonpartisan primary ballot for all offices, the advancement rules, or the inability to support a real alternative in an unopposed contest. They reject a presumption derived from the surrounding rules’ validity. Sore-loser prevention does not explain a primary ban and reaches only some general-election candidates; party-raiding and uncontested-primary interests do not justify the prohibition’s entire reach. Their position does not establish a general constitutional write-in entitlement.

Stone-Zsela separately would reverse and remand for workable prospective relief from the complete prohibition within this system. Fifteen signatures make initial filing accessible, but the primary-ticket and advancement conditions must enter the burden inquiry. An actual single-candidate contest presents electoral choice even though the Constitution guarantees neither competition in every race nor all late candidacies. Sore-loser prevention cannot explain the primary ban; raiding does not justify excluding genuine support for a candidate on the selected primary ticket; and an election omitted under the uncontested-winner rule has no general-election ballot needing protection. He rejects arbitrary fictional-message tallies and confines relief to legally eligible candidates. He reserves reasonable write-in procedures, valid qualifications, narrow anti-raiding and sore-loser restrictions, and materially different systems. He would not prescribe statewide filing, certification, or counting machinery or independently invalidate other access rules.

Both writings are noncontrolling on the electoral-choice application and remedy. Their agreement on contextual scrutiny and rejection of general protest expression is identified in the Holdings block.

- **Eastman Kodak Co. v. Image Technical Services, Inc. (1992-06-08):** Scalia, joined by O’Connor and Thomas, would reverse and restore summary judgment on both preserved claims. They regard ordinary dependence on a brand’s replacement parts after buying durable equipment as derivative power that equipment competition generally disciplines. In their view, information and switching costs of the kind asserted do not warrant extending the per se tying category or treating every brand-specific aftermarket as an antitrust monopoly absent the necessary primary-market power. They consider the Court’s approach liable to burden ordinary integration, distribution choices, and proprietary component sales without the competitive power the Sherman Act requires. Their disagreement concerns the legal significance of this asserted aftermarket dependence and the sufficiency of the evidence, not a finding of respondents’ entitlement to final relief. The separate rule-of-reason route was not preserved below. This writing is noncontrolling; it does not displace the Court’s evidence-based inquiry or create an immunity for manufacturers.

- **United States v. Thompson/Center Arms Co. (1992-06-08):** Scalia and Thomas regard the express treatment of parts in other firearm definitions, and the intended shoulder-firing configuration shown by this record, as the source of statutory uncertainty. They do not accept a usefulness classification or legislative-history expansion of criminal coverage. Stone-Zsela applies text and structure to the actual dual-use set, followed by Crandon's uniform residual lenity, and reserves single-use regulated kits, sham alternatives and actual short-rifle assembly. These differences explain why no whole rationale has five joins. White, joined by Blackmun, Stevens and Kennedy, would tax the ready short-rifle set because an additional lawful barrel does not undo its manufacture. Stevens additionally would give controlling weight to the prior administrative notice and the civil refund posture rather than extend lenity to defeat this tax; that position is noncontrolling.

- **Sochor v. Florida (1992-06-08):** O'Connor explains that a bare assertion of harmlessness cannot replace a principled explanation grounded in the record; her concurrence clarifies the substantive requirement without demanding a particular phrase. White and Thomas view the entire state opinion, its removal of coldness, the three remaining factors, the no-mitigation finding and the cited correction decisions as a sufficient determination that the sentence would be unchanged beyond a reasonable doubt. Stevens and Blackmun would reach the jury-heinousness issue because Florida's fundamental-error power and alternative federal merits treatment undermine the asserted procedural independence in their view. They regard the jury recommendation as a consequential step and would include both its unconfined heinousness instruction and unsupported coldness submission in the corrective inquiry. Scalia would affirm because the surviving valid aggravators satisfy the required narrowing; he rejects a freestanding constitutional obligation to perform the mitigation weighing that Florida chose, as explained in his Walton position. These contrary grounds are noncontrolling.

#### Chunk 11 published separate positions

- **Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife (1992-06-12):** Scalia's Part III-B, joined by White and Thomas, would independently reject redressability because the action agencies are absent and their response, and the foreign projects' response, to a revised rule is not adequately established. Three Justices adopt that application; it does not control.

Kennedy, joined by Souter, reserves that redressability question. Their concurrence leaves room for factually demonstrated ecosystem or professional injuries and for Congress to define injuries and causal chains, while requiring Congress at least to identify the injury and relate it to the persons entitled to sue.

Stone-Zsela concurs in the judgment because the past visits and indefinite return intentions do not show imminent renewed personal use. A definite undertaking or an established return practice could supply the necessary evidence; purchased tickets or a particular travel document are unnecessary. He reserves disputed redressability, the outer constitutional scope of congressionally created procedural rights, and ESA geographic coverage. Actual use of an affected ecosystem presents a materially different record; multiple contributors alone would not defeat such a claim.

Stevens concurs in reversal while finding standing. In his view, the personal aesthetic and professional interest suffers when the threatened species and habitat are destroyed, and consultation can provide meaningful relief. He reads §7(a)(2)'s domestic consultation and exemption structure, alongside the Act's express international provisions, to exclude foreign actions. That construction receives no majority and is not the Court's law.

Blackmun, joined by O'Connor, dissents from ending the action on summary judgment. The prior visits, professional interests and intended return permit favorable inferences about future exposure, and the agencies' involvement and potential project influence create a factual issue about redress. Their procedural-injury discussion concerns Congress's capacity to protect concrete interests through required processes; it does not supply a controlling exception to Article III.

- **Republic of Argentina v. Weltover, Inc. (1992-06-12):** Stone-Zsela concurs in the judgment. A private borrower can promise dollar repayment at a creditor-selected financial center; monetary policy explains but does not replace the nature inquiry. New York was validly selected, and the payment was missing at the agreed place of performance. Adding substantiality would rewrite the exception. Assuming personhood solely to answer Argentina's interpretive objection, the purposeful New York payment and financial arrangements satisfy minimum contacts; he makes no independent constitutional personal-jurisdiction ruling. He reserves personhood, ultimate breach defenses and attachment immunity, and emphasizes that ordinary regulatory or expropriatory acts are not commercial simply because they affect money. No other separate writing issues.

- **FTC v. Ticor Title Insurance Co. (1992-06-12):** Stone-Zsela concurs in the judgment on substantive examination of the challenged private rate setting. Arithmetic checks and unanswered information requests cannot show that the State actually took responsibility for the rates. Written substantive review can suffice; no oral hearing, uniform form or rejection quota is required. He reserves materially different supervision records, distinct sovereign pricing and unpresented insurance exemptions.

Scalia joins the Court and concurs separately. He accepts that the active-supervision inquiry will create uncertainty for private participants, including outside negative-option systems, but regards that consequence as required by the unchallenged doctrine. His skepticism about exempting state-programmed private collusion does not overrule Parker or alter the Court's holding.

O'Connor, joined by Thomas, dissents. They distinguish the absence of power to review private conduct in prior cases from staffed, funded regulatory schemes with authority and actual agency responses here. They would treat required filings and nonobjection in an operating scheme as adequate state supervision, without assessing regulatory vigor. O'Connor emphasizes that regulated parties cannot control how thoroughly officials examine a filing, and retrospective uncertainty may discourage participation and burden regulators. The discussion of possible private treble-damages suits identifies consequences of their competing rule; no such award is before this Court.

- **Burlington Northern Railroad Co. v. Ford (1992-06-12):** Stone-Zsela concurs in the judgment on the approved as-applied venue distinction. The legitimate interest is the parties' competing convenience, not local incorporation as a reward. An actual local headquarters may reasonably receive a protection that a Texas-headquartered corporation's Montana office does not. Ordinary approximation is permissible, and this record does not show an irrational mismatch. He reserves the corporation incorporated elsewhere but actually headquartered in Montana, separately presented discriminatory commerce burdens and all FELA merits. He adds no independent alternative based on another corporation's hypothetical injury. No other Justice writes separately or dissents.

- **United States v. Alvarez-Machain (1992-06-15):** Stevens, joined by Blackmun and O’Connor, would affirm. The Treaty’s comprehensive surrender framework and reserved national choice would lose effect if a contracting government could bypass them by force. Rauscher permits enforcement of necessary structural implications, and Cook distinguishes a government’s treaty-limited power from wrongful private acquisition; the DEA’s involvement and Mexico’s protest therefore matter.
